Introduction: Why ask the question

Draft · 1,866 words · 14 October 2026

In the spring of 2026 I rode up Sa Calobra faster than I ever had. The climb on the north coast of Mallorca is nine and a half kilometres at about seven per cent, with twenty-six hairpins and nowhere to recover, and I took two minutes and eleven seconds off my best time.

I knew why, or thought I did. I had spent the winter in my garage on a smart trainer, four or five times a week, riding for 45 minutes to an hour at a heart rate of about 142 beats per minute. It was the dullest training I had ever done. Nothing hurt, nothing was worth posting, and my ride history turned into a column of identical flat grey lines. Then I went to Mallorca, rode the effort I had learned by heart all winter, and found there was a lot more power under it than before.

That winter was Zone 2 training, and I wrote about it with the enthusiasm of a convert. I told readers of my website that easy riding was the single most effective way to build mitochondria, the structures in muscle cells that use oxygen to make energy. I believed it. I had heard it from people who sounded as if they knew.

A few months later I sat down to read the research properly, and that sentence didn't survive. The largest summary of the evidence, a review of 353 training studies published in 2024, found that mitochondrial content rose by similar amounts whether people did steady continuous training (about 23%) or either kind of interval training, high-intensity or all-out sprints (27%). Per hour of training, the sprints did almost four times as much [1]. In 2025 four Canadian researchers made the same point at length, in a review they called "Much Ado About Zone 2" [2]. Zone 2 had done something real for me. It just hadn't done it for the reason I had been told, or not only for that reason.

This book grew out of that gap: between what people confidently say about endurance training and what the studies actually show.

A short history of training fashions

Endurance training goes through fashions, and they tend to follow the same pattern. Someone finds something real in a study. It gets explained with a mechanism that sounds convincing. Then it spreads faster than the evidence behind it, until the claims have little to do with what was found.

In 1996 a Japanese research group led by Izumi Tabata had seven people do a few minutes of very hard 20-second efforts on a bike, with 10 seconds of rest between each, five days a week for six weeks. Both their aerobic fitness and their capacity for short, hard efforts improved [3]. It was a small study of a punishing protocol. Within a few years "Tabata" had become a name for almost any four-minute workout in a gym class, most of them nothing like what the study's subjects did.

Ten years later Martin Gibala's lab in Canada compared two small groups of active young men over two weeks. One group did short sessions of 30-second all-out sprints. The other did long steady rides, about four times as much training in total. Both groups improved by similar amounts [4]. It was a careful study with a striking result, and it launched a decade of articles promising the benefits of endurance training in a fraction of the time. Fewer of those articles mentioned that the study lasted two weeks, involved 16 people, and measured early changes in muscle, not what happens over a season.

Around the same time, the Norway-based physiologist Stephen Seiler was describing how the world's best endurance athletes actually trained. Across sports, he found, they did roughly four easy sessions for every hard one, with very little in between [5]. That observation became "80/20" and "polarised training", and for a while every training plan had to be polarised.

Then came Zone 2. Its rise owed a lot to podcasts, in particular a 2019 conversation between the physician Peter Attia and the physiologist Iñigo San-Millán, who worked with professional cycling teams [6]. San-Millán's research on how well athletes and patients burn fat and clear lactate was real and interesting. Repeated across thousands of episodes and social media posts, it became something larger: a training intensity with special powers over mitochondria, metabolism and even lifespan.

None of these fashions was simply wrong. High-intensity intervals do work, elite athletes do mostly train easy, and easy riding does improve aerobic fitness. What each fashion got wrong was its confidence, and its silence about who was studied, for how long, and what was actually measured.

What this book tries to do

The first thing this book tries to do is explain the mechanism. For each training claim, I want you to understand the biology underneath it: what happens in a muscle, a heart or a bloodstream when you ride easy or ride hard, and why that might make you faster. Mechanisms matter because they tell you why something should work. They are also where fashions start, because a plausible mechanism is far easier to find than evidence that the training actually improves performance. "Easy riding burns the most fat, so it must build the most mitochondria" is a mechanism argument. Whether it's true is a different question, and the answer turned out to be no.

The second is to help you judge research yourself. Each chapter includes a short section called Reading the research, which takes one study from that chapter and uses it to practise a single skill: telling correlation from cause, spotting a sample too small to trust, noticing when "a trend" means "not significant", or asking who was actually studied. By the end of the book you should be able to read a training study, or a confident claim about one, and decide for yourself how much weight it can bear. The next fashion is already on its way, and this book won't be there when it arrives.

To keep the book honest about its own claims, the important ones carry a grade:

  • A: several randomised trials, or a well-conducted meta-analysis, pointing the same way.
  • B: consistent findings, but from small or short studies, a single lab, or one good trial.
  • C: observational data, mechanism, case studies or expert opinion.

A grade C claim isn't necessarily wrong. Most of what coaches know started as grade C, and some of it will become grade A. But you should know which is which, and so should anyone selling you a training plan.

How the book is organised

The book is built around one question, which is what actually limits how fast you can ride for an hour, or for five. Chapter 1 answers it with a model that physiologists have used for decades. Endurance performance depends on how much oxygen you can use at most (your VO2max), how close to that maximum you can ride for a long time, and how efficiently you turn that oxygen into power. Recent research adds a fourth factor: how well those three hold up when you're tired. Almost every training question in this book comes down to which of those four a session changes, and how strong the evidence is.

Part I, The engine, sets out the model, the biology of how bodies adapt to training, what the brain and nerves contribute, how to read a training study, and how to define and find your training intensities. Part II, The easy part, covers what low-intensity riding does to the body, why elite athletes do so much of it, durability, and the claims about health and longevity. Part III, The hard part, covers intervals: why time spent close to VO2max seems to matter, how to design a session, and how many hard sessions a week make sense. Part IV, Everything else, weighs the things besides ordinary training that are credited with making riders faster: fuelling, strength training, heat training and drugs. Part V puts it together into a training week, deals with special cases such as masters riders, women and riding indoors, and looks at how to tell whether any of it is working. The last chapter answers the title's question.

You can read the chapters in order or dip into the ones you care about. If you skip anything, don't skip Chapters 2 and 4. The biology and the research skills in those two chapters are what the rest of the book builds on.

Who this is for

This book is written for cyclists who want to understand their training rather than just follow it. It gets technical where the science needs it to, and it names researchers and studies throughout, because "studies show" is exactly the habit the book is trying to break. You don't need a science background. You do need some patience with numbers.

Most of the research is in cyclists, and where it isn't, I say so. Plenty of the best training studies were done in runners, rowers and cross-country skiers, and I use them where the physiology carries across.

This is not a training plan, and it isn't medical advice. If you have a heart condition or another medical reason to be careful with hard exercise, talk to a doctor before you change how you train.

About me, and a disclosure

I am not a sports scientist. I am a quantitative researcher in finance, where I use behavioural analytics to help portfolio managers improve the way they invest, and I read a lot of finance research as part of the job. Before that I studied maths and worked at the European Space Agency and in banking. So I come to training studies as someone who judges quantitative claims for a living, in a field where a persuasive story with weak evidence behind it costs money.

I am also a rider short of time. I raced mountain bikes in my teens with my two older brothers. These days I have two children and a busy life, I ride about three sportives a year, and I go on a couple of cycling holidays with other dads from my children's school. I wanted to know what training would do the most for someone like that.

I should also be clear about an interest. I built an app, JustZone2, that controls a smart trainer to hold your heart rate in a target zone. I built it for my own winter training and other riders now use it. It gets one mention at the end of the book and none in between, and it has no bearing on what the evidence says. If anything, writing this book has made me more cautious about some of the claims I used to make for easy riding. Where the research says Zone 2 is overrated, this book says so.

The title asks whether it's just Zone 2. It isn't, quite, and to see why, we need to start with what limits endurance in the first place.

Chapter 1: What limits endurance

Draft · 3,458 words · 14 October 2026

In 1923 the physiologist Archibald Hill and his colleague Hartley Lupton described something that every endurance athlete now takes for granted. As exercise gets harder, the body takes up more oxygen, until it reaches a ceiling. Beyond that point the work can continue for a while, but the extra energy has to come from somewhere other than oxygen, and it can't come from there for long [1]. That ceiling became known as maximal oxygen uptake, or VO2max, and for most of the following century it was the number physiologists reached for first when they wanted to explain why one athlete was faster than another.

It explains less than you might think. Two riders can have the same VO2max and finish an hour-long time trial minutes apart. A rider with a modest VO2max can beat one with a much higher one. To see why, you need the rest of the model, and that model is what this chapter is about. Cyclists talk about a rider's engine without saying what they mean by it. In this book the engine is the whole aerobic system that the model describes, and VO2max is one part of it. The model is also the framework for the whole book. Every training claim in later chapters comes down to a simple question: which part of this model does it change?

The question

Strip away tactics, aerodynamics and the weather, and endurance performance comes down to one thing: how much power you can sustain for the length of the event. For a 40-minute effort or a two-hour climb, that power comes almost entirely from aerobic metabolism, the process in which muscle cells use oxygen to release energy from fat and carbohydrate.

The real question, then, is what sets the rate at which you can produce energy aerobically, and how much of that energy ends up turning the pedals.

In 2008 the physiologists Michael Joyner and Edward Coyle set out the answer that is still the standard one [2]. It has three parts:

  1. VO2max, the maximum rate at which you can take up and use oxygen.
  2. The fraction of VO2max you can sustain, which depends on where your lactate threshold sits.
  3. Efficiency, the share of the energy you produce that ends up as power at the pedals.

Multiply the first two and you get what Joyner and Coyle call your performance VO2, the rate of oxygen use you can hold for the duration of a race. Multiply that by your efficiency and you have your sustainable power. The rest of this chapter takes each part in turn, then adds a fourth that the original model left out.

How it works: from air to muscle

Before the three factors make sense, it helps to follow oxygen from the air to the muscle.

You breathe it into your lungs, where it crosses into the blood and binds to haemoglobin in red blood cells. Your heart pumps that blood out to the body. The amount it can pump each minute, the cardiac output, is your heart rate multiplied by the volume of blood ejected with each beat, the stroke volume. In the working leg muscles, blood flows through a dense mesh of tiny vessels called capillaries, and oxygen moves out of the blood into the muscle fibres. Inside each fibre it reaches the mitochondria, small structures that use it to break down fuel and make ATP, the molecule that powers muscle contraction.

Any link in that chain could, in principle, set the ceiling. In practice, the evidence points mostly at delivery: how much oxygen the heart and blood can bring to the muscle. Bassett and Howley's influential review set out three lines of evidence [3]. When oxygen delivery is changed, by blood doping, by breathing thin air at altitude, or by drugs that slow the heart, VO2max changes with it. When people train, the rise in VO2max comes mainly from a bigger maximum cardiac output, not from muscles extracting more oxygen from each litre of blood. And when a small muscle group is given more blood than it would normally get, it can consume oxygen at a far higher rate than it does during whole-body exercise, which suggests the muscle could use more oxygen if only it were delivered. A

Joyner and Coyle identify the size of the heart's stroke volume as the most important single difference between champions and everyone else, alongside a larger blood volume, more capillaries and more mitochondria in the trained muscles [2]. Those last two matter for a different reason, covered in the next section: they don't raise the ceiling much, but they change how close to it you can ride.

The mechanics of how training changes the heart, the blood and the muscle are the subject of Chapter 2. For now, the important point is that there are two broad sites of adaptation: the delivery system, which mostly sets VO2max, and the muscle itself, which mostly sets how much of it you can use.

VO2max: the ceiling

Champion endurance athletes have VO2max values of roughly 70 to 85 millilitres of oxygen per kilogram of body weight per minute, about 50 to 100% higher than healthy, untrained young adults [2]. Elite women's values tend to be about 10% lower than elite men's, which Joyner and Coyle attribute mainly to lower haemoglobin concentration and a higher proportion of body fat.

A high VO2max is close to a requirement for elite endurance performance. You won't find a Tour de France winner with an average one. But among athletes who are already good, VO2max becomes a poor predictor of who wins, because the riders in a professional peloton all have high values. Once the ceiling is high enough, the other two factors decide most of the differences that remain.

VO2max is also only partly trainable. It rises quickly in untrained people and much more slowly in those who have trained for years, and part of it is set by genetics. That matters later in the book, when we look at how much of the case for high-intensity intervals rests on VO2max, and how much of the case for easy riding rests on everything else.

The fraction you can sustain

Nobody can ride at 100% of their VO2max for long. The question is how close they can get for an hour, and the answer depends on what happens to lactate in the blood as effort rises.

At low intensities, blood lactate stays close to its resting level. As intensity rises, it reaches a point where lactate starts to accumulate, and above a second, higher point it keeps rising until the effort has to stop. The first of these points is the lactate threshold that Chapter 5 explores in detail. For now, the useful fact is where it sits. In untrained people, lactate begins to rise noticeably at around 60% of VO2max. In elite endurance athletes it can sit at 75 to 90% [2]. A

That shift is one of the largest effects of endurance training, and it has little to do with the heart. It comes mainly from changes in the muscle: more mitochondria, more of the enzymes that use oxygen, better capacity to burn fat, and so less reliance on producing lactate at a given rate of work [3]. Bassett and Howley argue that the lactate threshold is the best single physiological predictor of endurance performance, because it combines all three factors in one number: how high your VO2max is, how much of it you can use, and how economically you use it.

This is where the case for large volumes of easy riding begins. If easy training builds mitochondria and capillaries, it should raise the fraction of VO2max you can sustain, even if it does relatively little for VO2max itself. That's a mechanism argument. Whether the training studies bear it out is a question for Part II.

Efficiency: turning oxygen into watts

The third factor is the easiest to overlook. Of the energy your muscles release from oxygen and fuel, only a fraction ends up as power at the cranks. The rest becomes heat. In cyclists, that fraction, the gross efficiency, typically ranges from about 18.5% to 23.5% at 300 watts [2]. Joyner and Coyle estimate that efficiency varies by 20 to 30% between cyclists, which is a large range for something that rarely appears in training discussions.

Taking the two ends of that range, a rider with a gross efficiency of 23% produces about a quarter more power from the same oxygen than a rider at 18.5% [2]. No training block raises VO2max by a quarter in a trained cyclist.

The best-known evidence on why some riders are more efficient than others comes from Edward Coyle's lab in 1992. In 19 highly trained cyclists, the proportion of slow-twitch (type I) fibres in the thigh muscle ranged from 32% to 76%, and riders with more of them were markedly more efficient: the correlation between type I fibre percentage and one measure of efficiency was 0.85 [4]. Type I fibres are the ones built for sustained, aerobic work, and they seem to produce force with less energy wasted as heat. B

Whether training can increase efficiency much, and how quickly, is less clear. Joyner and Coyle suggest it may improve by 1 to 3% a year with continued endurance training [2]. The most famous evidence for that idea is a single case study, and it is worth looking at closely.

Reading the research: what one athlete can and can't tell us

In 2005 Coyle published a paper following one cyclist from age 21 to 28 [5]. Over those seven years, the paper reported, the rider's VO2max stayed at about 6 litres a minute, but his muscular efficiency rose by 8%, and together with losing body fat before each race season, his steady-state power per kilogram at a given rate of oxygen use rose by 18%. The paper suggested the improvement probably reflected changes in muscle proteins after years of training intensely for three to six hours on most days. The rider was described as a six-time consecutive champion of the Tour de France. He was Lance Armstrong.

The paper was widely cited as evidence that efficiency improves with years of training. It also drew immediate challenges. In the same journal and the same year, a published exchange asked whether Armstrong's efficiency had improved at all [6]. In 2008, a letter from four researchers was published under the blunt title "Delta efficiency calculation in Tour de France champion is wrong" [7]. And in 2012 the United States Anti-Doping Agency found that Armstrong had doped, and his Tour de France results from 1999 onwards were disqualified [8].

The specific dispute about the calculation is technical. The general lesson isn't, and it applies far beyond this one paper. A case study follows one person. It can't separate the effect of training from everything else that changed in that person's life over the same period: equipment, testing methods, illness, weight, and, in this case, drugs that alter blood and muscle. Measurements taken years apart, on different occasions, may not be comparable at all. Case studies are valuable for raising questions. They can't answer them.

When you see a training claim resting on one athlete, however famous, treat it as grade C at best. Efficiency may well improve over years of training. This paper can't show it.

One study, up close: when efficiency makes up for a lower VO2max

In 2002 Alejandro Lucía and colleagues in Madrid tested 11 world-class professional road cyclists [9]. Their average VO2max was 72 mL/kg/min, firmly in the elite range. Each rode a ramp test to find their VO2max, then 20 minutes at a steady power equal to 80% of it, which averaged 385 watts. From the second test, the researchers calculated each rider's efficiency, which averaged 24.5%.

The striking result was the relationship between the two. Riders with a higher VO2max tended to be less efficient, and riders with a lower VO2max tended to be more efficient. The correlations were around −0.65 to −0.72, depending on how VO2max was scaled for body size. The authors concluded that, at the top of the sport, high efficiency seems to compensate for a relatively low VO2max.

It's a small study, and it describes riders who had already reached the top. That matters. If you only study successful professionals, you only see combinations that worked: a rider with a modest VO2max and modest efficiency wouldn't be in the sample, because they wouldn't be a world-class professional. The study can't tell us that training trades one factor for the other, and it doesn't claim to. What it shows is that there is more than one route to the same performance, and that VO2max alone would have ranked these riders wrongly. B

Putting the three together

Because the three factors multiply, a change in any of them changes sustainable power in proportion. Take two riders, as an illustration. Both have the same VO2max. One can sustain 80% of it for an hour and the other 75%. All else equal, the first rider's sustainable power is about 7% higher, simply because 80 is about 7% more than 75. Now make the second rider 7% more efficient, and the two are level again.

That multiplication explains several things that puzzle riders. It's why VO2max alone ranks experienced riders poorly. It's why a training block that leaves your VO2max unchanged can still make you faster, if it raises your threshold or your efficiency. And it's why arguments about whether easy riding or hard intervals are "better" often talk past each other: they may be arguing about different factors. Broadly, and with the details still to come, the case for high-intensity training rests heavily on VO2max, and the case for large amounts of easy training rests on the fraction you can sustain and, possibly, on efficiency.

Joyner and Coyle described elite performance as the product of these factors working together, which is why an elite cyclist can hold around 400 watts for an extended effort [2]. They also warned that this physiological picture leaves things out: the nervous system's role in fatigue, motivation and the realities of racing. Their model explains a great deal, and it doesn't claim to explain everything. Chapter 3 takes up what it leaves out.

The power–duration curve and critical power

There's another way to describe what limits endurance, and it's the one that power meters have made familiar. Ask a rider to produce as much power as possible for 10 seconds, then for a minute, five minutes, twenty and an hour. Plot the results, and you get a curve that falls steeply at first and then flattens out. Riders who use training software know it as their power curve.

In 1965 the French physiologists Hugues Monod and Jean Scherrer noticed something about that curve's shape [10]. For efforts lasting from a couple of minutes to the best part of an hour, the relationship between power and the time it can be held follows a simple mathematical form, a hyperbola, which flattens towards a particular power. They called that power the critical power.

The idea has held up remarkably well. A 2016 review led by David Poole and Andrew Jones describes the hyperbolic power–duration relationship as consistent across species, types of exercise and individual muscles [11]. For cycling it reduces to two numbers. Critical power is the boundary: the highest power at which the body's responses, including oxygen uptake and lactate, can still settle to a steady state. Above it, they can't, and the effort will end. The second number, known as W′ (pronounced W prime), is the fixed amount of work you can do above critical power before that happens. Ride 50 watts above your critical power and you'll use up W′ in a predictable time. Ride 100 watts above it and you'll use it up roughly twice as fast.

Critical power also divides exercise into the intensity domains that will come up throughout this book. Below the lactate threshold is the moderate domain. Between the lactate threshold and critical power is the heavy domain. Above critical power is the severe domain. Martin Burnley and Andrew Jones showed that fatigue behaves differently in each [12]. In the moderate domain it develops slowly and seems to come mainly from the nervous system. In the heavy domain, both the nervous system and the muscles fatigue, often in step with falling muscle glycogen. In the severe domain, the muscle's internal chemistry is progressively disrupted until it can't continue, and that happens faster the further above critical power you ride. They also make a point every cyclist has felt: the range of power you can actually sustain is remarkably narrow. A for the shape of the curve; B for the details of fatigue in each domain

Critical power isn't a fourth factor alongside the other three. It's a different view of the same physiology, because where critical power sits depends on all three: how much oxygen you can use, how much of it you can sustain without losing control of your muscle chemistry, and how efficiently you turn it into power. Its advantage is practical. You can estimate it from a power meter, and it gives researchers a way to measure how the whole system holds up when you're tired.

The fourth factor: durability

Every number in this chapter so far is usually measured on a rested rider. Laboratory tests are done in the morning, after an easy day, with fresh legs. Races aren't.

In 2021 a group of physiologists led by Ed Maunder argued that this was a serious gap. The attributes measured in routine testing aren't fixed: they shift during prolonged exercise, and models that ignore those shifts will misjudge how athletes perform and how they should pace [13]. They proposed measuring durability: when, and by how much, those characteristics deteriorate as exercise goes on.

The size of the problem is now becoming clear. In a 2019 study from Andrew Jones's group, 16 people did a three-minute all-out test fresh, and again after 40 minutes, 80 minutes and two hours of hard cycling. Their critical power held up for the first 80 minutes, but after two hours it was 9% lower [14]. In a 2024 review, Jones described critical power falling by about 10% on average after two hours of heavy-intensity cycling, with individual changes ranging from less than 1% to about 32% [15]. He argued that this resilience to fatigue should be treated as a fourth dimension of endurance performance, alongside the three in Joyner and Coyle's model. B

That range is the important part. Two riders who look identical in a fresh lab test could be almost a third apart after two hours. For anyone training for long events, that may matter more than a few per cent of VO2max. Chapter 9 looks at durability in detail: how it's measured, what the professional peloton shows, and what, if anything, trains it.

What this means for training

The model gives every training question in this book the same shape. A session can make you faster by raising your VO2max, by raising the fraction of it you can sustain, by improving your efficiency, by making all three hold up better late in a ride, or by some combination. Arguments about training methods usually turn out to be arguments about which of these a method changes, and by how much.

The rest of the book takes those questions one at a time. Chapter 2 looks at the biology of adaptation, the chain of events by which a training session, repeated often enough, becomes a bigger heart, more capillaries or more mitochondria. Chapter 3 covers what the brain and nerves contribute. Chapter 4 is about judging the studies. Then Part II asks what easy riding does, and Part III asks the same of hard intervals.

Carry one thing forward from this chapter. Each of the four factors can be trained, but they respond to different stimuli, on different timescales and to different degrees in different people, and some of them respond slowly. A training method that claims to improve everything, for everyone, is claiming more than physiology allows.

Chapter 2: How bodies adapt to training

Draft · 4,908 words · 14 October 2026

In 1966 five healthy 20-year-old men in Dallas agreed to spend three weeks in bed. Physiologists measured their hearts and their VO2max before and after, then put them through a training programme and measured them again. The study became one of the best known in exercise science, mostly for how much fitness the men lost while lying down.

Thirty years later the same five men, now 50, came back to the laboratory. Their VO2max averaged 2.90 litres of oxygen a minute, against 3.30 when they were 20, a fall of 11%. (Those are totals for the whole body, not adjusted for weight like the per-kilogram figures in Chapter 1.) The researchers who retested them concluded that three weeks of bed rest at 20 had done more damage to the men's capacity for physical work than three decades of ageing [1].

The Dallas study is usually told as a warning about bed rest. It also shows how training works. The body does not hold on to capacity it isn't using: a big heart, a large blood volume and muscles packed with mitochondria all cost energy to maintain, and they are kept only while something keeps demanding them. Training is that demand. This chapter follows the chain of events from a single ride to a lasting change in the heart, the blood and the muscle, and sets out how quickly each change arrives and how quickly it goes.

A session is a disturbance

At rest, a muscle cell keeps its internal chemistry within narrow limits. A ride pushes it outside them, and the cell has sensors that register how far.

The first disturbance is to the energy supply. Muscle contraction is powered by ATP (adenosine triphosphate), the molecule every cell uses to carry energy from the fuel it burns to the work it does. ATP has three phosphate groups, and the energy is released when one is split off. A working muscle uses ATP many times faster than a resting one, and it stores only enough for a few seconds, so it has to keep rebuilding it. Each ATP molecule that is spent leaves behind ADP, with two phosphates, and when ADP builds up some of it is converted to AMP, with one. The ratio of AMP to ATP is the cell's fuel gauge, and an enzyme called AMPK (AMP-activated protein kinase) reads it. When energy demand outruns supply, AMP rises and AMPK switches on.

The second disturbance is calcium. A muscle fibre contracts when a nerve impulse makes it release calcium from internal stores, and relaxes when the calcium is pumped back. A fibre that contracts 90 times a minute for three hours is flooded with calcium again and again, and a second family of enzymes, the calcium-dependent kinases (CaMK), responds to that. The third is mechanical: the fibres are stretched and loaded, and proteins that sense tension respond in their turn [2,3].

These sensors are kinases, enzymes that work by attaching a phosphate group to other proteins and so changing what those proteins do. Some of their targets act within seconds: AMPK, for example, tells the cell to take up more glucose and burn more fat during the ride itself. Others act on the cell's DNA, and those are the ones that produce training adaptation.

Every muscle cell carries the instructions for every protein the body can make, and at any moment it is using only some of them. To make more of a protein, such as an enzyme inside the mitochondria, the cell first copies the relevant gene into a short-lived molecule called messenger RNA (mRNA). The mRNA travels out of the nucleus and is read by the machinery that assembles proteins. More mRNA for a protein means, some hours later, more of the protein. The signals set off by exercise change which genes are being copied.

So the outline of adaptation is a chain with four links. A session disturbs the cell. Sensors register the disturbance. Genes are switched on, and mRNA is made. Protein follows. Repeat the session often enough and the extra protein accumulates into something that can be seen under a microscope: more mitochondria, more capillaries around each fibre.

How it works: from one ride to more mitochondria

Much of the signalling from exercise converges on one protein. Its name is PGC-1α, and it came out of research on cold, not exercise. In 1998 Bruce Spiegelman's laboratory in Boston was studying how mice keep warm, and found a previously unknown protein in their brown fat, the tissue that burns fuel to make heat. When the mice were put in the cold, the gene for this protein became far more active, in brown fat and in skeletal muscle. When the researchers put the protein into ordinary white fat cells, the cells made more of the enzymes of the mitochondrial respiratory chain and more mitochondrial DNA [4].

PGC-1α is a co-activator. It doesn't bind to DNA itself. It attaches to the proteins that do, and switches on whole groups of genes together: the ones needed to build a mitochondrion are scattered across the cell's chromosomes, with a few more inside the mitochondrion's own small genome, and something has to coordinate them.

Four years after the discovery, the same laboratory bred mice whose muscles made extra PGC-1α. Muscles that would normally be pale and rich in fast-twitch fibres turned redder. They switched on mitochondrial genes, made proteins typical of slow-twitch (type I) fibres, and when stimulated electrically they resisted fatigue for much longer [5]. Extra PGC-1α, with no training at all, had produced something that looked like an endurance-trained muscle.

That experiment is why PGC-1α is often called the master regulator of mitochondrial biogenesis, the building of new mitochondria. Exercise activates it through the sensors described above, AMPK and the calcium-dependent kinases among them, and it also raises the amount of PGC-1α the cell makes [2].

"Master regulator" overstates it. Mice bred with no PGC-1α at all still increased their mitochondrial enzymes when they were trained for five weeks, by about as much as normal mice, according to a Danish study described by Christopher Perry and colleagues [6]. The cell evidently has other routes to the same result. PGC-1α is the best understood of them and the one most often measured, and that second fact matters later in this chapter, because a great many claims about training rest on measurements of it.

One study, up close: seven sessions, nine biopsies

Most studies of training take a muscle sample before a programme and another after it. That shows what changed and nothing about the order in which it happened. In 2010 Perry and colleagues at the University of Guelph in Canada sampled the muscle all the way through [6].

Nine men in their early twenties, active but not in training, did seven interval sessions over about two weeks. Each session was ten 4-minute efforts at about 90% of maximal oxygen uptake, with 2 minutes of rest between them. A biopsy was taken from the thigh before the first session, and then 4 hours and 24 hours after the first, third, fifth and seventh sessions. Each man gave nine pieces of muscle [6].

Four hours after the first session, the mRNA for PGC-1α had risen more than tenfold. Twenty-four hours after it, the mRNA was back where it had started. The same thing happened after the third session, the fifth and the seventh, with a smaller rise each time. The authors called it a saw-tooth.

The protein behaved differently. PGC-1α protein was 23% above its starting level a day after the first session, and it did not fall back. It kept rising and levelled off at about 40% above the start by the fifth to seventh sessions. The mitochondrial enzymes came later again. The mRNA for citrate synthase, an enzyme commonly used as a marker of how much mitochondrial material a muscle contains, was up 48% after one session, but the activity of the enzyme itself did not rise until after the third session, when it was 17% higher. The third session fell on the fifth day. Another mitochondrial protein, COX-IV, did not increase until after the fifth session [6].

The study shows the chain in order, in human muscle: a burst of message after every session, gone within a day, and protein building up underneath from one session to the next. No single session produced the adaptation, and the authors concluded that the gains in protein came from the cumulative effect of the repeated bursts.

There is a second finding in the data. The men got fitter over the two weeks, and to keep their heart rates at the target the researchers raised the power from 203 watts in the first session to 239 in the seventh. The sessions were harder in absolute terms and the mRNA response still shrank. The authors suggest this may be part of why progress slows as training goes on: the same relative effort disturbs a trained muscle less. That is their interpretation and they label it as one [6].

The limits are those of most biopsy studies. There were nine volunteers and no control group, so nothing rules out an effect of the repeated biopsies themselves. All of them were young untrained men. The training was hard intervals only, for two weeks only. The authors cite one other group that found the same ordering in human muscle. The exact percentages belong to these nine men. B

What changes in the muscle

The discovery that endurance training changes the muscle itself is usually dated to 1967, when John Holloszy at Washington University in St Louis trained rats on a treadmill and found that the capacity of their leg-muscle mitochondria to use oxygen had roughly doubled [7]. Until then, the improvement from training had been credited mainly to the heart.

In people, the changes are smaller over the length of a typical study and just as consistent. In a Scandinavian study from 1977, five volunteers cycled for 40 minutes, four times a week, at 80% of VO2max. After eight weeks the activity of two mitochondrial enzymes in their thigh muscle was about 40% higher, and the density of capillaries, the smallest blood vessels, was 20% higher [8]. In a Swiss study in 2015, 16 untrained men trained three or four times a week for six weeks. The volume of mitochondria in their muscle fibres rose by 43% and the number of capillaries per fibre by 18% [9]. A for the direction of these changes; the sizes vary from study to study

Why this should make a rider faster needs spelling out, because the muscle changes do little for VO2max, as the next section shows.

Take a rider producing 200 watts. That power needs a fixed rate of ATP supply, whatever the rider's fitness. In an untrained muscle, the mitochondria are few, so each has to work at a high fraction of its capacity to meet the demand, and it takes a large disturbance in the cell's chemistry (low ATP, high ADP and AMP) to drive them that hard. That same disturbance accelerates the breakdown of carbohydrate and the production of lactate. With twice the mitochondria, each works at half the rate, the disturbance needed is smaller, more of the fuel is fat, and less lactate is produced at the same 200 watts. This is the explanation Holloszy gave for his own finding, and it is the link to Chapter 1: more mitochondria raise the power at which lactate starts to accumulate, and so the fraction of VO2max that can be sustained.

More capillaries help in a similar way. They shorten the distance oxygen has to travel from blood to mitochondria and give the blood more time in contact with each fibre.

Volume or intensity?

Which kind of training builds mitochondria best is one of the questions this book is about, and the mechanism alone doesn't answer it.

David Bishop's group in Melbourne has reviewed the human training studies twice. Their reading is that the amount of mitochondrial material in the muscle tracks the volume of training, the total amount done, while how well the mitochondria work, measured as the rate at which they can use oxygen, tracks the intensity [10,11]. If that is right, long easy rides and short hard ones are not substitutes: they improve different properties of the same structure. The authors describe these as trends drawn from a small number of studies.

Not every experiment fits. In 2017 Martin Gibala's laboratory in Canada had ten men train one leg with intervals and the other with continuous riding, six sessions over two weeks, with the work done by each leg matched to within a kilojoule per session. Using two legs of the same person removes differences in genes, diet and sleep. Citrate synthase activity ended about a fifth higher in the interval leg [12]. When the total work is the same, in other words, intensity won.

The two findings can both be true. Matching the work forces the comparison onto short sessions, about half an hour here, and nobody doing low-intensity training rides for half an hour. The practical comparison is between the amount of hard riding a person can tolerate in a week and the much larger amount of easy riding they can tolerate, and a single-leg study over two weeks cannot make it. Part II and Part III take up that comparison with the studies that tried. C that volume determines mitochondrial content and intensity determines function; the reviewers themselves call it provisional

What changes in the blood and the heart

The blood adapts in two stages. The first is fast. Early in training the volume of plasma, the liquid part of the blood, expands: the kidneys hold on to more sodium and water, and more protein is kept in the bloodstream to hold the water there. A review by Victor Convertino found that for the first two to four weeks nearly all the increase in blood volume is plasma. After that, red cells start to make up a share [13].

More plasma means more blood returning to the heart between beats. The heart fills further, and a fuller heart ejects more with each contraction, a property of heart muscle known as the Frank–Starling mechanism. Stroke volume goes up, and heart rate at any given power comes down. This is one reason a new rider's heart rate at a fixed effort drops early in training, before the heart itself has changed. It is also the adaptation that heat training exploits, as the chapter on heat describes.

The second stage is slower. The number of red blood cells rises over weeks, under the control of the hormone erythropoietin (the chapter on drugs covers that system in detail). In the Swiss study mentioned above, six weeks of training raised red cell volume by 8% and total haemoglobin mass by 6% [9].

That study then did something unusual. VO2max had risen by 9% over the six weeks. The men's muscles had more mitochondria and more capillaries, and their blood had more red cells. To find out which of those was responsible, the researchers took blood from each man until his red cell volume was back at its pre-training level, and tested him again. VO2max fell by 7%. Most of the gain went with the blood, even though every adaptation in the muscle was still in place [9].

This is direct evidence for the claim in Chapter 1 that oxygen delivery sets VO2max. It applies to untrained men in their first six weeks, and it should not be stretched further than that. It also doesn't mean the muscle adaptations were wasted. They don't show up in a test of maximal oxygen uptake because they act on something else: how much of that maximum the rider can hold. B

The heart itself is the slowest of all. In a study published in 2014, Benjamin Levine's group in Dallas, the successors of the bed-rest researchers, took 12 sedentary adults and trained them for a year, with the aim that each would finish a marathon. By the end they were training seven to nine hours a week. Their hearts were scanned by MRI every three months. VO2max rose from 40 to 49 millilitres per kilogram per minute. The wall of the left ventricle, the main pumping chamber, thickened over the first six months. The chamber itself barely enlarged in that time: its volume was 8% greater at six months, and most of the enlargement came in the second half of the year. After twelve months the hearts had the mass of an elite endurance athlete's, and they were still stiffer, less able to stretch and fill [14].

The study had no control group, which the authors acknowledge, and twelve people. But the pattern is hard to attribute to anything except the training, and it sets a timescale. The large, compliant heart that Joyner and Coyle identified as the mark of a champion takes more than a year of serious training to build, and the authors raise the possibility that part of it has to be built while the body is still growing. B

That last point is a suggestion from the authors, and their study could not test it: a heart that is still stiff after one year might simply need five.

The same laboratory later ran a trial that bears on it. Sixty-one sedentary people with an average age of 53 were randomly assigned to two years of supervised training or to a control group, and their hearts were measured with catheters before and after. In the training group VO2max rose 18%, from 29.0 to 34.4 mL/kg/min, and the left ventricle became measurably less stiff. Neither changed in the controls [15]. A heart that had been sedentary for five decades could still be made more compliant. The trial did not compare its volunteers with elite athletes, so it doesn't say how close to a champion's heart a late starter can get. B

If the authors' suggestion about growth is true, it concerns the very top of VO2max and nothing else. These twelve adults, who had never trained, raised their VO2max by a fifth in a year, and every muscle and blood adaptation described in this chapter was measured in adults. So the worst case is that someone who starts as an adult never gets a champion's heart, while still getting a great deal fitter.

Reading the research: a signal is not an adaptation

In 2010 a group in Dublin had eight sedentary men ride twice. On one occasion they rode at 40% of VO2max and on the other at 80%, in each case until they had burned 400 kilocalories. Three hours after the easy ride, PGC-1α mRNA in their thigh muscle was 3.8 times its resting level. Three hours after the hard ride it was 10.2 times. AMPK was activated immediately after the hard ride and not after the easy one [2].

It is a careful study, and it is easy to read too much into it. The tempting conclusion is that hard riding builds mitochondria nearly three times as well as easy riding. Results like this are quoted in support of high-intensity training all the time. Nothing in the study measured mitochondria.

Start with what was measured: one messenger molecule, at one moment, after one session. Perry's study showed that this message is gone within a day and shrinks as training proceeds, while the protein it codes for keeps climbing. A snapshot of mRNA at three hours says which session produced the bigger burst. It does not say how much protein is eventually made, and it says nothing about what months of such sessions would do.

Then look at who was studied. The men were sedentary. For someone untrained, 40% of VO2max is a gentle effort that barely disturbs the muscle. The response of a trained cyclist to a session at their own endurance pace may be quite different.

And look at what was held equal. To match the energy burned, the easy ride lasted 70 minutes and the hard one 36. A cyclist's weekly long ride is not 70 minutes. The comparison a rider cares about, four hours easy against one hour hard, is not the one the study made [2].

Bishop's group, reviewing this whole field, concluded that the changes seen after single sessions are "not necessarily predictive" of the adaptations seen after training [11]. The same caution cuts in both directions. Signalling studies are quoted for Zone 2 as readily as for intervals, and in both cases they are evidence about mechanism: they show that a pathway exists and that a session activates it. Whether one way of training makes riders faster than another is a question only a training study can answer, by training people and measuring them. Chapter 4 is about how to read those.

How fast it comes, how fast it goes

Put the studies in this chapter side by side and a rough order appears. Plasma volume expands within the first two to four weeks. Mitochondrial enzymes rise within three hard sessions. Capillaries and red cells follow over a period of weeks. The heart's walls thicken over months, and its chambers are still enlarging after a year. These figures come from different studies of different people on different programmes, so the order is more reliable than the exact durations.

What happens when training stops was measured in 1984 by Edward Coyle, then working in Holloszy's laboratory. Seven endurance-trained people stopped training completely and were tested 12, 21, 56 and 84 days later [16].

Their VO2max fell 7% in the first three weeks, and by eight weeks it had settled at 16% below where it began. The early fall came with a drop in stroke volume, which went all the way back to the level of people who had never trained [16].

The muscle lost its gains more slowly and only in part. The activity of mitochondrial enzymes fell with a half-time of 12 days, meaning half of what was going to be lost had gone by then, and settled at a level still 50% above untrained people's. The capillaries did not decline at all in 12 weeks. After 84 days without training, these seven still had a VO2max of 50.8 mL/kg/min, against 43.3 in a comparison group who had never trained [16]. B

For a rider, the useful reading is that the adaptations lost fastest are also the ones regained fastest. Two weeks off the bike costs mainly plasma volume and some mitochondrial enzyme, which the studies above suggest return within a few weeks of riding again. What took years to build, the capillary network and probably the heart's structure, is much more durable. That second sentence is my inference from putting Coyle's findings next to the training studies. No study in this chapter measured the return directly.

From the saddle. When I've been off the bike with an illness, two weeks at most, the first ride back always feels terrible. But I'm always surprised how quickly it comes back, within maybe two or three rides. Sometimes I think the forced rest helps.

Why the same training gives different results

Everything so far describes averages. The spread around them is wide.

The largest study of it is HERITAGE, run in North America in the 1990s. It put 481 sedentary adults from 98 families through the same 20-week training programme. The average gain in VO2max was about 400 millilitres a minute. Some people gained more than a litre a minute, and some gained little or nothing [17].

Because whole families took part, the researchers could ask how much of that spread was inherited. The gains varied 2.5 times more between families than within them, and the statistical model put the heritable share of the training response at up to 47%. How much you improve on a given programme is, to a considerable degree, a family trait. All the participants in that analysis were white, which limits how far the figure can be generalised [17]. B

From results like these came the idea of the non-responder, the person who trains and doesn't improve, and a figure that around one adult in five is one. In 2017 David Montero and Carsten Lundby in Zurich tested whether that is a fixed characteristic of a person [18]. They split 78 healthy adults into five groups, who did one, two, three, four or five one-hour sessions a week for six weeks. A non-responder was defined as someone whose maximal power in a ramp test changed by less than the test's own measurement error, just under 4%. In the group training one hour a week, 69% were non-responders. At two hours it was 40%, at three hours 29%, and at four and five hours none. The non-responders were then given another six weeks with two more sessions a week. Every one of them improved.

The definition deserves attention. A test of maximal power, repeated on the same person with no training in between, will not give the same number twice, and here the difference could be as much as 4% either way. A person whose result "didn't change" may have improved by 3% and had a bad day on the second test. So some of what gets reported as non-response in any study is measurement noise, and the lower the training dose, the more people's real gains are small enough to be hidden by it [18].

Taken together, the two studies say that people differ greatly in how much they gain from a given dose, that much of the difference is inherited, and that in healthy adults at these doses a failure to improve was a matter of too little training, not of a body that can't adapt. They were both done in untrained people. Whether a trained cyclist who has stopped improving on ten hours a week would improve on twelve is a different question, and neither study answers it. B

What this means for training

The signal comes from the tissue that was disturbed. A muscle fibre that wasn't recruited during a session received no calcium flood and no energy shortfall, and it has no reason to change. This is the biology behind the old coaching principle of specificity, and it is why the question in Chapter 1, which part of the model does this session change, has a physical answer: the part it stressed.

Adaptation is cumulative. In Perry's study the message from each session was gone within a day, and the proteins rose in steps across sessions. The mechanism favours training that can be repeated over training that can't, and a session that leaves a rider unable to train properly for four days has to be worth four days of bursts. That is an argument from mechanism, and it should be weighed as one. It is plausible, and Parts II and III look at whether the training studies bear it out.

The same session becomes a smaller stimulus as you adapt to it. In Perry's volunteers that showed within two weeks, even as the power went up. A plan that never changes will stop producing change, and how to progress, by adding volume or adding intensity, is the subject of much of the rest of this book.

The parts of the system keep different time. A new rider's early gains are largely plasma volume and muscle enzymes, which arrive in weeks and leave in weeks. Red cells take longer. The heart takes years. A training block of six weeks, which is the length of most of the studies this book relies on, is long enough to see the fast adaptations and too short to see the slow ones. When a six-week trial finds that one method beats another, it has compared them on the adaptations that arrive quickly. Keep that in mind in every chapter that follows, because the case for years of steady volume rests largely on the adaptations that six-week trials cannot see.

Chapter 3: Effort, pain and the brain

Draft · 4,148 words · 14 October 2026

In 2009 Markus Amann and his colleagues at the University of Wisconsin gave eight cyclists an injection in the lower spine and then asked them to race a 5-kilometre time trial [1]. On one day the injection was fentanyl, a powerful opioid, placed where it would block the nerve signals travelling up from the legs to the brain without affecting the signals going down. The riders could still drive their muscles normally, and their leg strength was unchanged. What they had lost was most of the feedback from their legs, including the pain. On another day the injection was a placebo. Neither the riders nor the researchers running the test knew which was which.

With the feedback blocked, the riders started faster. Their power over the first half of the time trial was 6% higher than on the placebo day. Over the second half it was 11% lower. When the researchers stimulated the nerve to the thigh muscle afterwards, to measure how much force the muscle itself could still produce, it had lost 46% of its force, against 33% after the placebo ride [1].

The riders hadn't been freed to go faster. They had lost the signal that normally stops a rider going out too hard, and their muscles finished in a worse state than the riders would ever have chosen. The experiment shows that in a time trial ridden as hard as possible, the legs are held back from their limit by something the brain does with the information coming up from them. This chapter is about that: what the nervous system contributes to endurance, whether pain or effort is what stops you, whether some people can stand more of it, and whether training changes how much.

From brain to muscle and back

The neuromuscular system is the chain that turns an intention into a pedal stroke. It starts in the motor cortex, the strip of brain that issues commands for movement. The command travels down the spinal cord to motor neurons, nerve cells whose fibres run out to the muscle. Each motor neuron connects to a group of muscle fibres, anything from a few dozen to more than a thousand, and when it fires they all contract together. A motor neuron and its fibres are called a motor unit. To produce more force, the nervous system recruits more motor units and fires them faster.

Traffic also runs the other way. Muscles are full of nerve endings that report back: how stretched the muscle is, how much tension it is under and, through a set of thin fibres known as group III and IV afferents, how its chemistry is changing. Those thin fibres respond to the by-products of hard work, and their signals are what the brain experiences as the ache and burn of exertion. They were the fibres Amann's injection silenced.

Fatigue can arise anywhere along this chain, and physiologists divide it in two. Peripheral fatigue is in the muscle: the same nerve signal produces less force than it did. Central fatigue is upstream: the brain and spinal cord drive the muscle less hard than they could. The two can be told apart by stimulating the nerve directly, as Amann's group did, and seeing how the muscle responds when the brain is bypassed.

A review by the Australian neurophysiologist Simon Gandevia established how common central fatigue is [2]. Even when people try as hard as they can, their nervous system usually does not activate the muscle fully, and during sustained efforts that shortfall grows. "Human muscle fatigue", the review ends, "does not simply reside in the muscle."

How it works: what makes you stop

For most of the twentieth century the standard account of exhaustion was the one that came down from Archibald Hill, whose discovery of VO2max opened Chapter 1. Muscles run short of oxygen, their chemistry is disturbed, and they fail. The brain had no part in it. The South African physiologist Tim Noakes has called this the "brainless model" [3].

Noakes's alternative is that the brain regulates exercise in advance, to make sure it ends before anything is damaged. On this view the brain continuously sets how much muscle it is willing to recruit, using the feedback from the body, the distance still to go and its memory of previous efforts, and the feeling of fatigue is the means by which it enforces that decision. He describes fatigue as an emotion, generated by the brain, and largely independent of the actual state of the muscles at the moment it is felt. The idea is known as the central governor.

A second alternative comes from Samuele Marcora, an Italian physiologist who has spent much of his career in Britain. In 2010 he and Walter Staiano ran an experiment that is easy to describe [4]. Ten fit men rode at a fixed 242 watts until they could no longer keep going, which took about ten and a half minutes. The instant they stopped, they were asked for one all-out maximal effort. They produced 731 watts.

That was well down on the 1,075 watts they could manage when fresh, so their muscles were certainly fatigued. But they had just declared themselves unable to continue at 242 watts, and their legs could still produce three times that. Whatever stopped them, it was not that their muscles could no longer do the work [4].

Marcora's explanation is that people stop when the effort required exceeds what they are willing to give, or when they come to believe the task is impossible. The quantity that matters in his account is the perception of effort, how hard the task feels, which rises steadily during a hard ride until it reaches the most a person will tolerate. Researchers measure it by asking. The usual tool is the scale devised by the Swedish psychologist Gunnar Borg, which runs from 6 to 20, and a rider's score on it is called their rating of perceived exertion [5].

One objection to the experiment is that a maximal effort lasting a few seconds draws on different energy stores from ten minutes at 242 watts, so the comparison flatters the muscle. That is my reservation, and it does not rescue the old model: a muscle that can still produce 731 watts has not failed.

The theories differ on what the brain is responding to, and the experiments in the rest of this chapter don't settle it. They agree on the point that matters for training. At the end of a maximal effort there is still capacity in the muscle, and what a rider can do depends partly on how the brain weighs the sensations coming from the body. B that exhaustion in these tests is not muscle failure; C for any one theory of why

Effort and pain are different things

Riders use "pain" for everything unpleasant about a hard effort. Researchers separate two sensations, and the distinction changes how the evidence reads.

Effort is the sense of how hard you are driving yourself. It seems to come from the outgoing command, the brain's own record of how strongly it is signalling the muscles. Pain is incoming. It is carried by the thin afferent fibres from the muscle and reports that something there is wrong. In the ordinary course of a hard climb the two rise together, which is why they feel like one thing. Experiments can pull them apart.

A study from Marcora's group in 2009 changed effort without touching the muscle [6]. Sixteen people did 90 minutes of a demanding computer task that required sustained concentration, and on another day watched documentaries for 90 minutes. After each they rode to exhaustion at 80% of their peak power. After the documentaries they lasted 754 seconds on average. After the mental task they lasted 640, about 15% less. Their physiological responses to the ride were largely the same on both days. What differed was their rating of effort, which was higher from the start after the mental task and so reached its maximum sooner.

A study by Alexis Mauger at the University of Exeter went at the pain side [7]. Thirteen trained cyclists rode a 10-mile time trial twice, once after taking paracetamol and once after a placebo, in random order and with neither riders nor testers knowing which. On paracetamol they averaged 265 watts against 255, and finished in 26 minutes 15 seconds against 26 minutes 45.

The riders did not report less pain on paracetamol. Their pain ratings were the same in both rides, and so were their ratings of effort, while their heart rates and blood lactate were higher. They had ridden harder until it hurt exactly as much as before. That suggests riders pace a time trial to a level of pain they are prepared to accept, and that a drug which dulls the signal lets them reach that level at a higher power. B

Paracetamol is not a training aid, and that result is not advice. A painkiller taken to ride harder removes a warning the body has reasons for, and the stronger drugs that work the same way are covered in the chapter on drugs, which discusses tramadol.

What the pain is for

Amann's group followed their time-trial study with one in which seven men rode at a fixed 318 watts for as long as they could, with and without the spinal block [8]. If pain were purely a brake, removing it should have let them ride longer. They lasted 6.8 minutes with the block and 8.7 without it.

The reason is that the feedback from the legs does a second job. It tells the brainstem how hard the muscles are working, and the brainstem raises breathing and blood flow to match. With the signal blocked, the riders breathed too little for the work they were doing, their blood carried less oxygen, and their muscles fatigued about a third more by the end.

So the same nerve fibres that make hard riding hurt also make it possible. The burn of a hard effort is the conscious edge of a control system that keeps oxygen delivery matched to demand and keeps the rider from damaging muscle. A rider who could switch it off would not be faster.

From the saddle. When I go into the red early in a ride, well above what I can sustain, it can be really hard to get back from there.

Amann's time-trial riders did the same thing with a drug's help. Riding 6% over their usual power in the first half cost them 11% in the second, and left their muscles more fatigued than a normally paced ride would have [1].

Can some people stand more than others?

Researchers who study pain measure two things. The pain threshold is the point at which a stimulus, such as pressure or cold, is first felt as painful. Pain tolerance is how long or how much of it a person will bear before asking for it to stop.

In 2012 a German group pooled 15 studies that compared athletes with normally active people on laboratory pain tests, 899 people in all [9]. Athletes had clearly higher pain tolerance. The pooled difference was 0.87 standard deviations, which is large: it puts the average athlete above roughly four in five of the comparison group. For pain threshold the studies disagreed, and once the weaker ones were set aside there was no significant difference.

Athletes, then, appear to feel pain at about the same point as everyone else and to put up with it for longer. B

The comparison has the weakness of every comparison between athletes and other people, the one met in Chapter 1 with the professional cyclists in Madrid. It cannot show whether training made the athletes tolerant or whether tolerant people are the ones who become athletes. The next section has the trials that tried to find out.

There is also the question of which pain. Mauger's group later tested 32 recreationally active people on two standard laboratory pain tests, a hand held in cold water and pressure on a muscle, and on a third they designed themselves: how much pain the person would tolerate during cycling when the effort was held at a fixed rating [10]. Then everyone rode a 10-mile time trial. Neither the cold-water test nor the pressure test predicted time-trial performance at all. Tolerance of pain during exercise predicted it strongly, with a correlation of −0.83.

That correlation needs care, because a fitter rider will score well on any cycling test. The researchers checked what pain tolerance added once ordinary fitness was accounted for. Peak power in a ramp test explained 74% of the differences in time-trial performance. Adding exercise-pain tolerance explained a further 7.5%. Fitness is most of it, and pain tolerance is a real but small part of what is left [10]. C: one study, 32 people, and a correlation

Does training change it?

The first trial was Australian [11]. Twelve people cycled for 30 minutes three times a week for six weeks, and twelve carried on as normal. Before and after, everyone took a tourniquet test, in which a cuff cuts off blood flow to the arm and the person squeezes a handgrip repeatedly until they choose to stop. The training group lasted longer after six weeks. The control group did not change. Pain threshold, tested with pressure, did not change in either group, and during the tourniquet test the trained group rated the pain as highly as before. They were tolerating the same pain for longer.

The test was on the arm, and the training was on a bike. Nothing about the arm's muscle had been trained, so whatever changed was in the nervous system.

That study had a flaw its successors pointed out: the volunteers chose their own group, and the control group did no supervised exercise of any kind [12]. The next trial fixed both.

One study, up close: intervals, steady riding and a tourniquet

Thomas O'Leary and colleagues at Oxford Brookes University recruited 20 healthy adults, four of them women, none of whom did endurance training [12]. They were randomly assigned to two groups of ten, balanced for fitness. Both groups trained on a cycle ergometer three times a week for six weeks.

One group did hard intervals: six efforts of 5 minutes, rising to eight after the third week, at a power halfway between the lactate threshold and VO2max, with a minute's rest between. The other group rode continuously at 90% of the power at their lactate threshold, which is roughly the top of what this book calls Zone 2, for as long as it took to do the same total work. That came to between 60 and 80 minutes a session [12].

Before and after, each person took the tourniquet test on an arm, without being able to see a clock. The researcher running the test did not know which group the person was in. Each person also rode to exhaustion at a fixed hard power.

Fitness improved by similar amounts in the two groups: the lactate threshold and VO2max went up about as much with steady riding as with intervals. Pain tolerance did not. In the interval group it rose 41%. In the steady group it changed by −3%, which is no change. As in the Australian trial, the pain ratings during the test were the same as before training, so the interval group were lasting longer at the same pain [12].

The rides to exhaustion split the same way. At the same power as before training, the interval group lasted 148% longer and the steady group 38% longer. And across all 20 people, those whose pain tolerance rose most tended to be those whose time to exhaustion rose most, with correlations of 0.44 and 0.51 for the two versions of the test [12].

The authors' interpretation is that repeatedly riding at an intensity that hurts teaches the nervous system to tolerate it, that this is separate from the gain in aerobic fitness, and that it may be part of why interval training improves hard efforts more than steady training does.

The limits are worth setting out. Twenty people is a small trial, and they were untrained, so six weeks of anything was a large change for them. The pain was in an arm starved of blood, which is not the pain of a climb, and the authors say plainly that tolerance of one kind of pain may not carry over to another. The correlations are modest: at 0.44 to 0.51, changes in pain tolerance go with about a fifth to a quarter of the variation in the performance gain. And the study shows that pain tolerance and performance improved together, which is weaker than showing that one caused the other. B that hard training raises tolerance of this kind of pain; C that this explains any of the performance gain

For this book's question the finding has a plain implication. An hour or more of steady riding just under the lactate threshold improved fitness as much as intervals did and did nothing for pain tolerance. If tolerating discomfort is trainable, the evidence so far says it is trained by discomfort.

Reading the research: when nobody can be blinded

The paracetamol study and the spinal-block studies were double blind. The riders didn't know what they had been given and neither did the people testing them. That matters most when the outcome depends on a decision by the participant, and in this chapter every outcome does: how hard to ride a time trial, when to stop a ride to exhaustion, when to let go of the handgrip.

A training study can't be blinded. Nobody does six weeks of intervals without knowing it. So ask what the participants might have believed. Someone who has spent six weeks doing sessions that hurt may reasonably expect to be better at enduring things, and a person who expects to last longer in a test with no fixed end point will often last longer. The 41% in O'Leary's study is partly a measure of how long people were willing to keep squeezing, and willingness responds to belief.

The researchers did what can be done. The participants were not told the hypothesis, and neither programme was presented as the better one. The tester didn't know the groups. The clock was hidden. The steady-riding group is the most important control, because they also trained hard for six weeks under supervision and could equally have expected to improve, and they didn't. That makes simple expectation a less likely explanation than it would be against a group that did nothing, which was the weakness of the earlier Australian trial.

It doesn't remove the problem. The interval group knew their training hurt and the steady group knew theirs didn't, and that knowledge is exactly the thing that might shape expectations about a pain test. When a study's outcome is the moment a person chooses to stop, check who knew what, and treat the size of the effect with more caution than the direction.

What about professionals?

The search for this chapter turned up no study of pain tolerance in professional cyclists. It did turn up one on a neighbouring question.

In 2016 a group at the University of Canberra and the Australian Institute of Sport tested 11 professional road cyclists and 9 recreational ones [13]. Each rider came in twice. On one visit they spent 30 minutes on the Stroop task, a test in which the names of colours appear in the wrong colour of ink and the person has to respond to the ink and ignore the word. It demands continuous suppression of an automatic response, which psychologists call inhibitory control, and half an hour of it is mentally tiring. On the other visit they did an easy task for 10 minutes. Then they rode a 20-minute time trial.

The professionals were better at the Stroop task itself, with 705 correct responses against 576. After it, the recreational riders' time-trial power fell from 226 watts to 216, a drop of about 4%. The professionals' power was 326 watts after the easy task and 323 after the hard one, which is no difference [13].

So professionals were better at overriding an impulse and less affected by mental fatigue. The link to riding is that holding a hard effort is itself a continuous act of overriding the impulse to ease off.

The same caution applies as to the athletes in the pain studies. The authors say the difference may be inherited, or developed through the training and life of a professional, or both, and their study can't tell. Twenty riders tested once is also a thin basis for a general claim. C

Whether Tadej Pogačar is in more pain than the riders he leaves behind, or less, nobody has measured, and nothing a spectator can see would tell you. What the studies in this chapter suggest about the best riders in general is narrower. They probably don't feel less: athletes' pain thresholds and pain ratings look like everyone else's. They can probably hold the same discomfort for longer and at a higher power, and they may be better at continuing to choose it. Most of the gap between a Tour winner and an amateur is still in Chapter 1.

How much is held back?

If the brain regulates effort with a margin, the margin should sometimes show. In 2012 a group in the north of England had nine trained cyclists ride 4,000-metre time trials against an on-screen avatar, which they were told was a replay of their own best previous ride [14]. In one trial that was true. In another the avatar had been set, without their knowledge, to ride at 102% of the power of their previous best.

They beat their previous best in both, and they went faster against the dishonest avatar than against the honest one. Riders who believed they were already at their limit found more when they thought they had done it before. The authors concluded that cyclists keep a reserve even in a time trial ridden flat out.

It was nine riders, a short effort and a 2% deception. Nobody should conclude that belief can replace fitness. The study is evidence that what a rider treats as their maximum has some give in it [14]. C

What to do with it

Hard sessions probably train something that easy sessions don't, apart from their effects on the heart and muscle. The only randomised comparison found that intervals raised pain tolerance and steady riding matched for work did not. That is one small trial in untrained people, and it is a reason not to drop hard riding entirely even if you are persuaded by the case for volume.

Arrive at hard sessions and events mentally fresh if you can. In the Canberra study, half an hour of concentrated mental work cost recreational riders 10 watts in a 20-minute time trial. A day of difficult meetings is not a Stroop task, and the comparison is loose, but the direction is the one most riders with jobs would recognise: the interval session after a draining day feels harder at the same power. If it does, the studies suggest that is a real effect on perceived effort and not a failure of character [13].

Treat the discomfort of a hard effort as information. The spinal-block experiments show what happens without it: riders start too fast, breathe too little and wreck their legs. Pacing by feel works because the feeling is tied to what the muscles and lungs are doing.

Don't take painkillers to train or race harder. The paracetamol result is interesting as evidence about how pacing works. As a practice it trades a warning signal for a few watts.

This chapter covers a part of endurance that the model in Chapter 1 leaves out, and the evidence for it is thinner than for the heart or the muscle. Most of the studies have between seven and twenty participants, most outcomes depend on a person deciding to stop, and the theories are still argued over. What can be said with some confidence is that the muscle is not the only thing that sets how hard you can ride, and that the rest is not fixed.
