Chapter 4: How to read a training study

Draft · 4,772 words · 14 October 2026

In 2006 Chris Beedie and colleagues at Canterbury Christ Church University told six well-trained cyclists that they were taking part in a study of caffeine [1]. Each rider would do three 10-kilometre time trials, the researchers explained, after a capsule containing either no caffeine, a moderate dose or a high dose. Before each ride the rider was told which one he had been given.

Every capsule was a placebo. When the riders believed they had taken the high dose, they produced 3.1% more power than in their baseline rides. When they believed they had taken the moderate dose, 1.3% more. When they believed they had been given nothing, 1.4% less. All six reported feeling the effects of caffeine [1].

It is a memorable result, and it is the kind that travels: belief alone is worth 3%, and more belief is worth more. There were six riders. The paper gives a range of uncertainty around each of those figures, and all of those ranges include zero: for the 3.1%, it runs from a loss of 0.4% to a gain of 6.7%. And the paper's own wording, that the effect was "likely beneficial", comes from a statistical method that sports science later had to abandon [1].

None of that means the finding is false. The six riders' power followed the order of their beliefs, lowest when they thought they had nothing and highest when they thought they had the most, and that fits the other evidence in Chapter 3 that expectation changes performance. But the study supports a much more modest sentence than the one that gets repeated: a placebo effect on cycling performance probably exists, and six riders cannot say how large it is. C

This chapter sets out the questions that get you from the headline to the modest sentence. They are the questions used to grade every claim in the rest of the book.

Three kinds of evidence

Almost every claim about training rests on one of three kinds of evidence, and the first thing to settle about any claim is which.

The first is mechanism. A study shows that a session switches on a pathway, or raises a marker, and the claim follows by reasoning: this should make you fitter because of what it does in the cell. The single-session studies of PGC-1α in Chapter 2 are mechanism evidence. So are most of the arguments for any new training idea in its first few years, because mechanism studies are quick and cheap and a training study takes months.

The second is observation. Researchers measure people as they are, without changing anything, and look for patterns: professional cyclists who do more easy riding fade less late in a race; athletes tolerate more pain than non-athletes. Observation can study people and timescales that experiments can't, including elite riders over whole seasons. What it can't do is separate cause from effect. Riders who are naturally durable may be the ones who can tolerate big volumes, and people who tolerate pain may be the ones who take up endurance sport.

The third is the experiment, in which the researchers decide who does what. The strongest form is the randomised controlled trial. Volunteers are allocated by chance to the training being tested or to a comparison, so that the two groups differ in nothing systematic except the training, and any difference at the end can be credited to it.

Randomisation matters more than it might seem. In the Australian pain study in Chapter 3 the volunteers chose whether to join the training group or the control group, and the two groups turned out to differ in fitness before anything had happened. The Oxford trial that followed allocated people by a computer-generated list, balanced for fitness, and its result can be trusted further for that reason alone.

The evidence grades used in this book follow from this division. Grade A needs several randomised trials, or a good pooled analysis of them, pointing the same way. Grade B is consistent evidence from trials that are small or short or from a single laboratory, or from one well-designed trial. Grade C is observation, mechanism, case studies or expert opinion. Training fashions typically arrive at grade C and are talked about as though they were grade A.

Who was studied

A result applies to people like the ones in the study, and the people in training studies are mostly not like the reader of this book.

The commonest volunteers are untrained or lightly active young men, often students. In untrained people almost any training works, and works fast: the nine men in the biopsy study in Chapter 2 raised their peak oxygen uptake by 12% in two weeks [2]. A trained cyclist does not gain 12% from anything. So a comparison of two methods in untrained people mostly shows that both work, and a method that wins among beginners may do nothing for someone with five years of riding behind them.

Studies of trained athletes are rarer, smaller and shorter, because trained athletes are few and unwilling to hand over their season to a research protocol. Studies of professionals are almost always observational. Women are under-represented at every level, and several of the studies in this book had none.

Will Hopkins, a sports scientist in New Zealand who has written a great deal about how performance should be measured, put the rule in one line of a 1999 paper: outcomes can be generalised only to athletes with abilities and practices represented in the study [3].

What was compared

A training group that improves has shown very little by itself, because people who are tested twice tend to do better the second time. In a pooled analysis of 101 studies of how repeatable performance tests are, Hopkins and colleagues found that people improved by 1.2% between their first and second attempt at a test with no training in between, and by 0.2% between later attempts [4]. That is why good studies include a familiarisation trial that doesn't count, and why a study with no comparison group can't separate training from practice.

When there is a comparison group, look at what it did. Doing nothing is a weak comparison: it shows the training beats no training. The informative comparisons are between two kinds of training, and there the detail to find is what was held equal.

Two methods can be matched for the total work done, or for the time spent, or for neither. The choice often decides the result. In Chapter 2, intervals beat steady riding for mitochondrial enzymes when the work done by each leg was matched to within a kilojoule. Matching work forces the steady session to be as short as the interval session, which removes the one advantage easy riding has, that far more of it can be done. A study matched for time would give the interval group several times the training stress. Neither design is wrong. They answer different questions, and the abstract will rarely say which question was asked.

Then there is everything around the test itself. A review from the Australian Institute of Sport lists influences on a performance test that studies often fail to control or report: how the test was explained, how much the rider was shouted at, whether they knew how far they had to go, who was watching, whether music was playing, and whether they arrived mentally tired [5]. Chapter 3 showed that several of these change performance by a few per cent, which is as large as the training effects most studies are looking for.

What was measured

Outcomes come at three levels. The lowest is a marker inside the body, such as an enzyme, a messenger molecule or haemoglobin mass. Above that is a physiological capacity like VO2max, the lactate threshold or efficiency, and at the top is performance: how fast or how far.

A change at one level does not guarantee a change at the next. The heat-training chapter describes trials in which haemoglobin mass rose and performance didn't measurably follow. When a study reports only a marker, it has shown that a mechanism was engaged and nothing more.

Performance itself is measured in two ways that produce very different-looking numbers. In a time trial the rider covers a set distance, or does a set amount of work, as fast as possible. In a time to exhaustion test the rider holds a fixed power for as long as possible. The first resembles a race and the second does not: nobody wins a race by lasting longest at 300 watts.

Time to exhaustion is also much noisier. Kevin Currell and Asker Jeukendrup reviewed the reliability of performance tests and found that time-to-exhaustion results vary by more than 10% from one attempt to the next in the same rider, where time-trial results vary by less than 5% [6].

And time to exhaustion exaggerates. The reason is in Chapter 1. Above critical power, the time a rider can last is the fixed reserve W′ divided by how far above critical power they are riding. Take a rider with a critical power of 250 watts and a W′ of 20 kilojoules. At 300 watts they are 50 watts over, and last 400 seconds. Raise their critical power by 4%, to 260 watts, and at the same 300 watts they are only 40 over, and last 500 seconds. A 4% gain in fitness has become a 25% gain in the test.

That arithmetic is an illustration with round numbers. It explains a result like the one in Chapter 3, where six weeks of intervals raised time to exhaustion by 148% [7]. Nobody got 148% fitter. When a study reports that a method improved endurance by half or doubled it, check which test was used before being impressed.

How many people

Of the 21 studies in people described in Chapters 2 and 3, the median had 12 participants. Only three had more than 50. That is normal for exercise science, where every participant may need weeks of supervised training and several muscle biopsies, and it has consequences that are easy to underestimate.

Imagine that one training method really is better than another, by a fixed amount. People vary, so any one trial will measure that advantage with some luck added or subtracted. With few people, the luck is large.

To see how large, I ran a simulation. Suppose the better method truly raises some measure by 3 points more than the other, and that individual results scatter around the group average with a standard deviation of 5 points. That is a modest amount of spread for a training response: in the six-week study in Chapter 2, VO2max rose by 9% on average with a standard deviation of 8 [8]. I simulated 20,000 trials with 10 people in each group, analysed each one with the standard statistical test, and did the same for trials with 50 in each group.

With 10 in each group, the trials observed everything from a small advantage for the wrong method to more than double the true effect. Nine per cent of them pointed the wrong way. Only a quarter reached statistical significance, so three times out of four a real effect would have been reported as "no significant difference".

The trials that did reach significance had something in common. To be significant with so few people, the observed difference has to be big, so the significant trials were the lucky ones: on average they reported an advantage of 5.7 points, almost twice the truth. Journals publish significant results far more readily than others, so the small trials a reader gets to see are drawn mostly from that lucky quarter.

With 50 in each group, 84% of trials reached significance, and those reported an average of 3.3.

The pattern has been documented in real research. In 2013 a group led by Katherine Button showed that studies in neuroscience were typically too small to detect the effects they were looking for, and set out what follows: real effects are missed, the effects that are found are overestimated, and results fail to repeat [9]. Everything in their argument applies to training studies of 10 people a side.

A small study that finds nothing has shown very little, because it would probably have found nothing even if the effect were real. And a small study that finds a large effect has probably overestimated it. The surprising, dramatic result from 16 volunteers is the kind most likely to shrink when someone repeats it with 60.

How big, and how sure

Most results in a training paper come from one calculation. The researchers take the difference they observed between the groups and divide it by its standard error, an estimate of how much that difference would vary by chance if the study were run again with different volunteers. The answer is a t-statistic: the signal measured in units of the noise. A t of 1 means the difference is no bigger than chance routinely produces. By convention a result counts once t passes about 2, and with 10 people in each group, as in the simulation, the threshold is 2.1.

The noise shrinks with the square root of the number of people, so halving it takes four times the volunteers. A small study therefore needs a large observed difference to pass the threshold, which is why the significant trials in the simulation were the ones that had overestimated.

The t-statistic is then converted into a p-value, the number most papers report, and "statistically significant" by convention means the p-value is below 0.05. The p-value answers one narrow question: if the training truly made no difference, how often would chance alone produce a difference at least as large as the one observed? Below 0.05 means less than one time in twenty.

It does not say how big the effect is, and it does not say whether the effect matters. With enough people, a difference too small for any rider to notice will be significant. With too few, a difference that would decide a race will not be.

The size of the effect in real units, such as watts, seconds or per cent, is more useful, and so is the confidence interval around it, the range within which the true effect plausibly lies given the data. A wide interval means the study couldn't pin the effect down.

Whether an effect matters depends on what it is being compared with. Currell and Jeukendrup point out that first and second place in sport are typically separated by less than 1% [6]. Hopkins calculated that to detect the smallest improvement that would change an elite athlete's results, a study needs somewhere between 16 and 65 athletes if each one tries both treatments, and between 65 and 260 if separate groups are used [3]. Compare that with a median of 12.

The best tests available vary by about 1 to 2% from one attempt to the next in the same person [4]. So an improvement of 1%, which would be worth having, is smaller than the noise in a single measurement, and small studies cannot see it. They can detect large effects in untrained people. They are close to blind to the small effects that matter to trained ones.

The trial of EPO on Mont Ventoux, described in the chapter on drugs, shows what reporting looks like when it is done well: 48 riders, randomly allocated, neither riders nor testers knowing who had the drug, and each result given as a difference with its confidence interval, so that the reader can see which effects were pinned down and which were not [10].

Choices made after the data are in

A researcher analysing a training study has many decisions to make: which of the dozen outcomes measured is the main one, whether to leave out the rider who fell ill, whether to compare two intervention groups separately or pool them, whether to adjust for starting fitness.

Each decision is defensible. The trouble comes when they are made after seeing the data, because it is then very easy, without any dishonesty, to settle on the set of choices that yields the more interesting result. In 2011 three psychologists showed by simulation and experiment that this flexibility pushes the rate of false positives far above the one in twenty that the p-value promises [11]. The practice is now called p-hacking.

There are signs of it in sports science. In 2020 a group in Dublin looked at 129 studies published over five months in four leading sports medicine journals and counted how many reported that their main hypothesis had been supported. The answer was 82% [12]. Researchers are not right that often. The likelier explanation is that hypotheses which fail go unpublished, or are quietly replaced by ones the data happen to support.

The field also has a case of its own. For about a decade many sports science papers used a home-grown statistical method called magnitude-based inference, which described results in phrases such as "likely beneficial" and "possibly harmful" and was promoted as better suited to small studies than conventional tests. In 2018 Kristin Sainani, a statistician at Stanford, re-ran the method's own published simulations and showed that at the sample sizes its developers recommended it produced false positives at two to six times the rate of standard testing [13]. Her paper ends: "Magnitude-based inference should not be used." A reader who meets "likely beneficial" in a paper from those years should read it as "not statistically significant by ordinary standards".

The defence against all of this is preregistration: the researchers publish their hypothesis, their main outcome and their analysis plan before collecting data, so that they cannot adjust them afterwards. It is required for clinical trials in medicine and still unusual in training research. When a study is preregistered, the paper says so, and it counts in its favour.

Without it, look for the signs. Be wary when the result appears only in a subgroup, or only after groups were pooled, or only on one of many outcomes, and when the headline measure is not the one the methods section seemed to be building towards.

When studies are repeated

Small samples, flexible analysis and journals that prefer positive results each make published findings less reliable. The direct test of how much damage they do together is to run published studies again and see whether the results come back.

Psychology did this first on a large scale. In 2015 a collaboration of psychologists repeated 100 studies from three of the field's journals, using the original materials where they could and designs with more statistical power [14]. Of the original studies, 97% had reported a significant result. Of the repeats, 36% did, and the effects they measured were half the size. The reading of those figures has been disputed, and one group of critics argued that the project's statistics were flawed and the true rate of replication was high [15]. The problem it pointed to is now known as the replication crisis, and other fields have since checked their own work.

Sports science did so in 2025. A project led from Technological University Dublin recruited volunteer laboratories to repeat 25 studies published between 2016 and 2021 in the field's highest-ranked journals, each drawn at random from the studies a laboratory had the equipment and expertise to repeat [16]. Fourteen of the 25 repeats found a significant effect in the same direction as the original. Seven of them, 28% of the total, also found an effect of a size compatible with the original, which was the project's test of a successful replication. In 22 of the 25 the effect was smaller the second time, and the median shrinkage was 75%.

The authors are frank about the limits. Twenty-five studies cannot stand for a field. Many of the repeats simply doubled the original sample, which the authors say left them with less statistical power than planned, so some real effects will have been missed. And the project covered biomechanics, psychology, physiology, nutrition and injury prevention, in experiments a volunteer laboratory could fit in. A training study that runs for months is far harder to repeat, and I know of no project that has repeated a set of them.

The shrinkage is the simulation's result appearing in real data. The simulated small trials that reached significance reported almost twice the true effect, because they were the lucky ones, and a repeat is a second draw that has not been selected for luck. When a single small study reports a striking effect, the reasonable working assumption is that the true effect is a good deal smaller than the published one. B

Individual results

Many papers now show each participant's change as a separate line or dot, and discuss who responded and who didn't. The plots are appealing, and they are easy to over-read.

Greg Atkinson and Alan Batterham made the problem clear with a simulation [17]. They created an imaginary trial in which every person's true response to a treatment was exactly the same, added ordinary day-to-day measurement variation, and plotted the results. The plot showed what looked like strong responders, weak responders and non-responders. All of the difference between individuals was noise.

Their rule is that individual differences in response can be claimed only by comparison with a control group. If the changes in the training group are no more spread out than the changes in people who did nothing, there is no evidence that anyone responded differently from anyone else. Very few studies, they note, make that comparison.

This is the statistical reason behind the caution in Chapter 2 about non-responders, and it applies to your own training too. One good or bad test result is one draw from your own day-to-day variation.

One study, up close: a trial that gets most of it right

Chapter 2 described a trial from Dallas in which sedentary people in their fifties trained for two years [18]. It is one of the best training trials there is, so it is a fair test of the questions.

What kind of evidence? A randomised experiment. A statistician allocated the volunteers in blocks, balanced for sex.

Who was studied? Sixty-one healthy, sedentary adults aged 45 to 64, chosen from 262 who were screened. People with high blood pressure, a body mass index of 30 or more, or a habit of regular exercise were excluded. The authors say that volunteers willing and able to take on an intensive programme may not represent everyone, and that most of them were white.

What was compared? Training against a control group that was also given something to do: yoga, balance and stretching three times a week for the same two years. Both groups wore heart-rate monitors, and each person in either group had an exercise physiologist following their progress. A control group that is simply sent home knows it is the control group. This one got the same attention as the trainers, which removes one explanation for any difference.

What was measured? The main outcome was named before the trial began: the stiffness of the left ventricle, worked out from a catheter in the heart while the amount of blood returning to it was lowered and then raised. That is a capacity and not a performance, but it is measured about as directly as is possible in a living person, and the cardiologist who measured the heart's volume did not know the pressures. VO2max was measured by collecting the air each person breathed out.

How many, for how long? The researchers calculated in advance that they needed 21 people in each group. They recruited 34 and 27, and 28 and 24 finished the two years. The training group completed 88% of the sessions it was set, each one recorded by a heart-rate monitor [18].

How big, and how sure? VO2max rose by 5.3 mL/kg/min in the training group, an 18% gain, with a confidence interval from 4.15 to 6.40. In the controls it changed by −0.3, with an interval from −1.3 to 0.7. The first interval is nowhere near zero and the second sits across it. The result for stiffness, the main outcome, is less emphatic: the difference between the groups had a p-value of 0.04. The gain in fitness is beyond reasonable doubt, and the change in the heart cleared the conventional bar narrowly [18].

Could expectations have produced it? Everyone knew which group they were in, because nobody can be blinded to two years of intervals. That matters little for a pressure reading from a catheter. It matters more for VO2max, which depends on a person being willing to go to their limit.

Choices after the data were in? The trial was registered, with one main outcome, and overseen by an independent monitoring board. A comparison of men and women is labelled as unplanned, and the authors say it "should be interpreted with caution". The analysis covers the 52 who finished and not all 61 who were randomised, which can flatter a treatment if the people who drop out are the ones it didn't suit. Six left the training group and three the control group. The paper also states that the data will not be shared with other researchers.

Has anyone repeated it? Not at this length. The authors describe it as the longest randomised trial of structured training in healthy middle-aged adults.

The trial does not say which part of the training did the work. The programme mixed easy sessions, sessions near the threshold, four-minute intervals and strength training, and its effects cannot be divided among them. So it shows that training changed these hearts, and it is silent on the question this book is about.

One comparison in 61 people took two years, a staff of exercise physiologists and catheters in 52 hearts, twice. There are few training trials like it for that reason, and most of the evidence in this book is weaker.

The questions

These are the questions this chapter has worked through. They are worth asking of any study, including the ones in this book.

  1. What kind of evidence is it: mechanism, observation or experiment?
  2. If an experiment, were people allocated at random?
  3. Who was studied, and are they like you?
  4. What did the comparison group do, and what was held equal?
  5. Was the outcome a marker, a capacity or a performance, and if a performance, a time trial or a time to exhaustion?
  6. How many people, for how long?
  7. How big was the effect in real units, and how wide was the range of uncertainty?
  8. Could the participants' expectations have produced the result?
  9. Was the analysis fixed in advance, or does the result depend on a subgroup, a pooling or one outcome out of many?
  10. Has anyone repeated it?

The last question is the one that most often settles the matter. One small study is one draw. When several independent groups, using different volunteers and different protocols, find the same thing, the luck averages out, and that is what a grade A means in this book.

There is a limit to what these questions can do, and it applies to this book as much as to anything else. Most of the studies it describes are small, short and done on people fitter or less fit than you. Applying the questions strictly would leave very little standing. The aim is to proportion belief to evidence: to hold the well-supported claims firmly, to treat the rest as reasonable bets, and to know which is which.

Chapter 5: Intensity, properly defined

Draft · 4,841 words · 14 October 2026

In 2006 Stephen Seiler and Glenn Kjerland published the training records of 11 nationally competitive junior cross-country skiers in Norway. Over 32 days the skiers had logged 384 sessions, and the researchers sorted the endurance sessions into three zones by heart rate. Three-quarters fell in zone 1 and 17% in zone 3. Zone 2 got 8%. The paper's summary ended by remarking that elite endurance athletes seem to "train surprisingly little" at that middle intensity [1].

In 2025 a panel of 14 sport scientists and coaches published a paper called What is "Zone 2 training"? It set out how Zone 2 sessions should be done and what they should be expected to achieve, for a kind of training that many people now say should make up a large share of an endurance athlete's week [2]. Seiler was one of the authors.

He had not changed his mind. The zone 2 of the 2006 paper lies between two physiological thresholds. The Zone 2 of the 2025 paper lies below the lower of them. A coach who tells you to stay out of zone 2 and a podcast that tells you to live in Zone 2 may be describing the same week of training.

Most arguments about training intensity have this problem somewhere in them. "Threshold" has at least two meanings and dozens of ways of being measured. "Easy" is often set as a percentage of a maximum heart rate that was itself guessed from the rider's age. Before Part II can ask what easy riding does, the words need fixing to something in the body.

Two boundaries, three domains

Chapter 1 introduced the idea that exercise intensity divides into domains: moderate below the first threshold, heavy between the first threshold and critical power, and severe above critical power. The boundaries are there because the body behaves differently on each side of them, and a 2017 experiment at the University of Exeter shows the difference more clearly than any definition [3].

Eleven recreationally active men rode to exhaustion at a constant power in each domain, on separate days, with muscle biopsies taken from most of them. In the moderate domain, at 20 W below their first threshold (113 W on average), they lasted three and a half hours. Their blood lactate barely moved from its starting level. When they stopped, the glycogen in their thigh muscle was down by 83%. The likeliest reason they stopped is that they had run out of fuel [3].

In the heavy domain, at an average of 231 W, they lasted 43 minutes, and the individual times ran from 20 minutes to 67. Twice the power had cut the time by four-fifths [3].

In the severe domain they did several rides at different powers, and lasted between 2 and 14 minutes. Every rider reached his maximum oxygen uptake on every one of those rides. The chemistry inside the muscle at the point of stopping (its acidity, its lactate, its store of phosphocreatine) did not differ measurably whether the ride had taken 2 minutes or 14. Above critical power the riders stopped at a fixed chemical limit, and how hard they were riding decided only how soon they reached it [3]. B

Martin Burnley and Andrew Jones had described the same pattern from earlier work [4], and the review led by David Poole and Jones calls the boundaries consistent across species and kinds of exercise [5]. That there are three domains, with different fatigue, different fuel use and different limits, is well established. What is unsettled is how to find the two boundaries in a given person, and what to call them.

How it works: lactate, and why breathing changes

Both boundaries were first found in the blood and the breath, so that is the place to start.

Muscle breaks glucose down in two stages. The first, glycolysis, is fast, needs no oxygen and ends in a molecule called pyruvate. The second takes place in the mitochondria, uses oxygen, and extracts most of the energy. When glycolysis runs faster than the mitochondria take up pyruvate, the surplus is converted to lactate. This happens all the time, at rest included. Lactate then moves out of the fibre that made it and into the blood, and other fibres, the heart and the liver take it up and burn it or turn it back into glucose. The physiologist George Brooks, who spent a career establishing this, calls the traffic the lactate shuttle: lactate is a fuel being passed between cells, and a signalling molecule as well [6].

The concentration in the blood is therefore a balance. It is the rate at which lactate appears minus the rate at which it is removed, and it says little about either on its own [7]. At rest and during easy riding the two are equal and the level sits near its baseline, around 1 mmol/L in most people. At the first threshold, production begins to outrun removal. The level rises and then settles at a new, higher balance. Push further and you reach an intensity at which it can no longer settle: lactate climbs for as long as the effort lasts.

Breathing follows. Lactate leaves the muscle together with hydrogen ions, which would make the blood more acidic if nothing absorbed them. Bicarbonate in the blood absorbs them, and that reaction releases carbon dioxide. The extra carbon dioxide has to be breathed out, so above the first threshold breathing rises faster than oxygen uptake does. That is the first ventilatory threshold, and it is why holding a conversation gets harder at about the point where lactate begins to rise. At the second threshold the buffering falls behind, the blood starts to acidify, and breathing rises again, now faster even than carbon dioxide output. A laboratory with a gas mask can see both breakpoints without taking any blood.

The man who connected breathing to lactate was Karlman Wasserman, in a 1964 paper with a colleague on detecting what its title called the threshold of anaerobic metabolism in cardiac patients [8]. His explanation was that the working muscle had run short of oxygen and switched to making energy without it. The method survived and the explanation did not. Lactate rises because glycolysis speeds up as the work gets harder, and muscle that has all the oxygen it needs produces it [7,9]. A 2021 review of the argument, with Brooks and Poole among its authors, carries the subtitle "50+ years of controversy" [9]. The name is still in everyday use. When you see "anaerobic threshold", read it as a threshold of some kind, and find out which.

One more change starts at the first threshold, and it matters more to a rider than lactate does. Below it, a given power costs a fixed amount of oxygen, and heart rate and breathing level off within a few minutes. Above it, the oxygen cost of holding the same power creeps upward for many minutes. Physiologists call this the slow component of oxygen uptake. It reflects the muscle becoming less efficient as it tires and as it brings in fast-twitch fibres that use more oxygen for the same work [10]. A steady effort above the first threshold therefore costs more than its power suggests, and costs more the longer it goes on.

The first threshold

It has many names: first lactate threshold (LT1), first ventilatory threshold (VT1), gas exchange threshold, aerobic threshold. They describe the same transition seen through different instruments, and where studies have used both lactate and breathing on the same riders, the instruments agree reasonably well. In 14 well-trained cyclists the power at the lactate threshold and at VT1 did not differ significantly, although the average gap was about 14 W and the correlation between the two was 0.72, which leaves room for larger gaps in individuals [11]. In 50 trained cyclists tested in Germany, in a study this chapter returns to, VT1 came at 200 W on average and the first rise in lactate at 199 W [12].

There is more than one way to read each instrument. A 2009 review found 25 separate lactate threshold concepts in the literature and sorted them into three families: fixed concentrations, the first rise above baseline, and methods aimed at the highest steady state [13]. The first-rise family alone includes "0.5 mmol/L above baseline", "1.0 above baseline" and several curve-fitting methods, and they give different answers from one set of blood samples [14].

Where the first threshold sits is a personal matter. Danilo Iannetta and colleagues in Calgary tested 100 men and women and found it at anything from 45% to 74% of VO2max, from 60% to 90% of maximum heart rate, and from 23% to 57% of the peak power reached in a ramp test [15]. Seventy per cent of maximum heart rate was in the moderate domain for some of those people and in the heavy domain for others. Their conclusion was that prescriptions based on fixed percentages of a maximum "do not adequately control the metabolic stimulus". B

The threshold also moves. Training raises it: Chapter 1 gave the figures, from around 60% of VO2max in untrained people to 75–90% in elite athletes [16]. And it falls during a long ride. In a study from Auckland that Chapter 9 describes in full, two hours of easy riding lowered the power at VT1 from 217 W to 196 W [17]. A number from a fresh laboratory test describes the first hour of a ride better than the fourth.

The second threshold

The upper boundary has more names and less agreement: second lactate threshold (LT2), second ventilatory threshold (VT2), respiratory compensation point, maximal lactate steady state, onset of blood lactate accumulation, critical power, functional threshold power, and, again, anaerobic threshold.

The oldest practical version is a fixed number. In the 1970s and 1980s physiologists, most of them German, took 4 mmol/L of blood lactate as the mark of the highest sustainable intensity [18]. In 1979 Wilfried Kindermann's group had seven national-level cross-country skiers run on a treadmill for at least 30 minutes at the speed that an earlier test had matched to 4 mmol/L. Their lactate rose to nearly that level and then stayed there for the rest of the run [19]. A rule that is still in use rested, at the start, on seven skiers.

It didn't hold up as a universal number. The lactate level at which a person's steady state tops out varies widely between individuals and between sports [18]. So the definition moved from a concentration to a procedure. The maximal lactate steady state (MLSS) is found by riding for 30 minutes at a constant power, on four or five separate days at different powers, and taking the highest power at which blood lactate rises by less than 1 mmol/L between the tenth minute and the thirtieth.

A 2018 study from Victoria University in Melbourne shows how far the shortcuts miss [14]. Seventeen trained cyclists had their MLSS measured the long way: it averaged 264 W. They also did stepped tests with stages of 3, 4, 7 and 10 minutes, and the researchers calculated 14 different lactate thresholds from each. The fixed 4 mmol/L rule put the threshold at 306 W when the stages lasted 3 minutes and at 279 W when they lasted 10, which is between 14 W and 41 W too high. The same 17 riders had 56 lactate thresholds between them, and the authors judged many of the traditional methods not valid.

MLSS has its own critics. Andrew Jones and colleagues argued in 2019 that it underestimates the true boundary by construction [18]. If the trial powers are 30 W apart and you take the highest one that passed, you will be about 15 W low on average. A lactate meter has an error of 0.2 to 0.4 mmol/L, in a test that turns on a difference of 1.0. Their candidate for the real boundary is critical power, which Chapter 1 described: the power the curve of your best efforts flattens towards. In the critical power model the boundary is defined by performance, and the physiology follows: just below it oxygen uptake and lactate stabilise, and just above it they climb to their maximum. A 2020 review of every common method concluded that there is some evidence for the first lactate and ventilatory thresholds as the lower boundary, and little evidence for anything except critical power as the upper one [20]. The authors of the 2019 paper are the researchers most associated with critical power, and their opponents point out that a rider can hold their measured critical power for only 20 to 30 minutes, which is short for something described as sustainable [18]. The gap between the two measures is small on a graph and large on the road. MLSS is, by its definition, a power that can be held for half an hour with lactate stable. Critical power as usually measured can be held for 20 to 30 minutes and no longer.

Most cyclists meet the second threshold as functional threshold power, or FTP. The term comes from Andrew Coggan, who defined it as the highest power a rider can maintain in a quasi-steady state for about an hour, and offered a field test: ride as hard as you can for 20 minutes and take 95% of the average [21]. It is a performance measure standing in for a physiological one, and whether it stands in well has been tested.

Reading the research: agreement on average is not agreement for you

In 2019 a Brazilian group compared FTP with the maximal lactate steady state in 15 trained cyclists [22]. On average FTP differed from the power at MLSS by 1.4%. The correlation between the two was 0.91, which the paper called nearly perfect. The authors concluded that trained cyclists can use FTP as a practical alternative to the laboratory measure.

A year later a Canadian group ran much the same comparison in 18 people [23]. The power at MLSS averaged 88.5% of 20-minute power, so the standard 95% set FTP about 7% too high. Ten of the riders came back after seven months of training. Their MLSS had risen by 12 W and their FTP had not risen. These authors concluded that using FTP to represent MLSS "would not be advisable".

The two papers are closer than their conclusions. The Brazilian abstract reports a third number beside the 1.4%, the 95% limits of agreement, which were plus or minus 9.2%. Limits of agreement describe individuals. They say that for 95% of riders like these, FTP will fall within about 9% of their MLSS in either direction. For a rider whose steady state is truly at 250 W, that is an FTP anywhere from roughly 230 W to 275 W. Among the less trained riders in the group the limits were plus or minus 11.8% [22].

A correlation of 0.91 doesn't contradict this. Correlation asks whether riders with a high MLSS also have a high FTP, and across a group that runs from modest club riders to very fit ones, they will. It would still be 0.91 if every rider's FTP were 30 W too high.

When a paper says a cheap test is valid against an expensive one, the average difference tells you whether the cheap test is biased, the limits of agreement tell you how wrong it can be for one person, and the correlation tells you least. Chapter 6 applies the same check to the methods for finding your own zones.

Three zones, five zones, seven

Once there are two boundaries, the simplest scheme has three zones: below the first, between the two, above the second. The framework goes back at least to a 1980 paper by James Skinner and Thomas McLellan, which described three phases in the transition from easy to maximal work [24]. Seiler adopted it for his studies of how athletes distribute their training, and most research on the subject has used it since [25]. In this scheme zone 2 is the middle band, the heavy domain.

Coaches wanted finer divisions. The scale used by the Norwegian Olympic federation has five zones. Its zones 1 and 2 both lie below the first threshold, zone 3 is the band between the thresholds, and zones 4 and 5 lie above the second [25]. Its Zone 2 is given as 72–82% of maximum heart rate, or a blood lactate of 1.5 to 2.5 mmol/L [12]. Most heart-rate zone systems in apps and bike computers have five zones of this general kind, and this is the Zone 2 of the podcasts.

Coggan's power levels, widely used in power-meter software, number seven and are all set as percentages of FTP. Level 2, "endurance", is 56–75% of FTP. Level 3, "tempo", is 76–90%. Level 4, "lactate threshold", is 91–105%, and three more levels sit above that [21]. These zones are easy to calculate, which is why software uses them. But the first threshold appears nowhere in the calculation. The top of Level 2 is wherever 75% of your FTP happens to be. In Iannetta's 100 volunteers the first threshold ranged from 23% to 57% of peak ramp power and the upper steady state from 44% to 71% [15], and with ranges that wide the gap between a person's two thresholds is unlikely to be a fixed proportion. A zone system built from one anchor has to assume that it is.

A second confusion hides in the numbers people quote. When Seiler reported that elite athletes do about 80% of their training at low intensity, he was counting sessions: a session with 20 minutes of hard intervals in it counts as a hard session, though most of its minutes are easy. In 2014 his group measured how much this matters, using 570 sessions by 29 elite cross-country skiers at a training camp [26]. Counted by session, 87% were zone 1, 11% zone 2 and 2% zone 3. Counted by minutes of heart rate in each zone, the same training was 96%, 3% and 1%. Someone who trains "80/20" by sessions and someone who trains "95/5" by time may be doing the same thing.

What Zone 2 means now

The 2025 panel was convened because the term had spread without a definition. Its 14 members agreed that Zone 2 training "should preferably be performed at intensities located immediately below the first lactate or ventilatory threshold", and that it can be done as continuous, variable or interval sessions [2]. They expected a broad range of adaptations from it, and added that these "might not be unique" to Zone 2 and could come from riding slightly harder or slightly easier. That last admission is easy to miss, and it is the subject of Part II. The definition is expert opinion and carries no more weight than that, but it is the nearest thing to an agreed meaning, and this book starts from it. C

The panel left one choice open, between lactate and breathing [2], and the book makes it. From here on the top of Zone 2 is the first lactate threshold: the last intensity before blood lactate rises 0.5 mmol/L above the rider's own baseline, in a test with stages of at least four minutes. Lactate is what the case for Zone 2 was built on, a rider can measure it with a handheld meter, and a rise above one's own baseline travels between people better than a fixed figure such as 2 mmol/L. The breathing threshold is its closest relative and on average falls close to it. Chapter 6 gives the reasons in full, along with the evidence that the choice is a practical one: no study shows lactate to be the better boundary of the two.

In practice the label is attached to many other things. The Canadian review that criticised the Zone 2 fashion listed the definitions it found in use: below the first lactate threshold, taken as 2.0 mmol/L; below the first ventilatory threshold; below the intensity of peak fat burning; under 45% of VO2max [27]. It also pointed out how far the same definition travels between people. The top of Zone 2 can be 24% of VO2max in a sedentary person, which is a walk, and 80% in an endurance athlete, which may be 300 W.

One study, up close: nine markers, fifty cyclists

In 2025 Benedikt Meixner and colleagues, working from the University of Würzburg, asked what happens when you apply the common definitions of Zone 2 to the same riders [12].

They recruited 50 cyclists, 30 men and 20 women, all with more than three years of regular riding. Their average age was 31 and their average VO2peak 54 mL/kg/min: trained amateurs [12]. Each came to the laboratory twice within a week. The first visit was a ramp test to find maximum oxygen uptake and peak power. The second was a step test, starting at 100 W and rising by 25 W every three minutes, with a mask measuring breathing gases and a drop of blood taken from the earlobe at the end of each stage. From the step test the researchers calculated the power at which each rider reached nine markers: three lactate concentrations, a rise of 0.5 mmol/L above baseline, VT1, 65% of VO2peak, peak fat burning, and 72% and 82% of maximum heart rate, the edges of the Norwegian Zone 2.

On average the markers ran from 125 W to 224 W. Three of them agreed: VT1 at 200 W, the first rise in lactate at 199 W, and 82% of maximum heart rate at 202 W. A lactate of 2.0 mmol/L came at 211 W and 2.5 mmol/L at 224 W, both above the first threshold for the average rider. Peak fat burning came far lower, at a median of 125 W, and the authors put it at roughly a quarter below VT1 as a share of peak power. A rider who sets Zone 2 by fat burning and one who sets it by 2 mmol/L of lactate are 86 W apart on these averages, and the second is no longer below the first threshold [12].

The Norwegian scale's two definitions disagree with each other. Its heart-rate band, 72–82% of maximum, ran from 148 W to 202 W in these riders. Its lactate band, 1.5 to 2.5 mmol/L, ran from 194 W to 224 W. The two bands share 8 W, and the lower lactate figure comes from only 41 riders. The other nine never showed a lactate below 1.5 mmol/L at any point in a test that started at 100 W, so for them the lactate version of the zone has no lower edge [12].

For individuals the picture is looser still. VT1 sat at 81% of maximum heart rate on average, with a standard deviation of 3.9 percentage points. Roughly a third of riders were therefore outside the range 77.5–85.3%. The authors note that a difference of five percentage points is about 10 beats a minute [12].

The study's headline finding needs care. The abstract reports "substantial variability", with coefficients of variation between riders of 6% to 29% depending on the marker. Those coefficients were calculated on raw watts and raw beats per minute. Riders differ in fitness, and in this group peak power itself varied by 19% and maximum heart rate by 5.7%. A marker that sat at an identical percentage of every rider's maximum would still show variation of about that size. Most of the 6–29% is the spread of fitness in the sample, and it says little about whether the markers are good or bad. The stronger evidence is in the table of averages and in the agreement analysis, which the paper shows only as charts [12].

Nobody in the study did any training. It shows that the markers land in different places, and it can't show which of them makes the better ceiling for an easy ride. It was one laboratory, one step protocol and riders mostly in their twenties and thirties. The authors declared no funding and no conflicts of interest. It does establish that the common definitions of Zone 2 pick out different intensities in the same rider. B

Power, heart rate and feel

A zone can be written in watts, in beats per minute or as a feeling, and each is a different kind of measurement. Power is what you put in. It responds instantly and takes no account of the day you are having. Heart rate is one of the body's responses. It lags behind a change in effort, and it drifts upward over a long ride for reasons Chapter 9 explains. The domains are defined by the body's response, so neither reading is the thing itself.

The Auckland study shows the two readings coming apart. After two hours the power at the first threshold had fallen by 21 W, and the heart rate at the threshold had risen from 142 to 151 beats per minute [17]. A rider who set out at the threshold power from the fresh test would have been 21 W above the threshold by the end. A rider who held the heart rate from the fresh test would have finished further below the threshold than intended.

Perceived effort does better than its reputation. In the 2006 study the skiers also rated each session on a scale of perceived exertion of the kind Gunnar Borg introduced [28]. Sorted by those ratings, the sessions came out at 76% zone 1, 6% zone 2 and 18% zone 3, against 75%, 8% and 17% by heart rate [1]. Over a month of training, the athletes' own ratings and their heart rate monitors gave the same account. Chapter 6 covers the talk test, which turns the same sense into a method.

From the saddle. George: which zones did you start out with: the ones your bike computer, Strava or a training app gave you? Did you know at the time what they were percentages of? And when you first tested your own threshold, how far did your Zone 2 move?

The terms as this book uses them

From here on the book holds each word to one meaning.

  • The first threshold is the first lactate threshold (LT1): the intensity at which blood lactate first rises above the rider's own baseline, taken as a rise of 0.5 mmol/L in a test with stages of at least four minutes. Breathing first rises out of proportion to oxygen uptake at about the same point, the first ventilatory threshold (VT1). Where a study measured the threshold from breathing, the text says so.
  • The second threshold is the highest intensity at which the body can still reach a steady state. Where a study measured critical power, MLSS or FTP, the text names the measure, because they are not interchangeable.
  • Easy, or low intensity, means below the first threshold: the physiologists' moderate domain and zone 1 of the three-zone model. Physiologists call this intensity moderate and riders call it easy, and the book uses the riders' word.
  • Zone 2, capitalised, means the upper part of that easy range, immediately below the first threshold, as the 2025 panel defined it. When a study uses the three-zone model, its zone 2 appears here as "between the thresholds".
  • Between the thresholds is the heavy domain. Riders call parts of it tempo, sweet spot and threshold; the book uses those words only for a session a study itself gave that name.
  • Hard, or high intensity, means above the second threshold, in the severe domain.

Whenever a study, a coach or an app uses one of these words, two questions are worth asking. Which of the two boundaries is meant, and on which map? And how was the boundary found for the person doing the training: measured, or calculated as a percentage of something else? Several of the studies in Part II set intensity as a percentage of a maximum, and Iannetta's figures mean that some of their volunteers were probably not training in the domain the paper's title names.
