Chapter 6: Finding your zones

Draft · 6,205 words · 14 October 2026

The top of Zone 2 is the first lactate threshold. Chapter 5 described where that boundary comes from. Muscle makes lactate all the time and the body clears it all the time, and the level in the blood is the balance between the two [1]. During easy riding the balance holds near its baseline, around 1 mmol/L in most people. At some power, different for every rider, production begins to outrun clearance and the level steps up. That power, and the heart rate that goes with it, is the first lactate threshold. Riding below it is easy in the sense this book uses the word, and Zone 2 is the band immediately beneath it.

Most riders have never had it measured. They ride to a heart-rate zone that a watch worked out from their age, or a power zone that an app worked out from a 20-minute test, and both are guesses at where the lactate threshold probably lies. The threshold is the target. A lactate test measures it, and everything else (a talk test, a chest strap, a percentage of maximum heart rate) is a stand-in whose worth depends on how closely it tracks the blood.

Why the boundary matters

Below the first lactate threshold a ride settles. Within a few minutes of a change in power, oxygen uptake, heart rate and lactate level off and stay level. Above it, as Chapter 5 described, the oxygen cost of holding the same power creeps upward for many minutes, because the muscle is tiring and calling on less efficient fibres [2].

How long the effort can last changes as well. In the Exeter experiment of Chapter 5, men riding 20 W below their first threshold lasted three and a half hours, and stopped when the glycogen in their muscles had nearly run out. Between the thresholds they lasted 43 minutes [3].

The cost afterwards also changes. In 2007 Stephen Seiler and colleagues had nine highly trained runners do four sessions on separate days: an hour below the first threshold, two hours below it, an hour that included 30 minutes between the thresholds, and an hour of hard intervals above the second. After each session the runners lay down, and for four hours the researchers followed their heart rate variability, a marker of how far the nervous control of the heart has been disturbed. After the easy sessions it was back to normal within 5 to 10 minutes, and two hours had cost no more than one. After the session between the thresholds it took about 30 minutes, and after the hard intervals it took the same 30 minutes. The authors called the first threshold a "binary" one for recovery: what counted was which side of it the session had been on [4]. B

That is nine runners and one marker of recovery, and the boundary in that study was found from breathing. It is still the clearest evidence for why the top edge of Zone 2 matters. Below it, training time is cheap: the effort settles, it can go on for hours, and it leaves little to recover from. A rider who drifts across the threshold is paying a price that the session's label does not show.

Zones are a way of knowing which side of a boundary you are on. The body has two such boundaries, and the five or seven zones of a watch or an app are subdivisions of the three domains they create. Whether riding just under the first threshold builds more fitness than riding well under it is a separate question, which Part II takes up. The 2025 panel expected much the same adaptations a little above Zone 2 and a little below it [5]. The case for finding the threshold rests on what it costs to cross it.

Why lactate

The 2025 panel placed Zone 2 immediately below "the first lactate or ventilatory threshold" and left the choice between the two open [5]. This book makes the choice. The top of Zone 2 is the last intensity before blood lactate rises 0.5 mmol/L above the rider's own baseline, in a test with stages of at least four minutes. The breathing threshold, and every other method in this chapter, is treated as a way of estimating that point.

The first reason is that lactate is what the argument for Zone 2 was built on. The case made on podcasts descends from work such as a 2018 paper by Iñigo San-Millán and George Brooks, which measured blood lactate and fat burning during exercise in professional endurance athletes, moderately active people and patients with metabolic syndrome. The professionals burned more fat and carried less lactate, and within each group the two measures moved in opposite directions, with correlations stronger than −0.9. The authors proposed blood lactate as an indirect reading of how well a person's mitochondria are working [6]. Whether that reasoning supports the training advice is Part II's subject. If the advice is to ride at the highest intensity that keeps lactate down, then the way to know you are following it is to measure lactate.

The second reason is that a rider can measure it. The first ventilatory threshold needs a mask, a gas analyser and somebody to read the result, and readers differ. In 1983 four exercise physiologists, working independently, marked the threshold on the breathing data from eight people's tests, and for a given person their answers spanned 16% on average [7]. A lactate test needs a drop of blood and a meter.

The third is the form of the rule. The Zone 2 of the podcasts is often given as a fixed figure, a lactate below 2 mmol/L [8]. Baselines differ between people. In the 50 cyclists of Chapter 5, 2.0 mmol/L arrived at 211 W on average, 12 W beyond the first rise, and nine of the riders never recorded a value below 1.5 at any power [9]. Handheld meters also tend to read lower than laboratory analysers [10,11]. A meter that reads low delays a fixed 2.0 and sets the zone too high, and it should make little difference to a rise measured against the same meter's baseline.

No study I have read shows the lactate threshold to be the better boundary of the two. A 2020 review of every common method found some evidence for both the first lactate threshold and the first ventilatory threshold as the lower boundary [12]. In the one study here that tested the same cyclists twice, the breathing threshold was slightly the more repeatable, with a standard deviation of 13 W in the retest differences against 19 W for the lactate threshold [13]. The two do not always coincide. In three studies of trained cyclists that measured both, the lactate threshold came out level with the breathing threshold, 14 W above it and 24 W above it [9,13,14]. And nobody has run the trial that would settle the matter, with one group training to a ceiling set by lactate and another to a ceiling set by breathing. The choice of lactate is made on lineage and practicality. C

The definition also holds only for a test of the right kind. Lactate takes time to pass from muscle into blood, so in a test that climbs quickly the blood lags behind the legs. In a study that raised the power by 25 W every minute, a rise of 0.5 mmol/L above baseline did not appear until 260 W, level with those riders' maximal lactate steady state at 255 W. In that test the rule found the second threshold [13]. In a study with stages of three to ten minutes, the same rise appeared 30 to 40 W below the maximal lactate steady state, and moved by only 10 W between the shortest stages and the longest [15]. The rule needs stages of several minutes, and this book uses four.

Measuring it

A laboratory lactate test is a step test. The rider starts easy and the power goes up every few minutes. At the end of each stage a drop of blood is taken from an earlobe or a fingertip and read on an analyser, and the values are plotted against power. For the first several stages they stay close to level, and then they begin to climb. That early level is the baseline, and the first threshold is read where the curve passes 0.5 mmol/L above it, the rule used in the Würzburg and Leuven studies [9,14].

The same test can be done at home with a handheld meter, test strips and the kind of spring-loaded lancet that people with diabetes use. When such meters were checked against laboratory analysers, the typical error on repeated readings of the same blood at low concentrations ran from 0.1 mmol/L for the best of three meters to 0.5 for the worst [10], and five models tested later were generally within 0.5 [11]. Against a rise of 0.5 that is not a comfortable margin, so a single high reading should not be believed until the next stage confirms it. The Leuven laboratory described below took a second sample whenever the first rise appeared, for the same reason [14].

The meter's error also bears on what to call the baseline. The papers that use the rule do not agree on it. One averaged the readings of the early stages [13], the Würzburg group's label for its baseline points to the lowest reading of the test [9], and others do not say [15,14]. This book takes the average of the early stages, because one stray low reading moves an average less than it moves a minimum. An average is never lower than the minimum, so on some curves this places the threshold a stage later than the lowest-reading version would. Early readings that differ by a tenth or two, or a second stage that reads lower than the first, are within the error of the meter and no reason to start again.

Technique matters as much as the meter. Sweat contains lactate, and a drop of blood that picks some up from the skin will read high. The skin is washed, dried and wiped before each sample, and the first drop is discarded. These are standard laboratory habits, and I have no figures for how much each one matters at home.

Even in a laboratory the answer moves from one test to the next. Fourteen trained cyclists did the same test twice. Their lactate threshold shifted by 2 W on average, and the differences had a standard deviation of 19 W, so for about a third of the riders the second test put the threshold more than 19 W from the first [13]. That test sampled blood only every 50 W, which will have widened the scatter. One test fixes the threshold to within perhaps 10 or 20 W, and a second on another day is worth the strips.

The test gives two numbers to ride by: the power at the threshold and the heart rate at it.

From the saddle. I'm too squeamish for a home lactate test. I wish I could do one, and that's why proxies are good for me. I set my own Zone 2 from a percentage of maximum heart rate, taken from the zones Strava suggested from my highest recorded heart rate. George: two details would sharpen this. Was that maximum from a deliberate all-out effort, or the highest figure that turned up on a ride? Is the 142 bpm in the Introduction where Strava's Zone 2 put you? And would you let a laboratory do the pricking for you?

Breathing, and the talk test

The nearest relative of the lactate threshold is the first ventilatory threshold (VT1). Chapter 5 gave the link: lactate leaves the muscle with hydrogen ions, buffering them releases carbon dioxide, and breathing rises to clear it. A laboratory reads VT1 through a mask. At home the only instrument for it is speech.

The talk test is credited to a Professor Grayson, who in 1939 is said to have advised British mountaineers to climb no faster than they could speak, though the paper that repeats the story gives no source for it [16]. The modern version comes from Carl Foster's group at the University of Wisconsin–La Crosse. During a test of rising intensity, at the end of each stage, the person recites a set passage aloud and is asked whether they can speak comfortably. "Yes" makes it a positive stage, anything uncertain makes it the equivocal stage, and "no" makes it negative.

It works because of what speech does to breathing. To talk, you have to let air out slowly and under control, and you can't do that while breathing hard. In 1981 two British physiologists had six people run at five steady speeds and, in the fifth minute at each speed, read a 100-word passage aloud, which took about half a minute. At every speed their ventilation while reading fell to about 55% of what it had been, and when they stopped it overshot by 14% before returning to normal within 30 seconds. The authors concluded that the demands of speech take precedence over the demands of gas exchange [17].

A talking rider is therefore breathing about half as much as the effort calls for and making it up between sentences. Below the first threshold there is room for that. Above it the extra carbon dioxide has to be breathed out, the drive to breathe rises out of proportion to the work, and half a minute at half ventilation becomes uncomfortable. That is the likely reason comfortable speech ends near the threshold. It is an inference from the two sets of findings, and no study I have read shows the steps in order. Talking also has a cost: in 14 runners it lowered ventilation and oxygen uptake and raised blood lactate, while heart rate did not change [18].

Foster's group checked the talk test against the ventilatory threshold in 2004. Sixteen healthy volunteers did tests of rising intensity on a treadmill and on a cycle ergometer, once with their breathing gases measured and once with the talk test. The authors concluded that the equivocal stage was "almost exactly equivalent" to the threshold on both machines, and that negative stages were consistently above it [19]. The figures in the paper's summary are less tidy. On the bike the threshold came at 82% of peak heart rate and the equivocal stage at 87%. Neither that difference nor the smaller one on the treadmill was statistically significant. With 16 people, a gap of five percentage points, about 9 beats for someone with a maximum of 180, can fail to reach significance and still be real, as Chapter 4 explained.

A second study tested whether the two move together. The researchers lowered people's thresholds by having them give blood and raised them with training, and both times the last positive stage of the talk test shifted to match [20]. This is stronger evidence than a correlation, because the researchers moved one thing and watched the other follow. The summary gives no numbers for the size of the shifts. It does report that when the intensity was a little above the threshold, speech took more than two minutes on average to become uncomfortable. It also reports that the errors leaned one way: people tended to pass the test when they were in fact above the threshold.

Trained cyclists have been tested twice, and the two papers recommend different stages. In 18 highly trained cyclists the equivocal stage matched VT1, at 148 beats per minute (bpm) against 150, and the negative stage matched the second ventilatory threshold, at 176 bpm against 177 [21]. In 12 highly trained cyclists in another laboratory the last positive stage came at 129 bpm, far below the threshold, the equivocal stage at 147 and the negative stage at 156, and the authors concluded that the negative stage was the one that estimated the threshold [22]. The equivocal stages of the two studies agree almost to the beat. The likeliest reason for the different advice is the question asked. The first study called a stage negative when the rider "could definitely not talk". The second called it negative when the rider answered "no" to whether he could speak comfortably, which happens much sooner. The talk test's answer depends on the wording of its question, and a rider using it should choose the words once and keep them.

The length of the stages matters too. Earlier talk-test studies had used stages of two minutes, and speech takes longer than that to become uncomfortable just above the threshold [20]. A short stage can end before the rider has found out. When 16 active people ran for 30 minutes at the pace of each stage found in a test of rising speed, all of them completed the half hour at the last positive stage, three could not complete it at the equivocal stage, and only two completed it at the negative stage [16]. An intensity just under the first threshold can be held for hours. For some of those runners the uncertain stage had evidently been well above it.

Taken across these studies, the equivocal stage is the best single estimate of the breathing threshold. It tends to run a few beats high, more so with short stages, and the last clearly comfortable stage lies on the safe side of it.

All of them took breathing as the reference. Only one reports lactate: in the 18 trained cyclists, blood lactate at the uncertain stage was 1.3 mmol/L, against 1.6 at VT1, which places that stage at or just before the first rise [21]. For a book that defines Zone 2 by lactate, the talk test is a proxy for a proxy, and how far it can be from one rider's lactate threshold has not been measured. It remains the best-supported method that costs nothing. Most of the evidence comes from one research group. B

Heart rate variability

The newest home method needs only a chest strap. A heart at rest does not beat like a metronome. The gaps between beats vary, and the variation has structure: long and short gaps follow one another in patterns that repeat across time scales. As exercise gets harder the structure dissolves and the variation becomes random. An index called DFA alpha 1, from a technique named detrended fluctuation analysis, puts a number on this. It sits around 1.0 in very light exercise and falls towards 0.5, the value for pure noise, at high intensity [23].

In 2021 a group led by Bruce Rogers proposed that the midpoint, 0.75, marks the first threshold. The value was chosen on theory, as halfway between order and noise. They tested it in 15 men running on a treadmill and it held: VT1 came at 152 bpm on average and the heartbeat threshold at 154, with a correlation of 0.97 [23]. Free phone apps now calculate the index live from a chest strap.

That correlation deserves the suspicion Chapter 5 recommended. The 15 men were aged from 19 to 52, and their heart rate at the threshold ran from 108 to 183 bpm. Across a spread that wide, almost any two measures that rise with effort will correlate. The limits of agreement are the better guide, and they ran from 12 beats one way to 8 the other [23]. Twenty beats from end to end is about the width of a heart-rate zone.

One study, up close: a chest strap against the laboratory

In 2026 a group at KU Leuven in Belgium tested the method on a bike, against lactate as well as breathing [14]. They recruited 24 active men and analysed 21, whose average age was 23, who did about 10 hours of sport a week, and whose VO2peak averaged 56 mL/kg/min. Each rode one test to exhaustion, starting at 40 W and rising by 30 W every four minutes. A mask measured breathing gases, a drop of blood was taken from the earlobe after each stage, and a chest strap recorded every heartbeat. The people who read the thresholds worked blind to the other results.

The first lactate threshold, defined as this book defines it, came at 153 bpm and 180 W on average. The 0.75 heartbeat threshold came at 161 bpm and 201 W: 8 beats and 21 W too high, a difference that was not statistically significant. The scatter was the problem. For individual riders the limits of agreement ran from 36 beats too high to 21 too low, and the correlation between the two heart rates was 0.07. A personalised cut-off, set from each rider's own starting value as Rogers's group had since proposed [24], matched the lactate threshold exactly on average and still had limits of about 30 beats either side [14].

Against the breathing threshold the method did worse. VT1 came at 144 bpm and 156 W, so the 0.75 cut-off was 17 beats and 45 W too high, and that difference was significant. The authors concluded that the method was not reliable enough for finding the first threshold in a test of this kind. A pooled analysis of 22 earlier studies had found the same lean, with the heartbeat threshold agreeing better with lactate than with breathing [25].

Those figures also show the laboratory's two measures of the first threshold disagreeing with each other. Lactate put it at 153 bpm and breathing at 144, in the same riders during the same test. For individuals the limits of agreement between the two ran from 30 beats one way to 11 the other, and the correlation between them was 0.51 [14].

The authors list the study's limits [14]. All 21 riders were young men. Each did one test, so nobody knows whether a rider's heartbeat threshold would come out the same the following week. Four-minute stages suit lactate sampling and may blur the ventilatory threshold, which is easier to see in a smooth ramp. The work was funded by the university and the authors declared no conflicts of interest. For a rider it settles one point. On average the heartbeat method lands near the lactate threshold, and for any one rider it can be 30 beats out, so a live reading on a phone should not set the top of Zone 2 unchecked. B

The 2024 pooled analysis looks more encouraging in its summary. Across 22 studies the average difference between the heartbeat threshold and the laboratory one was trivial, and the pooled correlation was 0.85. The body of the paper reports that about 90% of the variation between those studies reflected real differences in what they found, and that a new study could expect a correlation anywhere from 0.06 to 0.99 [25]. The trivial average comes from studies that erred in both directions.

Reading the research: validated against what?

A validation study compares a cheap method with a reference and reports the gap. The gap belongs to both of them. If the reference is itself uncertain, part of the error charged to the cheap method was never its fault, and no cheap method can be shown to be more accurate than the reference is.

The Leuven study lets a reader see this because it measured two references. Against breathing, the heartbeat threshold was 17 beats out and the difference was statistically significant. Against lactate it was 8 beats out and the difference was not. Had the group collected only breathing, the paper would have reported a method that fails. Had they collected only lactate, it could fairly have reported one that agrees with the laboratory on average. Studies in this field differ in which reference they use, and the pooled analysis found that the choice changes the result [25].

So when a paper says a home method agrees with the laboratory, find out which laboratory measure was the reference: lactate or breathing, and by which of Chapter 5's definitions. The talk-test studies above all used breathing, and this book's definition uses lactate. Find out how repeatable the reference is. If the same rider were tested again, or the same test read by another physiologist, the answer would move: by a standard deviation of 19 W in the retest of 14 cyclists, and by 16% between the four physiologists of 1983 [13,7]. And check whether the people who read the reference were kept blind to the home method's result, as they were in Leuven. A threshold found by eye is easy to nudge towards a number the reader already knows.

A lactate threshold from a single test is uncertain by 10 or 20 W, and lactate and breathing were about 10 beats apart on average in Leuven [13,14]. A home method that lands within 10 beats of the lactate threshold has done about as well as the laboratory does against itself.

Percentages, drift and feel

Heart-rate percentages are the commonest stand-in and the weakest, because they stack two guesses. The first is the maximum. The formula 220 minus age was never derived from a study: two physiologists who went looking for its source in 2000 traced it to a remark in the caption of a figure in a 1971 review [26,27]. The best modern equation, fitted to 3,320 healthy Norwegian adults, has a standard error of 10.8 bpm, which puts about one adult in three more than 11 beats from the prediction [28]. The second guess is the percentage. In 100 adults with a measured maximum, the first lactate threshold lay anywhere from 60% to 90% of it [29]. The rule printed on gym machines and built into many apps puts Zone 2 at 60–70% of maximum. In the 50 trained cyclists of Chapter 5 the first threshold averaged 81% [9], so for the average rider among them the top of that band was about 20 beats too low.

Percentages of VO2max do no better. When 21 fit men rode for an hour at 60% of their VO2max, their blood lactate averaged 2.1 mmol/L and ran individually from 0.7, a resting level, to 5.6 [30]. Phil Maffetone's 180 formula, which is 180 minus age with adjustments of 5 or 10 beats for health and training history, leaves the maximum out. Its author's page cites no study that compares it with a measured threshold [31], and I could not find one.

Coaches often recommend a drift test. Ride for an hour at a steady power and compare your average heart rate in the second half with the first. A rise of less than 5% is taken to mean the power was at or below the first threshold. The physiology under it is real: over a long steady effort heart rate creeps upward while the volume of blood pumped with each beat falls [32], and above the first threshold the oxygen cost creeps as well. I could find no study that tests the 5% rule against a measured lactate threshold. Heat, dehydration and the length of the ride all raise drift for reasons unconnected to the threshold, as Chapter 9 describes, so a failed test may only show that the room was warm. C

Breathing through the nose is a cue cyclists often pass on: if you can keep your mouth shut, you are in Zone 2. In 30 adults exercising at rising intensity with a mask over the nose, 20 changed from breathing through the nose alone to nose and mouth together at an average of 105 W. Four breathed through the mouth from the start and five never opened it, and what related most closely to each person's switch point was how hard breathing felt and how much work the nose demanded [33]. For nine of those 30 people the switch could not have marked a threshold of any kind.

In 2008 a group of exercise psychologists led by Panteleimon Ekkekakis had 30 young adults do 15-minute treadmill sessions below, at and above their own ventilatory threshold, and rate repeatedly how good or bad they felt. Above the threshold their pleasure fell, and it fell in much the same way across the group [34]. Zone 2 is, roughly, the hardest riding that still feels good. A later review found that the heart rates people settle at when asked to hold a given feeling vary too much for the feeling to find the top edge [35]. C

A step test for the turbo trainer

A smart trainer makes a step test easy to run properly. In ERG mode the trainer holds a set power whatever the cadence, so each stage is exactly the power it is meant to be. The protocol below takes lactate and the talk test together, and a rider without a meter can run the talk test alone. It is assembled from the studies above. Nobody has tested it as a package, and each choice is given with the finding it rests on.

  1. Pick a day when you are rested, and ride in a cool room with a fan. Heat and fatigue both raise heart rate at a given power.
  2. Warm up for 10 minutes at a very easy power. With a meter, take a first sample at the end.
  3. Start well below where you expect Zone 2 to be and ride four-minute stages, adding about 15 W each time. Four minutes, because lactate needs time to reach the blood [13,15] and because speech takes more than two minutes to become uncomfortable just above the threshold [20].
  4. In the last 40 seconds of each stage, note your heart rate, then say the same passage aloud: about 100 words that you know by heart, the length used in the 1981 breathing study [17]. Ask yourself the same question every time: could I speak comfortably? Answer yes, not sure, or no, and settle the wording before you start [22].
  5. With a meter, take a blood sample at the end of each stage, from clean dry skin, discarding the first drop.
  6. Your baseline is the average of the early stages, before the readings begin a steady climb. Expect those readings to differ by a tenth or two, and do not be surprised if the second is lower than the first. The first stage that reads 0.5 mmol/L or more above the baseline is over the threshold, provided the next stage reads higher still. A lone high reading is more likely a bad sample [10].
  7. The top of your Zone 2 is the heart rate and power of the stage before that rise. Without a meter, it is the last stage with a clear "yes", and the "not sure" stage is roughly the threshold [19,20].
  8. Stop one stage after the rise is confirmed, or at the first "no". The test goes nowhere near your maximum.
  9. Repeat it on another day. The lactate threshold moved by a standard deviation of 19 W between two tests in trained cyclists [13], and heart rate at a fixed effort varies by several beats from day to day [36].

A rider who does both gets a check on each. If the last comfortable stage and the last stage before the lactate rise are the same or adjacent, the result can be trusted further than either alone. If they are three stages apart, something went wrong with one of them.

Anyone with a heart condition, or on medication that alters heart rate such as beta blockers, should ask a doctor before testing or training by heart rate. The talk test asks for no maximal effort, and it has been used with cardiac patients [37].

The second threshold

Zone 2 needs only the first threshold. The hard sessions of Part III need the second, and lactate is a harder way to find it: the maximal lactate steady state takes four or five 30-minute rides on separate days, as Chapter 5 described. The practical routes are tests of performance.

Chapter 5 covered the 20-minute test: FTP matched the laboratory steady state on average in one study, overshot it in another, and can be 9% out for one rider [38,39]. Critical power can also be found without a laboratory. In one validation study, all-out efforts of 12, 7 and 3 minutes in a single session, with 30 minutes between them, gave a critical power with a standard error of 4.5% against the laboratory figure and limits of agreement of −26 W to +29 W. The best 3-, 7- and 12-minute powers picked out of the riders' ordinary training and racing files did nearly as well, at 5.2% and −34 W to +44 W [40]. The talk test reaches the second threshold too. In the 18 trained cyclists, the stage at which they could definitely not talk matched the second ventilatory threshold, and their blood lactate there was 4.2 mmol/L [21]. The heartbeat index also did better here than at the first threshold: in Leuven its 0.5 value came within 1 beat and 2 W of the second lactate threshold on average, with limits of 17 beats and 54 W either side [14].

A once-popular home test for the second threshold has not lasted. In 1982 Francesco Conconi and colleagues had 210 runners circle a track at steadily rising speed. Heart rate climbed in a straight line with speed and then, near the top, bent away from the line. The paper reported the bend in every runner, and in the ten whose blood lactate was also measured it coincided with the lactate threshold [41]. Fifteen years later Andrew Jones and Jonathan Doust repeated the test with 14 trained runners on a treadmill. The bend appeared in nine of them, at about 95% of maximum heart rate and 14 beats above the lactate turnpoint, and runners asked to hold a pace just below it lasted 16 minutes on average [42]. The original link to lactate had rested on ten runners out of 210.

A rider who wants percentages as a cross-check should measure a maximum heart rate on the bike and not predict one. A properly measured maximum is accurate to about two beats [26]. Older riders, untrained riders and anyone with a heart condition should not go looking for one without medical advice.

Keeping the numbers right

How often to retest depends on which number. In 2000 Alejandro Lucía and colleagues tested 13 professional road cyclists three times in a season: in November during their break, in January, and in May when they were racing. The power at their thresholds rose as they got fitter. The heart rates hardly moved. At the lactate threshold they averaged 154, 152 and 154 bpm across the three tests, and at the second ventilatory threshold 178, 173 and 176 [43]. The authors concluded that one test at the start of the season is enough to set a professional's heart-rate targets for the year.

Those were professionals with many years of training behind them, and nobody has shown the same for a rider in a first season, whose heart may be changing faster. For an established rider the finding suggests that the heart rate at the lactate threshold, once found, should last, and that the power that goes with it is what needs rechecking. Every couple of months, and after illness or a long break, is a reasonable interval, though no study sets one.

Within that, heart rate wobbles from day to day. Forty-four people who did the same submaximal running test at the same time on five consecutive days showed a spread of about 5 bpm at the stage where they were most consistent, near 90% of maximum, and more at the easier stages [36]. It also moves within a ride. In the Auckland study that Chapter 9 describes, two hours of easy riding raised the heart rate at the first threshold by 9 bpm and lowered the power by 21 W [44]. A ceiling written as a single number of beats claims more precision than any of these measurements has.

A lactate meter stays at home, and the numbers it gave you are what you ride to. Of the stand-ins, the talk test is the one that goes out on the road. If you are riding at your Zone 2 heart rate and can't finish a sentence comfortably, the number is wrong for that day.

Chapter 7: What easy riding does to the body

Draft · 5,866 words · 14 October 2026

Easy riding is riding below the first lactate threshold, and for most cyclists it is most of what they do. Since about 2019 a great deal has been claimed for it: that it is the best way to build mitochondria, that it teaches the body to burn fat, that it trains the clearing of lactate, and that its upper edge, Zone 2, is an intensity with effects no other has. Those claims can be checked against training studies, and most of them do not hold. What the evidence supports is plainer. Easy riding is a small stimulus to the muscle, smaller per minute than any harder riding, and it costs so little that it can be repeated in very large amounts. What it does for a rider, it does through the amount.

That matters because the amount is the thing riders differ in. A professional can train for most of the day, and an amateur with a job and a family may have a handful of hours a week. An account of easy riding that is true for one of them can be poor advice for the other.

What an easy ride asks of the muscle

A muscle is a bundle of fibres, each a single long cell, and the fibres are not all alike. What sets them apart is the kind of myosin they build, the protein that produces force. Human limb muscle makes three kinds, which gives three named fibre types: type I, called slow-twitch, and two fast-twitch types, IIA and IIX [1]. Slow fibres contract more slowly, are rich in mitochondria and are slow to tire. Fast fibres produce force quickly and tire sooner.

The types differ in colour, because slow fibres hold more myoglobin, the red protein that stores oxygen in muscle. A chicken shows it at the table. The legs, which carry the bird all day, are dark meat, and the breast, which powers a few seconds of flapping, is white. Human muscles are not sorted that way. Slow and fast fibres lie mixed together through the same muscle, in an arrangement that looks random, and a thin slice has to be stained before the types can be told apart under a microscope [2,3]. A fibre takes its type from the nerve cell that drives it [4], and each nerve cell drives many fibres of one type, scattered among the fibres of its neighbours. Where a slice shows large groups of a single type, pathologists read it as a sign of disease in the nerves that supply the muscle [2].

The mix varies widely from person to person. Chapter 1 described 19 trained cyclists whose thigh muscle ran from 32% slow fibres to 76% [5]. Among white North American adults, about one in four has either fewer than 35% or more than 65% slow fibres in that muscle. Jean-Aimé Simoneau and Claude Bouchard estimated in 1995 that about 45% of the variation between people is inherited, about 40% comes from the muscle's surroundings and how much it is used, and the remaining 15% is error in sampling and measurement [6]. Endurance athletes tend to have more slow fibres and sprinters more fast ones [7].

How far training can change the mix is not settled. Training studies lasting a few months have mostly shown shifts between the two fast types, and their findings on shifts between slow and fast have not agreed [3]. The most striking evidence is a single pair of identical twins tested at the age of 52. One had done endurance training for more than 30 years and the other had not, and the trained twin's thigh muscle was 95% slow fibres, far more than his brother's [8,3]. One pair of twins cannot say how typical that is. C A fibre's type is also a separate matter from its contents. The mitochondria inside it, which are the subject of this chapter, increase with training without the type having to change [3].

The three names suggest three tidy bins, and single fibres do not fall into them so neatly. Many are hybrids, carrying two kinds of myosin at once. In 2025 a group working in Ghent and Copenhagen took about a thousand single fibres from the thigh muscle of healthy adults, nearly all of them men, and measured the proteins in each, and did the same for the genes switched on in another thousand. About a third were pure slow fibres and a little over a third pure IIA, 16–19% were hybrids of the two fast kinds, and at the level of protein not one was pure IIX. Slow and fast fibres did form two separate groups, joined by a few hybrids. Within each group the fibres varied continuously, and the fibres of one person differed more from each other than one person's did from another's [1]. Slow and fast are real families, and within each there is a spectrum.

The nervous system calls on these fibres in a fixed order. Slow fibres are used first, and fast fibres are added as the force required goes up, or as the slow fibres tire. The classic demonstration was published in 1974. Philip Gollnick, Karin Piehl and Bengt Saltin had people ride at intensities from 30% of VO2max upward, took samples of thigh muscle, and stained them for glycogen, the muscle's store of carbohydrate. A fibre that has been working uses up its glycogen and stains pale. At every intensity below VO2max the slow fibres emptied first, and the fast fibres began to empty only as the ride went on. After three hours at low intensity a large amount of glycogen was still in the muscle, nearly all of it in fast fibres [9]. At 75% of VO2max, by contrast, a later study found the slow fibres and the IIA fibres working together from the first minute [10].

Chapter 2 ended on the rule that a fibre adapts only if it was disturbed. An easy ride disturbs the slow fibres. It reaches the fast ones only if it goes on long enough for the slow ones to run down.

The disturbance is also small. Below the first threshold the cell's chemistry settles into a steady state, so the energy sensor AMPK, which responds to a shortfall, has little to respond to. Chapter 2 described the Dublin experiment in which a ride at 40% of VO2max did not activate AMPK and raised the messenger RNA for PGC-1α less than half as far as a ride at 80% [11]. In 2025 four Canadian physiologists collected the measurements of this kind that they could find for exercise at Zone 2 intensity, in a review they called Much Ado About Zone 2, and concluded that the signals are small, inconsistent, or both [12].

Duration seems to help, up to a point. In the studies that review lists, AMPK activity was unchanged after two hours of easy exercise and raised at three and a half. The message for PGC-1α rose after 60 and 90 minutes in one study and not after 30, and did not rise after 90 in another. Two to three and a half hours of easy exercise also lowers muscle glycogen, and low glycogen is itself a signal to AMPK [12].

How intensity and duration combine was mapped in rats in 1982. Gary Dudley and colleagues trained groups of rats on treadmills for eight weeks on 19 different schedules, covering six running speeds and several daily durations at each, and measured a mitochondrial protein, cytochrome c, in each type of muscle fibre. At any one speed, running for longer each day produced more adaptation up to a ceiling and then no more. The height of the ceiling was set by the speed. The faster the running, the higher the ceiling and the shorter the daily run needed to reach it. The least-used fast fibres did not adapt at all until the speed reached about 80% of VO2max, and above that their response rose steeply [13].

Those were rats, and their minutes and speeds do not translate to a cyclist. The shape is what matters, and the human evidence below is consistent with it. More easy riding adds adaptation at a diminishing rate, the rate depends on how easy it is, and some fibres are barely touched by it.

What it builds

One study, up close: 353 training studies

The largest test of what continuous riding builds over a training programme was published in 2025 by Knut Sindre Mølmen, Nicki Winfield Almquist and Øyvind Skattebo, working in Norway [14]. It is the review the Introduction quoted.

They searched three databases for every study that had trained people and measured their muscle before and after. Of 5,224 records, 425 papers met their criteria. The analysis of mitochondria used 353 studies, containing 506 separate training groups and 5,650 people. For each group they recorded how long, how often and how hard the training was, and how fit the people were at the start. The outcome was the percentage change in the muscle's mitochondrial content, pooled from five markers: the volume of mitochondria seen under an electron microscope, and four mitochondrial enzymes.

Each group's training was put in one of three classes. Sprint training meant all-out efforts of 4 to 90 seconds. High-intensity training meant work above the second threshold. Everything continuous below the second threshold went into a single class that the authors call endurance training, at low or moderate intensity [14].

All three raised mitochondrial content, and by similar amounts: 23% for continuous training, 27% for high-intensity training and 27% for sprints, differences that were not statistically significant. Gains were visible after two weeks. Training six times a week produced more than four, and four more than two. Age, sex and disease made no difference to the response [14].

Per hour of training the three were far apart. High-intensity training raised mitochondrial content about 1.7 times as much per hour as continuous training, and sprints about 3.9 times as much. The same held for VO2max, where sprints were about 4.9 times as efficient per hour as continuous training [14].

Capillaries told a slightly different story. The number of capillaries around each fibre rose by 15% with continuous training, 13% with high-intensity training and 10% with sprints, with no significant difference. Capillary density, the number per square millimetre of muscle, rose more with continuous training (13% against 7%), which the authors put down to the fibres themselves growing less. Most of the gain had arrived within four weeks [14].

The finding that bears most on a trained cyclist is about starting fitness. The authors sorted the groups into untrained (an average VO2max of 35 mL/kg/min), moderately trained (49) and well trained (62). The untrained gained most. Well-trained people gained about 7% in mitochondrial content, averaged over every kind of training. Expressed per hour, well-trained people showed no measurable gain from continuous training or from high-intensity training, and a gain only from sprints. Their capillaries did not change significantly with any training, and nor did their VO2max per hour [14].

What the analysis says about easy riding in particular is limited, first of all by its own categories. The continuous class runs all the way up to the second threshold, so it mixes easy riding with riding between the thresholds. The 23% belongs to that mixture. The analysis cannot say how much of it easy riding alone would produce, and the Canadian review makes the same objection [12].

The studies are short and small in dose. The average programme of continuous training lasted 11 weeks in untrained people and seven in the others, and in untrained and moderately trained people it added up to about 36 hours of training in total. A cyclist riding eight hours a week does that in a month. The authors say their findings describe mainly the first response to a change in training [14].

And the well trained are barely represented. Of 270 groups doing continuous training, 14 were well trained, with 104 people between them. Nearly two-thirds of all the measurements came from untrained people, 36% of whose groups had a disease, and three-quarters or more of the participants in every class were men [14]. The authors did not test for publication bias, which they acknowledge. They declared no funding and no conflicts of interest.

What the analysis establishes is that continuous riding below the second threshold builds mitochondria and capillaries in people who are not yet well trained, about as much per programme as harder training and much less per hour. A For riders who are already well trained it found no measurable gain from more of it, on thin evidence. B

Studies that kept the riding easy

A handful of studies trained people at an intensity that was clearly easy and measured the result. Each is small, and they point the same way.

Four weeks is not enough at low volume. In a 2016 study from David Bishop's laboratory, 29 men did 12 sessions over four weeks of sprints, of four-minute intervals, or of continuous riding below the lactate threshold for 20 to 36 minutes. Citrate synthase, the usual marker of mitochondrial content, did not change in any group, and the mitochondria's capacity to use oxygen rose only after sprints [15].

At equal work, easy training does not raise the ceiling. A Norwegian trial assigned 40 moderately trained men to four ways of running, three days a week for eight weeks, with the total oxygen consumed matched between groups: slow running at 70% of maximum heart rate, steady running at 85%, and two kinds of interval. VO2max rose by 5.5% and 7.2% in the interval groups, significantly more than in the other two, and the heart's stroke volume rose by about 10% after intervals [16]. The summary of the paper gives no figures for the slow group.

With enough hours, easy training does improve some things as well as hard training does. In a Finnish trial that Chapter 9 describes, 35 sedentary or recreationally active adults did ten weeks of either easy riding, averaging 6.8 hours a week, or intervals, averaging 1.6 hours. Durability, the ability to hold a steady effort for three hours without drifting, improved by about the same amount in both groups. VO2max rose only in the interval group [17]. The easy group needed four times the hours to match the interval group on one measure and did not match it on the other.

Over a year, in people starting from nothing, easy training does change how fuel is used. Seventeen sedentary adults jogged or walked for 45 minutes three times a week for 12 months at a moderate, fixed heart rate. Their peak rate of fat burning rose, and it came at a higher intensity, moving from 35% of VO2max to 50%. Their weight did not change and their body fat fell by 3.4 percentage points [18]. The summary mentions no control group.

In athletes who are already elite, easy training holds what they have. Twenty elite junior cross-country skiers trained 12 to 15 hours a week for five months, one group mostly at 60–70% of VO2max and the other mostly at 80–90%, close to the lactate threshold. The harder group improved more in a 20-minute run and raised their speed at the lactate threshold by 3.2%. The easier group's threshold speed did not change, and neither group's citrate synthase did [19,20].

The two studies furthest from ordinary training are ski expeditions. In 1983 six people skied 30 kilometres a day with backpacks, six days a week for eight weeks, at about 45% of VO2max. In their arms, which were doing work they were not used to, citrate synthase rose by 90% and the number of capillaries per fibre by 40%. In their thighs nothing changed. The authors concluded that long duration is not, by itself, enough to make the leg muscles adapt [21]. In 2015 nine people were tested before and after 42 days of skiing at 60% of maximum heart rate. Their peak oxygen uptake was unchanged. The capacity of the mitochondria in their thighs to use oxygen had fallen by about a fifth, and citrate synthase by 15% [22].

The likely explanation is that the skiers' legs were already trained for work at walking pace, so many hours a day of it gave them nothing new to adapt to, while their arms had never worked so hard and for them the same expedition was a training programme. What counts as easy is relative to what the muscle is already used to. This is the point the Canadian review presses hardest. It cites an earlier analysis by Bishop's group suggesting that exercise below about 60% of a person's maximum work rate is not expected to raise mitochondrial content at all [12,23], and an older pooled analysis of 34 studies found that sedentary people raised their thresholds at any training intensity, while people who were already trained showed gains that were not statistically significant and tended to respond better to high intensity [24].

Capillaries follow the same pattern. A 2022 analysis of 57 training groups found that in sedentary people, capillaries per fibre grew 21% more after moderate continuous training than after low-intensity training, and 54% more after high-intensity intervals. In people who were already trained, no kind of training added any [25].

I know of no study that adds easy hours to a trained cyclist's week and measures the muscle, or that compares riding at the top of Zone 2 with riding lower at the same hours. The evidence that exists says that easy training builds a great deal in the untrained, and that in the trained, at the volumes and durations anyone has studied, it maintains more than it builds. B

Three claims

That Zone 2 is the best way to build mitochondria

It is not. Per hour, continuous riding builds less mitochondrial content than harder riding, and the continuous riding in that comparison was harder than Zone 2 on average [14]. The signals from a single easy session are small [12]. In the one four-week study that isolated riding below the threshold, mitochondrial content did not move [15]. The claim described in the Introduction, that easy riding is the single most effective way to build mitochondria, has no trial behind it.

The opposite claim, that easy riding does nothing for mitochondria, is also wrong. Continuous training raised mitochondrial content by nearly a quarter across 270 training groups [14], and enough of it, in a muscle that is not yet used to it, clearly works. The Canadian review's conclusion is carefully limited: Zone 2 has not been shown to be the optimal intensity, and people with little time should put harder riding first. It is about the general population, and says of athletes that the question is open [12].

That Zone 2 is where you burn the most fat

From the saddle. I ride at the top of where I think my Zone 2 is, partly because I think that is the "fat max". Managing my weight is definitely a goal, so that's why I do the top of Zone 2. But is that really fat max? Could I protect my hard rides by riding even easier?

Fat burning rises with intensity to a peak and then falls as carbohydrate takes over. Where the peak sits depends on what you have eaten. In 33 trained endurance athletes tested in Asker Jeukendrup's laboratory, the intensity of peak fat burning and the intensity of the first rise in lactate were the same, 63% and 61% of VO2max [26]. That laboratory's practice was to test after an overnight fast, though the paper's summary does not say so of this study. When it gave 11 trained people 75 g of glucose 45 minutes before the same test, the peak rate of fat burning fell by 28% and the intensity at which it occurred fell from 60% of VO2max to 52% [27]. In the 50 cyclists of Chapter 5, who had been told to eat a carbohydrate meal three hours beforehand, fat burning peaked at 56% of VO2max and the first threshold came at 70%. That is a gap of 75 W and 16 beats per minute [28].

So the belief is right for a fasted rider and wrong for a fed one. That the difference between the studies is the feeding is my inference: their test protocols differed as well. The peak is also broad and hard to pin down. In moderately trained cyclists, fat burning stayed within 10% of its peak from 55% to 72% of VO2max [29], and when 15 men were tested twice, each man's peak moved by 16–21% between the two tests [30].

For body fat, where the peak sits matters much less than it seems. Sixteen adults each spent three days in a sealed room that measured everything they burned. On one day they rested, on one they did 400 kcal of exercise at 40% of VO2max, and on one they did 400 kcal at 70%. Over the full 24 hours, the fat they burned did not differ between the three days [31]. A review by the same group concluded that exercise of an hour or less has little effect on fat burned over a day [32]. The likely reason is that the body settles its fuel account over the day, so that a ride which burned mostly fat is followed by hours that burn a little less. B

Training trials agree. A pooled analysis of 31 studies found no difference in body fat between people assigned to intervals and people assigned to steady moderate training, and neither produced a loss the authors considered clinically meaningful [33]. Training at fat max does reduce weight in people with obesity, by about 4 kg across 11 trials of 8 to 20 weeks [34], though the summary of that analysis does not say what the comparison groups did.

What differs between the top of Zone 2 and the bottom is the energy. On the averages of the 50 cyclists, the first threshold is 75 W above the fed-state fat max [28]. An hour at the higher power is 270 kilojoules more work, which is roughly 270 kcal more burned, against a difference in fat burned during the ride of probably a few grams. A rider who wants to lose weight has a reason to ride at the top of Zone 2, and the reason is the energy.

That Zone 2 trains the clearing of lactate

The idea comes from the 2018 paper described in Chapter 6, in which professional endurance athletes carried less lactate and burned more fat than moderately active people or patients with metabolic syndrome [35]. Its reasoning is sound as far as it goes. Mitochondria are where both fat and lactate are burned. A muscle with more mitochondrial capacity can meet a given power while burning more fat and letting less lactate build up, which is the explanation Chapter 2 gave for why trained riders have higher thresholds. So the lactate and fat-burning curves from an exercise test are a window on how much mitochondrial capacity a person has, and the authors' name for a body that switches easily between fuels is metabolic flexibility.

The step from there to a training prescription is the weak one. The paper compared three groups of different people at one moment. It did not train anybody. Professionals differ from patients in years of training of every kind, in how much of it they do, and in whatever made them professionals in the first place, and a comparison of that sort cannot say which of those produced the difference. It is a description of what a very fit body looks like, and it is used as if it were evidence about how to build one.

The one training study I found that measured the machinery directly points the other way. In the elite skiers described above, the researchers also measured MCT1, the transporter that carries lactate into muscle fibres to be burned. After five months of mostly easy training it had fallen by 12%. In the group that trained near the lactate threshold it was unchanged, and theirs was the group whose threshold speed improved [20]. That is 20 skiers, and the summary does not say how they had trained before, so the fall may reflect a step down from harder training. It does not support the idea that easy riding is what builds lactate clearance. C

Reading the research: who chose the studies?

Two reviews run through this chapter, and they sound as if they disagree. One says that continuous training builds as much mitochondrial content as intervals. The other says Zone 2 is not the best way to build mitochondria. They rest on much the same body of studies.

They are different kinds of document. The Norwegian analysis is a systematic review. Its authors wrote down in advance which databases they would search and which studies would qualify, screened 5,224 records, and report how many they excluded and why [14]. Another team following the same rules should arrive at nearly the same set of studies. The Canadian paper is a narrative review, and says so: its authors did not run a systematic search, and drew on reference lists, on papers they knew, and on articles shared through social media [12]. A narrative review is an argument made by experts. It can be right, and this one mostly is, but the reader has no way to know which studies were left out.

It also helps to ask what question each was answering. The Norwegian authors asked how much each kind of training changes muscle, per programme and per hour. The Canadian authors asked whether Zone 2 is the optimal intensity for people who are inactive or doing about two and a half hours of exercise a week. "Not shown to be optimal" is a much weaker statement than "shown not to work", and the paper is often quoted as if it had made the second.

And look at who is writing. One of the Canadian authors declares an advisory role and shares in a longevity company whose services relate in part to exercise, and has spent much of his career showing what short hard sessions can do [12]. That does not make the review wrong. The Norwegian authors declare no interests, did not test for publication bias, and put easy and moderate riding in one category, which happens to be the choice that decides what their headline number means for Zone 2. Both papers need reading past the summary.

What easy riding is good for

If easy riding is a weaker stimulus per hour than any other kind, the case for it has to rest on something else, and it does. It rests on cost.

A comparison per hour assumes that time is what a rider is short of. For some riders it is. For others the scarce thing is the capacity to recover, and measured against that, the ranking of the three kinds of training reverses.

Chapter 6 described the evidence. Riders 20 W below their first threshold kept going for three and a half hours [36], and trained runners had recovered within 5 to 10 minutes of an easy session whether it lasted one hour or two, against 30 minutes after anything harder [37]. There is a limit to how many hard sessions a rider can absorb in a week, which Chapter 13 examines. Beyond that limit, every further hour of training has to be easy or it will not get done.

So easy riding is how a rider with time turns that time into training. The Norwegian authors found that training load, the product of volume and intensity, was the best predictor of the gain in mitochondrial content [14], and easy riding is the only way to raise the volume term very far. This is why the athletes of Chapter 8 do so much of it. It does not follow that a rider with four hours a week should spend them the same way.

Easy riding may also build things that short studies cannot see. Chapter 2 gave the timescales: capillaries and the structure of the heart change over months and years, and nearly every trial in this chapter lasted weeks. Chapter 9 describes durability, the ability to hold power late in a long ride, which in one ten-week trial improved as much on nearly seven hours a week of easy riding as on about an hour and a half of intervals, although only the intervals raised VO2max [17]. The argument that years of easy volume build an engine that intervals alone cannot rests on what elite athletes do and on mechanism. No trial has run long enough to test it. C

How easy, and how long

This book's rule for an easy ride is that it should carry as much work as it can without taking anything from the hard sessions. For a rider on six to ten hours a week that means the top of the easy range, Zone 2. At equal hours it is more work, more energy burned and a larger disturbance to more of the muscle than riding lower, and the ski expeditions show what can happen when the intensity is too low for a muscle that is already trained. No trial has compared the top of the range with the bottom. The best evidence against the rule is that Olympic champion skiers do most of their easy training well below Zone 2. Chapter 8 gives their figures, and my reason for thinking they follow the same rule with different numbers: they fit about 15 easy hours a week around their hard sessions [38], where a seven-hour rider has five or six and a day or more between hard sessions. C

If the ride is truly below the first threshold, Seiler's runners suggest that riding easier still buys very little extra recovery [37]. That is nine runners and one marker of recovery, and I would not lean on it for more than the direction.

The risk at the top is of being over the threshold without knowing. Chapter 6 put the uncertainty of a ceiling found without a lactate test at about 10 beats [39], and the threshold itself falls during a long ride [40]. A margin of a few beats under the estimated ceiling costs little and covers both.

The better way to protect a hard session is to arrive at it fuelled. Riding near the top of Zone 2 uses more carbohydrate than riding lower: in the 1974 study, glycogen was used 2.7 times as fast at 64% of VO2max as at 31% [9]. Chapter 14 covers how to replace it. That last step is my inference. No study here tested whether eating more or riding easier does more for the next day's intervals.

On duration the evidence is thinner. The signalling studies suggest that an easy ride has to last well over an hour before the sensors register much [12], the glycogen staining suggests that fast fibres join in only late in a long ride [9], and the rat study shows a ceiling beyond which more minutes add nothing [13]. None of that tells a cyclist whether one three-hour ride is worth more than three of an hour. It does suggest that a week of 45-minute easy rides is the form of easy riding with the least to recommend it on mechanism, and I say that as someone who spent a winter doing exactly that and got faster.

From the saddle. Before that winter I'd been doing mainly high-intensity sessions, mostly racing online on Zwift, and rather inconsistently. At the weekends I rode with friends, and those rides were generally high intensity too. It came to maybe three or four hours a week. That winter my hours went up to about six or seven a week. I generally felt better, and I fell ill less often with coughs and colds. George: the Introduction's four or five rides of 45 minutes to an hour come to three to five hours. What made up the rest of the six or seven: weekend rides, and were they hard?

The intensity, then, was not the only thing that changed that winter. The hours roughly doubled, from three or four a week to six or seven, and the riding became regular, where before it had been hard sessions done inconsistently. Four or five sessions a week is the frequency at which the Norwegian analysis found the gains growing [14]. Per hour, the evidence in this chapter favours harder riding over easy, so the extra hours and the regularity are likelier causes of the improvement than the lower intensity. That is an inference from one rider and one climb. It is also this chapter's argument in a single case. Easy riding was something I could do twice as much of, week after week, and feel better for. It does not show that easy riding was the best use of those six or seven hours.

Who it is for

A new or returning rider gains from easy riding in almost any form, because a muscle that is not used to training adapts to nearly anything, and most of the 23% in the Norwegian analysis was measured in people like that [14].

A trained rider with only a few hours a week gets the least from it. The muscle is already used to the stimulus, the hours are too few for volume to do its work, and per hour every harder kind of riding does more. For that rider easy riding fills the days between the hard sessions, and the hard sessions are where most of the training is.

A trained rider with many hours is the one it is for. Once the week holds as many hard sessions as the rider can absorb, every further hour has to be easy, those hours cost little, and they are the only way left to raise the training load. Whether they go on building the muscle and the heart for years, as the practice of elite athletes suggests, is the next chapter's question, and no trial has run long enough to answer it.

For anyone riding to manage their weight, the hours and the power matter and the fuel mix does not.

That is my reading of studies that were mostly not designed to sort riders this way, and the chapters of Part III test the second of those statements against the trials that compared easy riding with intervals directly. C
