Chapter 8: Why elite athletes ride easy

Draft · 5,384 words · 14 October 2026

Eleven Norwegian cross-country skiers and biathletes, each the winner of at least one individual Olympic or world championship gold medal, kept a diary of every training session in the year that led to the best result of their careers. Researchers at the Norwegian Olympic Federation later added the diaries up. The athletes had trained for 770 hours each on average, in 470 sessions, which is about 15 hours a week across the whole year. Of the time they spent on endurance training, 91% was below the first lactate threshold [1].

Records like these are where the advice to train mostly easy comes from, and they are usually read as a recipe: four easy sessions to one hard one, or 80/20. I think that reading takes the wrong number from the diaries. In every phase of the year the same athletes logged about 1.3 hours a week above the first threshold, spread over two or three sessions, and that figure hardly moved while their easy training rose to 15 hours a week in summer and fell to 10 in the racing season [1]. The hard training looks like a fixed dose, of a size within an amateur's reach. The easy training is whatever a full-time athlete has the hours and the recovery to add on top. If that is right, the ratio is a consequence of how much the champions train, and a rider with seven hours a week who copies it has copied the wrong thing.

That is an inference from diaries, and the evidence carries it only part of the way.

What the records show

Stephen Seiler, whose 2006 study of junior skiers opened Chapter 5, summarised the descriptive studies in 2010. Athletes of national and international standard who trained 10 to 13 times a week converged, he wrote, on about 80% of sessions at low intensity and about 20% built around hard work such as intervals. They appeared to "self-organize" towards a high volume of training with hard sessions applied carefully [2]. Chapter 5 explained that the 80 is a count of sessions. Counted in minutes the same training comes out nearer 90 or 95% easy, because most of a hard session is warm-up, recovery and cool-down [3]. The eleven champions show both figures at once: 77% of their sessions were easy, and 91% of their time [1].

From here on a distribution is written as three percentages of training time: below the first threshold, between the thresholds, and above the second. The champions' was 91 / 3 / 5.

Other sports show the same first number. Thirty-six German junior rowers of national and international standard rowed at 95 / 2 / 3 over a 37-week season [4]. The most successful female cross-country skier in the history of the sport, a Norwegian with six Olympic gold medals, trained at 92 / 3 / 5 across her five best years [5]. World-class distance runners cover at least 80% of their distance at low intensity all year round [6].

Where the records disagree is in how the remainder is split. A distribution with more time above the second threshold than between the thresholds is called polarised, and one with more between them is called pyramidal. When Thomas Stöggl and Billy Sperlich reviewed the published records in 2015 they found that most described a pyramid. Polarised distributions turned up in three studies of cross-country skiers and one of marathon runners [7]. The name that became attached to elite training describes a minority of the elite training on record.

Road cycling is the untidy case. Thirteen professional cyclists followed through a season rode at 88 / 11 / 2 in the rest period, 78 / 17 / 5 before the racing began and 77 / 15 / 8 during it [11,7]. Eight professionals who wore heart rate monitors through 22 stages of a Tour de France spent the race at 70 / 23 / 7 [8]. A study of 28 riders from the sport's top division, published in 2025, found a pyramid in every phase of the season [13]. At the other extreme is the German squad that broke the world record for the 4,000-metre team pursuit in 2000, a track event that lasts about four minutes. Its riders covered 29,000 to 35,000 km a year, with road stage races as the heaviest blocks, at 94 / 4 / 2 [9,7].

The records of 30 under-23 professionals do not fit the pattern at all. Through a whole season their heart rates were below the first ventilatory threshold for 17 to 19% of their riding time, and between the thresholds for 51 to 63% [12]. The authors offered three explanations: that the coaches wanted it so, that the riders rode their easy sessions too hard, or that the zones had been set against the wrong heart rates. I can't choose between them from the paper.

Part of the untidiness comes from the counting. Fifteen well-trained road cyclists had the same ten weeks of training sorted three ways. By heart rate it was 87 / 9 / 4. By power it was 80 / 9 / 12. By the riders' own rating of each session it was 45 / 30 / 25 [14]. The rest, I suspect, comes from the sport. A skier or runner training alone chooses the pace, and a cyclist in a road race or a group has it chosen for them.

The diaries also record how easy the easy training was. The Norwegian federation's scale has five zones, and Chapter 5 placed its zones 1 and 2 both below the first threshold. In the champions' version zone 1 ran up to 73% of maximum heart rate, with blood lactate under 1.2 mmol/L, and Zone 2 from 74 to 83%, with lactate of 1.3 to 2.0 mmol/L. That Zone 2 is the band this book calls Zone 2, the upper part of easy. The champions spent 86.0% of their endurance training time in zone 1 and 5.3% in Zone 2. In the summer preparation phase that was 14.3 hours a week against 0.9 [1].

Runners at the top do the same. Most of their low-intensity running is in the lowest zone of the scale used to describe it, and a typical easy run is 3 to 5 km/h slower than the athlete's marathon pace [6].

For professional cyclists I have found no record that divides easy riding into its lower and upper parts. The nearest thing is Tadej Pogačar's account of himself. In September 2024, as the reigning Tour de France champion, he gave a long podcast interview about his training [15]. As it was reported, he put his Zone 2 at 320 to 340 W, said that on flat roads he will ride in it for five hours, and added that by the end of a ride like that the power has slipped to about 300 W [16,17]. He weighed about 65 kg at that year's Tour [15], which puts his Zone 2 at roughly 5 W/kg. The 14 trained cyclists and triathletes of Chapter 9, who rode about nine hours a week, reached their first threshold at 217 W [18]. He judges the zone, he said, from heart rate, power, speed and how he feels [15].

That is an interview and not a training record, and his Zone 2 is whatever his team's testing says it is. Taken at face value, it has the Tour de France champion doing his long rides in Zone 2, where the skiers do theirs below it.

Chapter 7 set this book's rule for an easy ride: it should carry as much work as it can without taking anything from the hard sessions, which for a rider on six to ten hours a week means Zone 2. I think the skiers and the cyclist are both following it. In summer the skiers fitted 15 easy hours a week around their hard sessions, in ten sessions, working their arms as well as their legs [1], and an hour near the top of the easy range burns carbohydrate far faster than an hour lower down [19]. For them the intensity that leaves the hard sessions untouched is a low one. Pogačar's account suggests that on a bike a professional can make five hours of Zone 2 a regular part of his week. A rider with seven hours, and a day or more between hard sessions, has more room than either and gives up work by riding lower. Nothing in these records tests that reading, and the skiers' figures come from diaries in which the athlete assigns each part of a session to a zone. C

How the pattern came about

Nobody designed it in a laboratory. A 2023 review of a century of distance-running training concluded that most of what runners do was arrived at by trial and error, and little of it by experiment [20]. From the 1930s to the 1950s the leading runners trained on intervals: Emil Zátopek ran sessions of 40 or 60 repetitions of 400 metres. In the 1960s the New Zealand coach Arthur Lydiard argued against the hard interval programmes and built his runners' preparation on 10 to 12 weeks of mostly easy continuous running, aiming at 100 miles a week. His athletes Peter Snell and Murray Halberg won four Olympic gold medals between them from 1960 to 1964. By the 1970s and 1980s many international runners had combined Lydiard's volume with interval sessions, on a pattern of hard days and easy days usually credited to the Oregon coaches Bill Bowerman and Bill Dellinger [20].

Rowing left numbers. Twenty-eight Norwegian rowers won international medals between 1970 and 2001, and 21 of them answered a detailed questionnaire about how they had trained. Between the 1970s and the 1990s the time the medallists spent training at low blood lactate went from 30 hours a month to 50. Their training at race pace and above went from 23 hours a month to about 7. Total volume rose by 20%, from 924 to 1,128 hours a year. Over the same period the medallists' VO2max rose by 12%, from 5.8 to 6.5 litres a minute, and their six-minute ergometer performance by almost 10% [21].

Dutch speed skaters made the same move without the extra hours. Across ten Olympic cycles from 1972 to 2010 the programmes of the medal winners went from 40 / 40 / 20 to about 80 / 12 / 8, with no trend in the total hours trained [22,7].

The skier with six gold medals went through it in a single career. Between the ages of 20 and 35 her training rose from 522 hours a year to 940. Early in her senior career she trained with long blocks of hard intervals, at about 88 / 2 / 10, improved quickly, and after a few years stopped improving. Her next improvement came with less hard training and much more easy training, at about 92 / 3 / 5, and it led straight into her five best seasons [5].

A squad of 51 German junior track cyclists gives a comparison between riders. Over 15 weeks of basic preparation, 17 of them raised their power at a fixed blood lactate by 11% and 17 lost 7%. The two groups had covered the same total distance. The improvers had ridden more of it at low intensity, and the decliners more of it around the threshold [23].

All of this is observation, and it points one way: athletes and squads did better as their training became bigger and easier. It cannot show that the training was the cause. Over the same decades altitude camps were added to the rowers' winters [21], and equipment, coaching and the number of people competing for a seat will have changed as well. A medal table also holds only the athletes a training system suited. The rower who broke down on 1,100 hours a year is not among the 28. And in the cycling squad the arrow could run either way: a junior who was losing fitness that winter for some other reason would have found the same speed harder, and more of his riding would have registered as threshold work. C

Why it might work

The first explanation is that hard training has a ceiling, and the champions' diaries show where it was for them. Their time above the first threshold came to 1.3 hours a week in the summer preparation phase, 1.3 in the autumn and 1.4 in the racing season. It was done in two sessions a week, rising to three before a championship, and over the year it made about 100 sessions and 63 hours. Their easy training in the same three phases was 15.2, 14.4 and 9.8 hours a week [1].

The single skier's records have the same shape. Her time between the thresholds and above the second, added together, came to 5.6, 5.4 and 5.7 hours a month in the three phases of her year, which is again about 1.3 hours a week, while her easy training went from 76 hours a month in summer to 55 in the racing season [5]. Her figures count the recoveries between intervals as hard time, so the efforts themselves were shorter than that.

Chapter 6 gave one reason a ceiling should exist. In Seiler's highly trained runners, heart rate variability was back to normal 5 to 10 minutes after an easy session and about 30 minutes after a harder one, and in less trained people the harder sessions took far longer to recover from [24]. Seiler's 2010 review found no convincing evidence that giving already well-trained athletes more hard training produced lasting gains [2]. One group in the trial described later in this chapter did 27 interval sessions in nine weeks, gained less than a group that did 14 alongside far more easy training, and lost 3.7% of its body weight on the way [25]. Chapter 13 takes up how many hard sessions a rider can use.

Once the hard training is at its ceiling, the only training left to add is easy, which was Chapter 7's argument. What the added hours do is less certain. They raise the total training load, which was the best predictor of mitochondrial content in the largest analysis of the question [26]. They may build the things that change over years and that short studies miss, the capillaries and the heart of Chapter 2 and the durability of Chapter 9. Reviews of elite training have also suggested that long easy sessions and short hard ones reach the muscle by different routes, the calcium signal of many thousands of contractions and the energy signal of a few very hard minutes, so that a programme with both gets more than either gives alone [27,20]. Chapter 2 described both signals. Nobody has shown that they add up in the way the suggestion needs. C

The polarised idea makes a second claim, which is that training between the thresholds should be kept small because it costs as much to recover from as harder training and achieves less. Seiler's runners support the first half: a session between the thresholds took the same 30 minutes to recover from as intervals above the second [24]. The records give the second half little support. Most elite distributions are pyramids [7]. Eight world-class Norwegian sprint skiers did more low-intensity and more moderate-intensity training than eight of national class [28]. And the model followed by the Norwegian runners who have lately won Olympic and world titles is built on the middle. It has three or four interval sessions a week run at a blood lactate of 2 to 4.5 mmol/L, checked with a meter during the session, and one session near VO2max, inside 150 to 180 km of running. Marius Bakken, the 5,000-metre runner who worked the method out on himself in the late 1990s, is one of the authors of the paper that describes it. His reason was that holding lactate down let him do much more fast running in a week than harder intervals had allowed [20].

In 2022 the journal Medicine & Science in Sports & Exercise printed two papers back to back. One, with Seiler among its authors, was titled "Polarized Training Is Optimal for Endurance Athletes" [29]. The other was titled "Polarized Training Is Not Optimal for Endurance Athletes" [30].

The pyramid, the polarised week and the Norwegian threshold model share a very large volume of easy training and a hard part controlled closely enough to be repeated every week for years. Where exactly the hard part sits looks to me like the smaller choice. C

Selection is the remaining explanation. These are full-time athletes who train eight to ten times a week [1], the men among the champions had a VO2max of 85 mL/kg/min [1], and the skier did 18 to 25% of her training at altitude camps [5]. She also took 12 years to build from 522 hours to 940, and the authors of her case study think the long build-up is what let her absorb the load [5]. What suits a person selected for the work and prepared for it over a decade is weak evidence for what suits anyone else.

What the trials found

No trial has assigned Olympic champions to a training programme. Stöggl and Sperlich wrote that neither elite athletes nor their coaches like to have training altered for an experiment [25], and the trials that exist used club and national-level athletes for periods of six weeks to five months.

An early one was published in 2007. Jonathan Esteve-Lanao and colleagues randomised 12 sub-elite runners to five months of training that differed only below the second threshold. Both groups did about 8% of their training time above it. One group did about 80 / 12 / 8 and the other 67 / 25 / 8. Both ran a 10.4 km cross-country time trial faster afterwards, the easier group by 157 seconds and the other by 122, a difference that reached statistical significance [31].

A trial in cyclists followed in 2013. Twelve trained men did two six-week blocks, one polarised at 80 / 0 / 20 on 6.4 hours a week and one threshold-heavy at 57 / 43 / 0 on 7.5 hours a week, in random order and with four weeks of detraining between. Their 40 km time trial improved after both blocks. Peak power rose by 8% after the polarised block against 3%, power at the lactate threshold by 9% against 2%, and time to exhaustion at a hard fixed power by 85% against 37% [32].

One study, up close: four ways to train for nine weeks

The largest of the early trials was run by Stöggl and Sperlich in Salzburg and published in 2014 [25].

Authors' conclusion
Authors' conclusionPolarised training gave the greatest improvements in most of the key measures of endurance. Threshold and high-volume training gave no further improvement.

The paper's title says that polarised training has a greater impact on endurance than the three alternatives. The full text supports something narrower.

The distributions are counts of sessions. The polarised group is described as 68 / 6 / 26, which means that 14 of its 54 sessions were interval sessions. Each held 16 minutes of hard effort. By my arithmetic from the paper's table, that is under four hours of intervals in 104 hours of training, less than 4% of the time [25].

The groups did not train for the same number of hours, and the polarised group trained for 38 hours more than the interval group over the nine weeks [25]. Only the comparison with the high-volume group is free of that difference.

That comparison has a problem of its own. Before the trial every athlete had been training the high-volume way, with at most two threshold sessions a week, and none had been doing intervals regularly. The high-volume group therefore carried on as before, and the authors say that this may be why it did not improve. The polarised group kept its volume and was given something new. What the trial shows most securely is that adding two interval sessions a week to a high-volume programme raised VO2peak in athletes who had not been doing intervals. It does not show that a ratio was responsible.

Nothing was raced, and the authors list the absence of a time trial as a limitation.

Four of the seven athletes who dropped out came from the threshold group, and among those who stayed in it the change in VO2peak ran from a gain of 4% to a loss of 20% [25]. A fifth of a trained athlete's VO2peak does not disappear in nine weeks of training three threshold sessions a week. Illness or a bad test is a likelier explanation, and in a group that had lost four of its members one such result moves the average a long way.

The headline number is also very large. The authors themselves wrote that an 11% rise in nine weeks in athletes of this standard "has to be put in perspective" [25], and Chapter 4 explained why the largest results from the smallest groups are the ones most likely to shrink when the experiment is repeated.

Later trials found less. Fourteen national-class rowers trained for 11 weeks at 94 / 1 / 6 or 93 / 3 / 2. Crews could not be split up, so the groups were not randomised. Power in a 2,000-metre ergometer test rose by 1.5% in both, which was not a significant change in either [33]. Sixty well-trained runners spent 16 weeks on a pyramid, a polarised programme, or eight weeks of one followed by eight of the other, at matched training loads. Every group improved its 5 km time by between 0.6 and 1.7%. The group that went from pyramid to polarised did best by about half a percentage point, a margin the authors acknowledged was smaller than the test could reliably detect [34]. An older trial had found that over 12 weeks the hard work might not be needed at all: 18 experienced rowers were split between training entirely at or below the lactate threshold and doing 30% of their training hard, and the two groups improved their 2,000-metre time and their VO2peak by the same amount [35].

Reading the research: an effect that shrank

The first pooled analysis of polarised training was published in 2019. Its authors found four randomised trials comparing polarised with threshold-heavy training in trained athletes, three of which measured a time trial and could be combined. Pooled, the polarised groups improved by 0.66 of a standard deviation more than the others, with a confidence interval from 0.15 to 1.17 [36]. The trials it drew on had 112 athletes between them [37].

Five years later a second group pooled 17 studies and 437 athletes. On time trials the difference between polarised training and everything else was 0.01 of a standard deviation, with an interval from −0.28 to 0.25, which is no difference. VO2peak still favoured polarised training, by 0.24, with an interval that only just excluded zero. That advantage appeared in programmes shorter than 12 weeks and in highly trained athletes and nowhere else, and the authors pointed out that the highly trained athletes were in the short studies, so the two could not be told apart [37].

In 2025 the author of the 2019 analysis returned to the question with the people who had run many of the trials, Seiler among them. They pooled the individual results of 348 athletes from 13 studies. Between polarised and pyramidal training they found no difference in VO2max or in time trial performance, and no significant difference between polarised training and any other distribution [38].

This is Chapter 4's simulation happening in real data. The first trials of a new idea are small, the small trials that get noticed are the ones that found something, and a first meta-analysis can only pool what exists. Nobody need have done anything wrong. When a training claim rests on a pooled result from three trials, the sensible reading is that the true effect lies at the low end of the interval or below it.

The same caution applies to what the later analyses found in subgroups. The 2025 paper reports that competitive athletes gained more VO2max from polarised training and recreational athletes more from pyramidal [38]. Chapter 4 listed the subgroup among the signs that a finding may not last, and this one has not yet been tested by a trial designed to look for it.

Cyclists have a pooled analysis of their own. Published in 2025, it took in 41 studies of trained cyclists, with 797 riders in 81 training groups. Training improved VO2max and time trial performance. Programmes classed as polarised did no better than those that were not. Longer programmes did better than shorter ones, and the volume of training, by the week or in total, showed no relationship with the size of the gain [39].

Taken together the trials support a narrow conclusion. Among programmes that are mostly easy and contain some hard work, no way of dividing the hard work has been shown to make athletes faster than another over 4 to 16 weeks [37,38,39]. B Whether one does better over years is unknown, because nobody has run the trial. C

Six to ten hours a week

The athletes in the records train 15 to 20 hours a week [1,5,21]. Most readers of this book train less than half of that.

The trials closest to that reader are of recreational runners. Thirty of them trained for ten weeks at about 77 / 3 / 20 or 46 / 35 / 19. The first group improved its 10 km time by 5.0% and the second by 3.6%, a difference that was not statistically significant [40]. Thirty-eight more trained for eight weeks at 77 / 3 / 20 or 40 / 50 / 10, with the two programmes matched for training load. Both groups improved by similar amounts, and the group that did more of its running between the thresholds got there in 24.8 hours of training against 29.9 [41]. The cyclists in the 2013 crossover were on six to eight hours a week and did better on the polarised block over six weeks [32]. The pooled analysis of cyclists found no difference [39]. Nothing here says the mix matters much at these volumes, and one trial says the threshold-heavy mix saves time.

The diaries suggest a different way to use the elite example, which is to copy the amount of hard training and ignore the ratio. The champions did about 1.3 hours a week above the first threshold, in two or three sessions [1]. In a seven-hour week that much hard training is 19% of the time. For the champions in summer it was 8%, and a seven-hour rider who trained to their percentage would do about half an hour of hard riding a week, which is far less than the champions did themselves.

Seiler is reported to have seen the same thing across athletes. According to a 2025 review, his view is that athletes training about 350 hours a year, which is a little under seven hours a week, tend to show threshold-heavy distributions, and that polarised and pyramidal patterns appear above about 750 hours [42]. It is a description of what people do, reported at second hand, and no evidence about what works best.

The professional peloton offers a small natural comparison. Over four years, 10 professional women trained fewer hours than 20 professional men, spent less of their time in the low zones, and arrived at a similar total training load. The authors' reading was that the women made up for lower volume with higher intensity [43].

Volume still counts for those who are adapted to it. When 18 male professionals were confined to indoor trainers for seven weeks in 2020, their training fell by a third, to about 12 hours a week with 2 of those hours at high intensity. Every one of them lost power over 5 and 20 minutes, by between 1% and 19% [44]. Those riders had also lost racing, the road and their routines, so the lost hours cannot be isolated. It is still a warning against reading this chapter as an argument that hours do not matter.

From the saddle. George: have you ever followed an 80/20 or polarised plan, or looked at a professional's easy ride and compared it with your own? What did your week look like then in hours, and how many of your rides were hard?

My advice to a rider on six to ten hours, on the strength of the elite records, is to decide the hard sessions first: two a week for most riders, a number Chapter 13 examines. Ride the others by Chapter 7's rule, with as much work in them as they can carry while the hard sessions still get done properly and the following week looks the same. On these hours that is Zone 2, a few beats under the threshold. Let the percentages fall where they fall. A ride between the thresholds is not forbidden by anything in this chapter. It should be counted as one of the hard sessions, because it costs as much to recover from as one. C

The best evidence that easy training matters comes from athletes who do 15 hours of it a week, and in the one set of diaries that divides it up, 86% of their training time was below Zone 2 and 5% was in it [1]. Elite practice is evidence for a large volume of training, most of it easy, and beyond one rider's interview it lends little to Zone 2 in particular. The case for Zone 2 is the narrower one from Chapter 7: a rider with few hours gets the most from the easy ones by riding near the top of the range.

Chapter 9: Durability

Draft · 5,956 words · 14 October 2026

175 kilometres alone

The final stage of the 2026 Tour de Luxembourg ran 177 km from Mersch to the Limpertsberg district of Luxembourg City. Mathieu van der Poel rode clear in the opening kilometres and stayed clear to the line. He said afterwards that it hadn't been the plan. He had found himself alone and assumed the chasers would come back. With 45 km to go, when the bunch swept up the riders who had been pursuing him, he still had three and a half minutes in hand, and he won by 1:04 from Mikkel Honoré [1].

Tadej Pogačar has made a habit of the same thing from shorter range. At Strade Bianche in March 2024 he attacked on the Monte Sante Marie gravel sector with 81 km left and won by 2:44. At the World Championships in Zürich that September he rode the last 50 km alone and held off Ben O'Connor by 34 seconds [2].

It is tempting to read these rides as proof of superior fitness, but a result on its own can't prove that. Van der Poel's lead came down by almost two and a half minutes in the last 45 km [1]. That could be a rider fading or a bunch finally racing for the finish, and nothing public tells us which. His power in the first hour and the last hasn't been released, so nobody outside his team can say how much he faded.

What the rides do is pose a question that the model in Chapter 1 leaves open. VO2max, the fraction of it you can sustain, and efficiency are all measured on a rider who is fresh. Races are decided four or five hours in. How much of what you had at the start do you still have at the end?

Most of us meet that question well below the level of a World Championship.

From the saddle. In 2025 I rode a tour of the Pyrenees. On the fourth day I was really struggling, while one of my friends in particular seemed to be getting stronger. He's an ex-professional golfer and spends a lot of his time on the golf course.

I can't tell you why he got stronger while I got weaker, and the research can't yet either. Most of it concerns a single long ride, and little of it reaches a fourth day in a row. But the thing I was short of that day now has a name and a growing set of measurements behind it.

What durability is, and what to call it

In 2021 a group led by Ed Maunder in Auckland argued that physiologists had been profiling athletes in the wrong state. Stephen Seiler, whose work on training intensity runs through this book, was among the authors. Lab tests are done rested. The qualities those tests measure shift during long exercise, and they shift by different amounts in different people. Maunder and his colleagues proposed the word durability for this, and defined it as the time of onset and the size of the deterioration in an athlete's physiological characteristics during prolonged exercise [3].

Three years later Andrew Jones, the Exeter physiologist whose lab worked with Nike's two-hour marathon project, made the stronger claim. He argued that this quality belongs in the Joyner and Coyle model as a fourth factor alongside the original three, and he called it physiological resilience: the ability to resist functional decline under stress [4]. The two terms describe the same thing. This book uses durability, because that is the word cyclists and coaches have adopted.

The most direct way to measure durability is to test a rider fresh, tire them, and test them again. The clearest examples come from Jones's own lab. In one study, 16 men did a three-minute all-out test fresh, and again after 40 minutes, 80 minutes and two hours of steady riding [5]. The test estimates critical power, the boundary described in Chapter 1 above which effort can't be sustained, and W′, the small reserve of work available above it. Critical power was 260 W fresh, 268 W after 40 minutes and 260 W after 80 minutes. After two hours it was 236 W, a fall of 9%. W′ went sooner: it was down 18% by 80 minutes and 23% by two hours. So the reserve you draw on for attacks and short climbs drains first, and the power you can hold for an hour stays put for a surprisingly long time and then drops.

The riders in that study were competitive amateurs with an average VO2peak of 52.5 mL/kg/min, much like the readers of this book and nothing like van der Poel. And the two-hour ride that cost them 9% of their critical power averaged 164 W, which began at about 64% of their VO2peak [5]. That is a steady endurance ride just above the top of Zone 2. Nobody was being broken.

Riders differed far more than the average suggests. Across three studies from the same lab, the fall in critical power after two hours averaged about 10%, but individual riders ranged from under 1% to about a third [4]. B On a fresh critical power of 260 W, that is the difference between losing a watt or two and losing more than 80.

A second way needs no lab. Researchers with access to professional riders' power files look for each rider's best efforts over set durations, then look again at the best efforts produced after a given amount of work has already been done that day. The work is counted in kilojoules per kilogram of body weight, the same kilojoules a bike computer shows. For a 70 kg rider, 45 kJ/kg is 3,150 kJ, roughly four and a half hours at an average of 195 W. Most of what is known about durability in professionals comes from this method, and a later section looks at what it shows.

A third is to watch what drifts during a long steady effort: heart rate climbing at constant power, or the oxygen cost of that power creeping up. This is the version you can observe on your own rides, and the thresholds that define your training zones drift too.

In 2022 Maunder's group took 14 trained cyclists and triathletes, people training about nine hours a week, and measured the power at their first ventilatory threshold (VT1), which marks the top of Zone 2. Then the riders did two hours at 90% of that power, an average of 194 W, and the threshold was measured again [6]. It had fallen from 217 W to 196 W, a drop of 10%. Individual falls ranged from about 9 W to 44 W. The heart rate at the threshold had moved the other way, from 142 to 151 bpm.

After two hours of riding in Zone 2, then, the top of Zone 2 was 21 W lower and 9 bpm higher than the fresh test said [6]. A rider holding fixed power drifts towards the threshold from below. A rider holding fixed heart rate sees their power sink. One caution: the riders had fasted overnight and drank only water, and the authors expect the shift to be smaller in someone who has eaten and is taking in carbohydrate. There was also no control trial in which the riders simply sat for two hours between tests, although it is hard to see why sitting would lower a threshold by 10%. B

How it works

The reviews of this field are candid that nobody can yet fully explain why two hours of steady riding lowers the power you can sustain [4,7]. Several mechanisms have some evidence behind them, and none yet explains why one rider loses 1% and another 30%.

Start with what critical power is. Below it, oxygen uptake rises when you start riding and then settles. Above it, oxygen uptake keeps climbing until it reaches VO2max and you have to stop. Critical power is the highest power at which uptake can still settle. It follows that anything which raises the oxygen cost of each watt, or lowers the amount of oxygen the body can deliver, will lower critical power, without anything having gone wrong in the usual sense of the word.

Both happen during a long ride.

Take the cost first. In the two-hour rides from Jones's lab, the power was held constant and oxygen uptake was not. In one study the same power went from 64% to 68% of VO2peak over the two hours [5]; in another, from 65% to 72% [8]. The rider's computer shows the same number throughout while the body works progressively harder to produce it.

Part of this is fuel. As a ride goes on and carbohydrate stores run down, the muscles burn proportionally more fat. In the second of those studies, carbohydrate use fell from 1.77 to 1.17 grams a minute while fat use rose from 0.68 to 1.08 [8]. Fat is the larger store by far, but it needs more oxygen than carbohydrate does to produce the same amount of ATP [7]. Jones and his colleague Brett Kirby estimate that the change in fuel accounts for roughly 30% of the rise in oxygen uptake over two hours. So fuel is part of the explanation and the smaller part.

A second contributor is which muscle fibres are doing the work. Chapter 1 introduced type I fibres, the slow, efficient ones. They are recruited first and do most of the work at moderate power. As those fibres tire or run low on glycogen, the nervous system has to call on fibres further up the recruitment order to keep the power constant. Those type II fibres can produce the force, but they use more ATP and more oxygen to do it [7]. The power on the screen is unchanged while a more expensive set of fibres is producing a growing share of it. The reviews favour this as the explanation for much of the rest of the rising oxygen cost. It is well supported as a general principle of how muscle is recruited. How much of the fall in critical power it accounts for in any particular rider has not been measured.

Delivery is the other side. Over a long ride you lose fluid as sweat, which lowers blood volume. Your core temperature rises, and blood is diverted to the skin to shed heat, where it competes with the working muscles. The heart fills a little less with each beat and beats faster to compensate. This is cardiovascular drift, the slow rise in heart rate at constant power that everyone who rides with a heart rate monitor has seen. In the Auckland study heart rate rose 8.2% over two hours at constant power [6]. If the drift goes far enough, the maximum amount of oxygen the heart can deliver falls, and VO2max, the thresholds and critical power can fall with it [7].

Heat makes all of this worse, and it is the mechanism I have felt most directly.

From the saddle. My power dropped massively during the heatwave of 2026, when the training room was 37 degrees.

Riding indoors in the heat is taken up properly in a later chapter. The point here is that part of what feels like fading legs is the circulation.

Then there is glycogen. The oldest explanation for fading late in a ride is that the muscles run out of stored carbohydrate, and nobody doubts that glycogen matters in long exercise. What Jones's lab found is that it doesn't neatly explain the fall in critical power. They took muscle biopsies from 14 men before and after two hours of riding. Glycogen fell by about 65% and critical power by about 11%, but the riders who lost the most glycogen were not the riders who lost the most critical power: the correlation between the two was 0.19, which is close to none [8].

That result is often repeated as "it's not about glycogen", which goes further than the data. The authors themselves point out that a biopsy samples a tiny piece of one muscle, and that a real relationship could be hidden by that alone. A study of 14 people is also too small to detect a relationship of moderate size reliably, a problem the Reading the research box in this chapter returns to. The fair summary is that in these data, glycogen loss did not predict who faded most. The same study found that the rise in oxygen cost did not predict it either. The two most obvious explanations both fail to account for the differences between riders, and the question is open. C

Carbohydrate during the ride is a clearer story. When the riders in the earlier study repeated the two-hour ride drinking 60 grams of carbohydrate an hour, their critical power afterwards was 254 W, against 236 W on a placebo and 260 W fresh [5]. Carbohydrate removed most of the fall. It did so without sparing muscle glycogen, which dropped by almost the same amount in both trials. Blood glucose and the rate of carbohydrate burning late in the ride were higher with the drink, and the authors' reading is that a steady supply of glucose from the blood kept the working fibres going. Fuelling gets its own section later in this chapter, with a larger and more recent trial.

Put together, the picture is of a rider whose watts become more expensive as the hours pass, whose oxygen supply slowly tightens, and whose fuel is shifting towards the kind that costs more oxygen to burn. Each change is modest. They compound, because critical power sits where supply and cost meet.

This also suggests why training might improve durability, and here the chapter moves from evidence to inference. If the trouble starts when type I fibres tire and more costly fibres take over, then anything that lets type I fibres carry the load for longer should delay it: more capillaries around them, more mitochondria inside them, a greater capacity to burn fat. Those are among the adaptations to endurance training described in Chapter 2. It is a plausible chain of reasoning, and it is the reasoning behind the long easy ride. Whether training actually changes durability, and which training, is a question for trials, and the rest of this chapter looks at how few there are.

Durability in the wild

It would be satisfying to say that van der Poel or Pogačar has exceptional durability, and plenty of commentary does. Here is what can be known from outside.

The results give distances, times and gaps. For the Luxembourg stage that means 177 km, a lead of 3:30 with 45 km left and 1:04 at the line [1]. They don't give power. A rider's speed on a long climb can be turned into an estimate of power, but the estimate rests on a guess at his weight, the wind and how long he sat in someone's slipstream.

The published science has the opposite limitation. The largest study of professional power files covers 112 riders and reports them as two anonymous groups [9]. It can tell you what WorldTour riders as a class do after 45 kJ/kg of work, and nothing about any one of them.

That is why this chapter makes no claim about van der Poel's physiology or Pogačar's. Their rides are here because they are what durability looks like from the roadside. Everything the chapter concludes comes from riders who were measured.

What the professionals show

Professional cyclists are the best-studied group in this field, for a practical reason: they race with power meters, their teams keep every file, and a season of racing contains maximal efforts made at every stage of fatigue.

The study with the most riders comes from a Spanish group led by Manuel Mateo-March, using data collected between 2013 and 2021 from 112 male professionals: 66 from the WorldTour, the sport's top division, and 46 from ProTeams, the division below [9]. For each rider they found the best power ever recorded for durations from 10 seconds to two hours. Then they found the best power for the same durations among efforts made after 15, 25, 35 and 45 kJ/kg of work that day.

Power fell at every duration as the work mounted up. After 15 kJ/kg the losses were between 1.6% and 3.0%, depending on the duration. After 45 kJ/kg they were between 6.0% and 9.7% [9].

The comparison between divisions is what made the study widely cited. Fresh, the WorldTour riders and the ProTeam riders were hard to tell apart: their best power did not differ consistently. After 45 kJ/kg the WorldTour riders had lost 4.7% to 8.8% and the ProTeam riders 7.6% to 11.6%, with the gap showing mainly in efforts of five minutes and longer [9]. On these figures, what separates the top division from the second is less what riders can do in the first hour than what they can still do in the last.

[FIGURE: best power by duration, fresh and after 45 kJ/kg, WorldTour against ProTeam, redrawn from Mateo-March 2022. Waits on the full text.]

Other groups have found the same pattern lower down the ladder. Among 53 climbers studied by Gabriele Gallo and colleagues, the fade after 20 to 50 kJ/kg was the only measure that separated the best professionals from the best under-23 riders, although not every comparison in that study reached statistical significance [10]. During the 2020 Vuelta a España, eight WorldTour and seven ProTeam riders were compared week by week. Fresh, the WorldTour riders were better only over 30 minutes. After 35 kJ/kg they were better over 30 seconds, one minute, five minutes, 20 minutes and 30 minutes [11]. That study is as close as the research comes to my fourth day in the Pyrenees, and with 15 riders it is a small one. B

Before taking these numbers home, it helps to see how far away they are. In a lab study of trained amateurs described later in this chapter, three hours at 169 W came to 24 kJ/kg [12]. The professionals' 45 kJ/kg is nearly double that. It describes the closing stages of a long professional race, a state few amateurs ever ride themselves into.

The method has a weakness, and it is worth understanding because power-file studies are becoming common. The "fatigued" figures are the best efforts a rider happened to make late in a ride. Nobody asked him to make them. A rider produces a maximal five-minute effort after 45 kJ/kg only if the race puts him in a position where it is worth doing, which means he is in the front group on the last climb. A domestique who has finished his work for the day rides in at whatever pace gets him to the bus. Better riders are more often in the finale, so they have more chances to record a high number late on. Some of the gap between divisions may be opportunity. This is my own reading of the method, and I have not seen the size of the effect estimated.

A second complication is that kilojoules are a blunt measure of how tired someone is. Two thousand kilojoules ridden steadily in the bunch and 2,000 that include repeated attacks are different days. A group led by Peter Leo counted only the work done above critical power and found that in under-23 riders, power for efforts of a minute or longer fell significantly after 5 kJ/kg of it, while professionals held on until 7.5 kJ/kg [13]. A 2026 systematic review of 21 studies reached the same conclusion more generally: hard work does more damage per kilojoule than easy work, with losses of 10% to 20% reported after as little as 2.5 to 15 kJ/kg of high-intensity riding [14]. For anyone comparing their own files, the lesson is to compare like with like. Power after three steady hours and power after a three-hour chain gang are answers to different questions.

Semi-professionals look somewhat different again. A Dutch-led group tested 16 of them three times across a season, fresh and after about 38 kJ/kg. One-minute power fell when they were tired. Ten-minute power did not [15]. That sits awkwardly beside the professional data, where the differences showed mainly in longer efforts, and I don't have an explanation for it beyond the obvious ones: a different level of rider, a lab protocol in place of race files, and 16 people.

Whether durability can be read off an ordinary lab test is disputed. In the amateur riders tested in Jones's lab, the fall in critical power was unrelated to VO2max or to critical power when fresh [7]. In 10 young professionals studied by James Spragg and colleagues, the change in critical power after a set of hard efforts correlated very strongly with VO2max, threshold power and efficiency, with correlations between 0.74 and 0.89 [16]. Ten riders is a very small group for claims of that kind, and the Reading the research box below explains why those numbers deserve less trust than they seem to. For now the fair statement is that nobody knows whether durability is a separate quality or mostly a reflection of being fit.

What trains it

This is the part of the chapter with the weakest evidence, and the people who know the field best say so. Jones and Kirby's 2025 review, the most thorough attempt to answer the question, describes the data as "scant" and states that no long-term study has yet set out to find the training that best improves durability [7]. What exists is one 10-week training trial in beginners, one study of strength training in trained cyclists, and a set of comparisons between groups: trained against untrained, faster against slower, professionals against under-23s.

Take the comparisons first. Trained runners drift less than active adults who don't run over an hour at the same relative intensity: in one study of 20 people the relative intensity rose by 2.6% in the trained group and 8.3% in the others [17]. Faster runners lose less running economy over 90 minutes than slower ones, 2.4% against 4.5% in a study of 44 men [18]. The same study, as Jones and Kirby describe it, found that among runners of similar ability, those who regularly ran for longer than 90 minutes held their economy better than those who didn't [7]. Professionals fade less than under-23s. Each comparison points the same way, and none can separate cause from effect. People who are naturally durable may find long sessions easier and so do more of them.

Spragg's group came closer by following 30 under-23 professionals through a season. Riders who spent more time below the first threshold improved their two-minute power when fatigued, and riders whose training became more polarised improved their fatigued 12-minute power [19]. The correlations were around 0.4. A correlation of that size is loose, and it is still observation: nobody was assigned to ride more easy hours.

The one trial that assigned people to easy or hard riding comes from Finland. Pekka Matomäki and colleagues took 35 sedentary or recreationally active adults and gave them 10 weeks of either low-intensity riding, averaging 6.8 hours a week, or high-intensity intervals, averaging 1.6 hours a week [20]. Before and after, each did a three-hour ride at an easy fixed power while the researchers tracked how much heart rate, energy cost and other measures drifted, and when the drift began.

Durability improved in both groups, and by about the same amount. In the low-intensity group the average drift went from 7.7% to 6.3% and its onset moved from 106 to 131 minutes, changes that fell just short of statistical significance. In the interval group the drift went from 8.8% to 5.4% and the onset from 108 to 137 minutes, both significant [20]. There was no significant difference between the groups.

In that trial, training of either kind made beginners more durable. Four times the hours of easy riding bought no more durability than the intervals did. And the changes were small. It can't tell you what happens in a rider with five years of training behind them, or over a year instead of 10 weeks, and the reviews' main suggestion is precisely that durability comes from volume accumulated over years [7]. A 10-week trial cannot test that idea. B for the claim that training improves durability in untrained people.

The study in trained cyclists was of strength training. Bent Rønnestad's group in Norway had 11 well-trained cyclists add heavy leg strength training twice a week for 12 weeks while nine carried on with endurance training alone [21]. Afterwards each rode for just over three hours at an easy pace and then did a five-minute all-out effort. The strength group's power in that effort rose from 371 W to 400 W. The control group's didn't change. It is one study of 20 riders, but it is an intervention in trained cyclists, measured late in a long ride, and the thing that worked was heavy weights. Strength training is covered in a later chapter. It belongs here as a reminder that the long ride is not the only candidate.

That leaves the long easy ride. The case for it is the mechanism described earlier, the cross-sectional comparisons, and Spragg's season of data. Against it stands nothing. No trial has shown that long rides fail to improve durability. There is simply no trial in trained riders that has tested them. I think the long ride is a reasonable bet. I would not tell you it has been shown to work. C

From the saddle. George: one or two lines on your own long rides: how often, how long, indoors or out?

There is one more observation to add, and it is only an observation. My friend in the Pyrenees, the one getting stronger on day four, spends a lot of his time on the golf course. If he walks his rounds, that is many hours a week of very low-intensity exercise, kept up for years. It fits the volume-over-years idea neatly, and a single golfer proves nothing.

Fuelling is a different matter, because there the trials exist.

From the saddle. One thing I try to do is out-fuel the riders I'm riding with. I can be a bit of a greedy pig, but that often helps keep me at the front of the group and gives me a strong finish in sportives.

The study from Jones's lab described earlier found that 60 grams of carbohydrate an hour removed most of the fall in critical power over two hours [5]. A larger and more recent trial tested whether more is better.

One study, up close: carbohydrate dose and critical power

In 2026 a group at Liverpool John Moores University recruited 16 trained cyclists and triathletes, 15 men and one woman, with an average age of 35 and an average VO2max of 51.8 mL/kg/min. All trained more than five hours a week [12]. They are a fair match for the readers of this book.

Each rider's critical power was measured fresh with a three-minute all-out test. Then, on three separate days at least six days apart, each rode for three hours at 95% of the power at their gas exchange threshold, which is the top of Zone 2 by another name. It averaged 169 W [12]. Straight afterwards they did the three-minute test again. On one day they drank only water, on another a drink supplying 60 grams of carbohydrate an hour, and on the third 120 grams an hour, as a mix of maltodextrin and fructose. The order was randomised and balanced, so that any effect of doing the rides first or last was spread evenly across the three conditions. This is a crossover design: every rider is compared with themselves, which removes the differences between people that swamp small studies.

The riders were well fed going in. For 24 hours before each ride they ate a supplied diet containing 8 grams of carbohydrate per kilogram of body weight, and they had a set breakfast [12]. Nobody started short of glycogen.

Fresh, critical power averaged 277 W. After three hours on water it was 236 W, a fall of about 15%. With 60 grams an hour it was 257 W, a fall of about 7%. With 120 grams an hour it was 266 W, a fall of about 4%. Each step was statistically significant. W′, the reserve above critical power, dropped by a similar amount whatever the riders drank [12].

The first step did most of the work: going from nothing to 60 grams an hour saved 20 W, and doubling it saved 9 W more. Stomach complaints were mild at every dose, though fullness and cramping scored higher at 120 grams than on water. And during the three-hour rides themselves, oxygen cost and efficiency did not differ between the drinks, so the carbohydrate was not working by making each watt cheaper. The riders on the highest dose could simply get closer to their VO2max in the final test [12].

What the study can't tell us is just as specific. The paper describes no attempt to disguise which drink was which, so the riders presumably knew when they were on water, and a three-minute all-out test depends on how hard someone is willing to go. Fifteen of the 16 were men. Carbohydrate was supplied by a supplement company, although the funding came from a public research foundation. Critical power was estimated from a single short test, and the authors themselves think the method may have understated the fall. And it measured one all-out effort after one ride. So it shows carbohydrate protecting late-ride power on the day, and leaves open whether fuelling well in training makes you more durable in the long run. B

Reading the research: correlation and small samples

Two studies in this chapter reported correlations, and they show two different ways a correlation can mislead.

Spragg's season-long study found that riders who spent more time below the first threshold improved their fatigued two-minute power, with a correlation of 0.43 [19]. Squaring a correlation gives the share of the variation it accounts for. Here that is 18%, which leaves 82% to everything else. Then there is the direction. Easy riding might build durability. Or riders who are already durable, or who are simply healthy and uninjured that year, might be the ones able to pile up easy hours. Or a third thing, such as a coach who prescribes both more volume and better fuelling, might drive both. An observational study can't choose between these, however carefully it is done. Only assigning riders to different training can.

Spragg's lab study found correlations as high as 0.89 between riders' lab measures and the change in their critical power [16]. That looks close to proof. It came from 10 riders. With a sample that small, the true value could plausibly lie anywhere from about 0.6 to 0.97, by the standard calculation of a 95% confidence interval. For the weakest of the reported correlations, 0.74, the plausible range runs from about 0.2 to 0.93. The same arithmetic applies in the other direction to the glycogen result earlier in the chapter. A correlation of 0.19 from 14 riders is compatible with a true value anywhere between about −0.4 and 0.65, which covers everything from a moderate negative relationship to a strong positive one [8]. With 10 or 14 people, chance alone can produce a large correlation or hide a real one.

When a paper reports a correlation, look for the number of people before the size of r. Below about 30, treat a striking correlation as a reason to run a bigger study. Chapter 4 goes through the reasoning in full.

What to do with it

Start by finding out how much you fade, because the range between riders is so wide that the average tells you little about yourself.

The simplest test needs a power meter and two rides. On the first, after a warm-up, ride as hard as you can for a fixed time: Spragg's group used two, five and 12 minutes [19], and five or 12 suits most amateurs. A week or so later, do the same effort at the end of a ride of two and a half to three hours at your usual endurance pace, eating and drinking as you normally would. The percentage you lose is a rough measure of your durability. Keep the route, the fuelling and the temperature as similar as you can, and expect the result to move by a few per cent from one attempt to the next for no reason at all.

You can also watch drift without a hard effort. On a long ride at steady power, compare your average heart rate in the last half hour with the half hour after you warmed up. In the Finnish trial, untrained people drifted by 5% to 9% over three hours at an easy pace [20]; in the Auckland study, trained riders who had not eaten drifted 8.2% in two [6]. Heat and dehydration push the number up on their own, so compare cool day with cool day.

For Zone 2 training, the Auckland result has a direct consequence. If you ride at the very top of your zone by power, the top comes down to meet you, and after two hours you may be above it. If you hold a heart rate ceiling set from a fresh test, your power will sink further than it needs to, because the heart rate at the threshold rises as you tire [6]. Neither method is right late in a long ride. My own rule of thumb is to set power 10 to 15 W under the top of the zone for rides over two hours, and to eat.

Eat on anything longer than about 90 minutes, which is roughly where critical power began to slide in Jones's lab [5]. Sixty grams of carbohydrate an hour gets you most of the protection in the trials above. More helps a little further if your stomach accepts it, and that is worth practising in training before trying it in an event [12].

Then there is the long ride itself. Keep doing it. The mechanism is plausible, the comparisons of durable and less durable athletes point to accumulated volume, and nothing else on offer has better evidence apart from fuelling and, on one study, the weights room. But hold the belief at its proper strength. If the next few years bring a trial showing that an hour of intervals builds as much durability in trained riders as it did in the Finnish beginners, that should change your mind, and it will change this chapter.

Chapter 10: Beyond performance

Draft · 4,061 words · 14 October 2026

Between 1991 and 2014 the Cleveland Clinic put 122,007 patients through a treadmill test, and in 2018 its cardiologists checked how many of them had since died [1]. In the quarter of patients least fit for their age and sex, 23.7% had. Among the 3,570 whose fitness put them in the top 2.3%, the figure was 2.6%. After adjusting for age, smoking, diabetes, heart disease and the rest, the least fit carried five times the risk of death of the fittest, and the authors wrote that being unfit was as dangerous as smoking or diabetes, or more so [1].

Figures like these are why Zone 2 is sold as medicine as well as training. Fitness predicts lifespan better than most things a doctor measures, steady riding builds fitness, and so an hour in Zone 2 is presented as an hour added to a life.

My reading of the evidence is narrower than that. Fit and active people do live longer. How much of the difference is the training and how much is the kind of person who becomes fit is not known, and the one long trial that randomised people to exercise found no difference in deaths. The trials do show that training lowers blood pressure and blood sugar, and the large surveys show that most of the lower risk arrives with the first few hours a week. A rider on six to ten hours has gone well past that point. For that rider, how the hours are split between Zone 2 and intervals is a question about performance, and the evidence on health gives no reason to favour Zone 2.

Fit people live longer

The Cleveland figures have limits that the headline leaves out. Fitness was not measured directly but estimated from the speed and slope of the treadmill when each patient stopped, and the patients were people sent for a test, mostly to look for heart disease or explain symptoms. Patients who had a drug-based stress test instead of exercising, 38,828 of them, were left out, as were those switched to one because they could not keep going [1].

The comparison with smoking also deserves a second look. The fivefold difference sets the least fit quarter against the top 2.3%, which is not the same kind of comparison as smokers against non-smokers. The smaller step, from just below average fitness to just above it, carried a hazard ratio of 1.41, exactly the figure for smoking [1]. A hazard ratio is how many times more often one group died at any point in the follow-up than the other, so 1.41 means 41% more. That smaller step is the one more readers could actually take, and it is still a large difference.

Surveys of activity tell the same story more modestly, because activity is a looser measure than fitness. In 2015 a group from the US National Cancer Institute pooled six cohorts in the United States and Europe: 661,137 adults, followed for a median of 14 years, who between them had 116,686 deaths [2]. They used the American guidelines as a yardstick, a minimum of 150 minutes of moderate or 75 minutes of vigorous activity a week, and grouped people by how many multiples of that minimum they reported in their leisure time.

Even activity below the minimum went with 20% lower risk than none. At one to two times the minimum the figure was 31%, at two to three times 37%, and at three to five times 39%, after which the curve went flat: five to ten times the minimum also gave 39% [2]. People who reported ten times or more had 31% lower risk than the inactive, and the authors found no evidence of harm at that level. Their summary was that meeting the minimum gave nearly the maximum benefit [2].

Riding hours can be placed on that curve, roughly. A MET is the body's resting rate of energy use, about 3.5 millilitres of oxygen per kilogram per minute. A rider with a VO2max of 55 mL/kg/min, close to the amateurs in the trials of Chapters 16 and 17, riding at 60% of it, near the first lactate rise in the two studies of Chapter 7's fat-max figure, uses about 33 mL/kg/min, or 9.4 METs. An hour of that is 9.4 MET-hours against the surveys' weekly minimum of 7.5 [2], so six hours a week is about seven and a half times the minimum and ten hours about twelve and a half. A rider on six to ten hours is somewhere between the flat part of the curve and the far end of the data, where few of the people surveyed were. The surveys asked about walking, gardening, running and the like, and gave each activity a standard value, so this is a placing, not a calculation the study made.

The lowest intensities carry an association too. A 2019 analysis that pooled eight cohorts in which 36,383 people wore movement sensors, rather than describing their own activity, found that the most active quarter had 73% lower risk of death than the least active over about six years, and that light activity on its own, the kind involved in slow walking and housework, followed the same pattern: 40%, 56% and 62% lower risk in the second, third and fourth quarters [3]. Time spent sitting ran the other way, with 2.63 times the risk in the most sedentary quarter.

Cycling has its own figures. In UK Biobank, 263,450 people who said how they travelled to work were followed for five years, and those who cycled had 41% lower risk of death from any cause than those who drove or took public transport, after adjustment for the usual factors. Those who walked had lower risk of heart disease but no significant difference in deaths overall [4].

That fit and active people live longer is one of the most consistent findings in epidemiology. That the training is the reason is a separate claim. C

Reading the research: marker or cause

The question is as old as the field. In the 1950s Jeremy Morris and his colleagues in London found less coronary heart disease among the conductors of double-decker buses, who climbed the stairs all day, than among the drivers who sat, and less among postmen than among civil servants at desks [5]. Morris knew the obvious objection, that heavier and less healthy men might choose the driver's seat, and his group went on to study the physique of busmen from the sizes of the uniforms they were issued [6].

The same objection applies to every cohort in this chapter. The Cleveland authors were explicit about it in their limitations: their study could not tell, they wrote, whether high fitness picks out patients who were going to live longer anyway or makes them live longer [1]. Training is not the only thing that makes a person fit, and each of the others would produce the same result on its own.

The first is illness. People who are already becoming ill, before any diagnosis, walk less far on a treadmill and also die sooner. Leaving out the patients who could not exercise removes the most obvious cases and leaves the hidden ones in.

The second is habit. People who exercise also tend to smoke less, eat differently and go to the doctor, and adjustment catches only what was recorded.

The third is inheritance. Chapter 2 described how differently people respond to the same training, and some of the fitness a person has is fitness they were born with. If the same genes that make a person fit also protect them in other ways, fitness would predict a long life without training having anything to do with it.

Twins offer a test of the third. Finland has followed same-sex twins born before 1958 for decades, and asked about their exercise in 1975, 1981 and 1990. Across individuals, those who kept up vigorous exercise through all three surveys had 45% lower risk of death to 2013 than those who never did, and 27% lower after adjusting for education, smoking, alcohol, weight, work and health [7]. Then the researchers found the pairs in which one twin exercised throughout and the other never did. Among non-identical twins, who share about half their genes, the active twin had 42% lower risk. Among identical twins, who share all of them, there was no difference at all: a hazard ratio of 1.00 [7]. An earlier analysis of the same cohort, over a shorter run of years, had found the same pattern [8].

The identical-twin result rests on 34 pairs, and its 95% confidence interval runs from about half the risk to about twice it [7]. Thirty-four pairs cannot show that exercise does nothing. They are, though, the nearest thing in the observational data to a controlled comparison, and the benefit that every other analysis shows does not appear in them. The authors suggest that some of the same genes influence how much people exercise, how fit they are and how long they live. C

The skill is to ask what else could make the exposed group different. A hazard ratio describes groups that differ in many ways at once, and says nothing directly about what happens to someone who moves from one group to the other.

A trial of exercise and death

The only way to settle the question is a randomised trial, and death is rare enough that a trial needs thousands of people, years of follow-up or a population old enough to die in useful numbers. Researchers in Trondheim chose the third.

One study, up close: Generation 100

In 2012 they invited every resident of the city aged 70 to 77, 6,966 people, to five years of training, and 1,567 agreed [9].
