The confidence intervals show how little five years could settle. Against the control group, the interval group's best estimate was 37% fewer deaths, with an interval running from 67% fewer to 20% more. The moderate group's was 24% more, from 27% fewer to a little over twice as many [9].

The trial had the problem that every long exercise trial has: the control group would not sit still. At five years 69% of them still met the guidelines, about one in five had taken up interval training on their own, and only about half of each exercise group kept to its programme. The volunteers were also healthier than their neighbours. Deaths in the control group ran at less than half the 10% expected over five years among Norwegians of that age, and the authors accept that a population this active may have had little room to gain [9]. A null result in people who were all exercising is not evidence that exercise does nothing.

What the trial does show is narrower and still useful. Five years of supervised training added nothing measurable to the survival of healthy, active 70-year-olds beyond what following the guidelines gave them. B And it offers no support for the idea that steady moderate exercise is the better medicine. The moderate sessions, at about 72% of peak heart rate and rated somewhat hard, were the nearest the trial came to steady Zone 2, although nobody measured whether they were below each person's first threshold. That group had the most deaths and the interval group the fewest, and the interval group ended with higher peak oxygen uptake than both others and better scores for physical and mental quality of life [9]. None of the mortality differences was statistically significant. Nor were the intervals dangerous: there were no heart problems during supervised sessions, and the three injuries were fractures from falls on slippery ground, two of them in the moderate group.

What training does to the risk factors

Trials of disease markers are easier to run, because blood pressure and blood sugar change in weeks, and there are many of them.

Blood pressure falls with endurance training. A 2013 analysis of 93 randomised trials involving 5,223 healthy adults found an average fall of 3.5 mmHg in systolic and 2.5 mmHg in diastolic pressure [10]. The size depended on where people started: 8.3 and 5.2 mmHg in people with high blood pressure, about 2 in those slightly above normal, and in people with normal pressure a fall of less than a millimetre in systolic pressure that could have been zero. A

Blood sugar falls too. In 47 trials of people with type 2 diabetes, structured exercise lowered HbA1c, a measure of average blood sugar over the previous two or three months, by 0.67 percentage points against controls [11]. Amount mattered: programmes of more than 150 minutes a week lowered it by 0.89 points and shorter ones by 0.36. Advice to exercise, without a programme, changed nothing unless it came with dietary advice. A

On intensity, the trials in patients lean the other way from the Zone 2 argument. In ten trials of 273 people with heart disease, high blood pressure, metabolic syndrome or obesity, interval training raised peak oxygen uptake by 3.03 mL/kg/min more than moderate continuous training, which the authors describe as almost double the gain [12]. In 50 studies of interval training, insulin resistance fell more after intervals than after continuous training, by a small to moderate margin [13]. Both sets of trials were small and short, and their authors call for longer ones. B

These are markers, the kind of measurement Chapter 16 warned about, and a marker that improves is a reason to expect fewer heart attacks and strokes, not a count of them. The markers that matter most for health respond to training of any intensity, respond more in the people who need them to, and respond more to more of it.

Zone 2 as medicine, weighed

The case for Zone 2 as medicine starts from metabolic disease, in which the muscles burn fat poorly and let lactate build up. If Zone 2 is the intensity that best trains a muscle to burn fat and clear lactate, the argument goes, it must be the best exercise for metabolic health, and through that for a long life.

The observation about diseased muscle comes from the 2018 comparison of professional cyclists, moderately active people and patients with metabolic syndrome described in Chapters 6 and 7 [14]. It describes three groups of different people at one moment, and it trained nobody. The claim about Zone 2 is the one Chapter 7 examined in detail. Per hour, harder training builds more mitochondrial content than continuous training, and the Canadian review that weighed the Zone 2 claims for the general population concluded that Zone 2 had not been shown to be the best intensity for either mitochondria or fitness [15]. The conclusion is contradicted by the trials above, in which intervals did at least as well on fitness and insulin resistance, and by the one trial of death, in which the trend ran towards intervals.

What the argument gets right is that steady exercise is effective medicine. It lowers blood pressure and blood sugar, it is safe for nearly anyone, and it can be done in amounts that hard training cannot, which matters because the diabetes trials found that amount counted. For a person starting from nothing, with a heart condition, or who simply will not do hard sessions, steady riding is a sound prescription. That is a long way from saying it is a special one. C

Heart rhythm and arteries in lifelong athletes

Tour de France riders give the most extreme cycling data. Of the 786 French riders who started the race at least once between 1947 and 2012, 208 had died by 2012, and their death rate was 41% lower than that of Frenchmen of the same age, with fewer deaths from both cancer and heart disease [16]. The authors could not separate the effect of the riding from the selection of unusually healthy young men, or weigh it against the effects of very heavy training and doping.

The costs show up in the heart's rhythm and its arteries. Among 52,755 people who completed the Vasaloppet, Sweden's 90 km cross-country ski race, between 1989 and 1998, those who had finished five or more times had 29% higher risk of atrial fibrillation, an irregular rhythm of the heart's upper chambers, than those who had finished once, and about twice the risk of an abnormally slow heart rhythm [17]. There was no link with the dangerous rhythms that cause cardiac arrest. The finding is stated for men.

In 284 men around 55 years old who played sport competitively or for recreation, CT scans found calcium in the coronary arteries of 68% of those with the most lifelong exercise, more than 2,000 MET-minutes a week, against 43% of those with under 1,000 [18]. But the most active men's plaques were more often fully calcified and less often of the mixed kind, which the authors describe as a more benign composition. At the 9.4 METs of the example rider above, 2,000 MET-minutes a week is about three and a half hours of riding, so every rider this book is written for is in that top group.

The most active endurance athletes, then, have more atrial fibrillation and more calcified plaque, and they still lived longer than the general population in the Tour data and longer than the inactive in the surveys. For a rider on six to ten hours the balance looks favourable. A rider who notices palpitations, or a heart rate that behaves strangely, should see a doctor, not a training book. C

Colds

In the winter described in Chapter 7, when I rode more and mostly easy, I fell ill less often with coughs and colds. That is one person's winter, and there is a literature that bears on it.

In 2011 David Nieman's group followed 1,002 adults through 12 weeks of autumn and winter. People who did aerobic exercise five or more days a week had 43% fewer days with cold symptoms than those who did it once a week or less, and the third who rated themselves fittest had 46% fewer than the least fit third, after adjusting for seven other factors [19]. The study was observational, and fitness was self-rated.

The same researcher's earlier work started the opposite worry. Of the runners who finished the 1987 Los Angeles Marathon without an infection in the week before, 12.9% reported one in the week after, against 2.2% of experienced runners who had entered and not run, and runners training at least 97 km a week had twice the odds of an infection in the two months before the race of those running under 32 [20]. From studies like this came the idea of an "open window" after hard exercise, when the immune system is suppressed.

The idea is now disputed. None of the marathon-era infections was confirmed in a laboratory, and when a later study swabbed elite athletes who reported symptoms, only 11 of 37 episodes turned out to be infections; the rest may have been allergy, asthma or airways irritated by hours of heavy breathing [21]. Marathons also put thousands of people together in crowds and on planes. In a 2020 debate article, researchers on both sides agreed that regular moderate exercise helps the immune system and that infection in athletes has many causes, and left open whether hard exercise in itself raises the risk [22].

So regular exercise goes with fewer colds, and big events and very heavy training may go with more, for reasons that may have little to do with the exercise. No study I found compared easy riding with intervals for infections. My winter fits the first finding, and it also fits the fact that I was riding more often. C

Weight

Chapter 7 dealt with the question of which fuel a ride burns and concluded that for weight it matters little. What matters is how much energy the riding uses. In the Midwest Exercise Trial, 141 overweight young adults exercised five days a week for ten months without being asked to change their diet, using either 400 or 600 kcal a session. Among those who completed nearly all their sessions, weight fell by 3.9 kg and 5.2 kg, while the control group gained 0.5 kg [23]. Across 50 studies of interval training, weight fell by 1.3 kg against controls [13]. Exercise alone takes off a few kilograms over months, in proportion to the work done, whatever the intensity. B

What this means for a rider on six to ten hours

The large surveys place a rider on six to ten hours well into the part of the curve where more activity buys no more lower risk, or beyond the end of the data. Whatever exercise does for lifespan, that rider has most of it already, and nothing in the evidence says that the split between Zone 2 and intervals changes it. If the one trial points anywhere, it points to the intervals.

That leaves the split as a question of performance, which is where Part III takes it up. It also leaves a simpler piece of advice for anyone riding mainly for their health, or advising someone who is. The first few hours a week of activity of almost any kind carry most of the association with a longer life, and the risk factors improve with any intensity and improve more with more. On the evidence, the best exercise for health is the one a person will keep doing. In Generation 100, about half of each exercise group was still doing its programme after five years [9]. C

From the saddle. George: Did health, or living longer, play any part in why you took up Zone 2, or in why you ride at all? Has anything you can measure changed since you started riding regularly, such as your resting heart rate or blood pressure? A sentence or two in your own words.

Chapter 14: Fuelling

Draft · 6,458 words · 14 October 2026

In a study published in 1989, researchers from Maastricht followed riders from one of the leading professional teams through the Tour de France, 22 days and about 4,000 kilometres, and had them write down everything they ate [1]. Eight riders were asked and five agreed to keep a food diary. One crashed out after four days and a team-mate took over, so four riders have a complete record.

They took in an average of 24.7 megajoules a day, which is about 5,900 kilocalories, and 32.4 megajoules on the hardest day in the mountains. Almost half of it, 49%, was eaten on the bike. Across 115 hours of racing that came to 94 grams of carbohydrate an hour. The largest single source of their energy was sweet cakes, eaten during the stages. A maltodextrin drink came second and bread third [1].

That figure is worth holding against the way fuelling is talked about now. The usual account is that professionals have recently learned to eat far more during races than riders once did, 100 or 120 grams of carbohydrate an hour from drinks and gels designed for the purpose, and that this is one of the main reasons racing has become faster. A 2026 review of the subject says the change "has been widely described as a 'carbohydrate revolution'" [2].

The number came from diaries, and a diary is not accurate to the gram, although these were checked each week by a nutritionist against what the team had supplied, and the drinks came in standard bottles [1]. It should make anyone cautious about the word revolution. Riders in the Tour were eating a great deal on the bike in the 1980s, and much of it was cake.

The Durability chapter covered what eating does to the fade late in a long ride. This chapter takes the rest: how much carbohydrate helps, whether very large amounts help more, whether the gut can be trained to take them, and what the evidence says about water, salt, caffeine, ketones, beetroot juice and the red drink riders are handed at the finish.

What a ride costs

A power meter makes the cost of a ride easy to estimate. The body turns roughly a quarter of the energy it burns into work at the pedals, and a kilocalorie is a little over four kilojoules, so the two factors nearly cancel. A ride that records 2,000 kilojoules of work has cost about 2,000 kilocalories.

Three hours at 200 watts is 2,160 kilojoules. A gram of carbohydrate supplies about 4 kilocalories, so 60 grams an hour for those three hours puts back 720 kilocalories, a third of what was spent. At 90 grams an hour it is half.

Nobody replaces everything on the bike, and nobody needs to. The body carries far more energy as fat than any ride could use. What it runs short of is carbohydrate, which is stored as glycogen in the muscles and the liver in amounts that a few hours of hard riding can empty. The harder the riding, the larger the share of the fuel that is carbohydrate. In one of the trials described below, highly trained cyclists riding at the top of Zone 2 after an overnight fast burned a little over 2 grams a minute of their own stored carbohydrate, about 130 grams an hour, on top of what they were drinking [3].

Professionals in a grand tour are at the far end of this. The researchers on the 1980s Tour estimated that the riders spent 25.4 megajoules a day, about 6,100 kilocalories, and their body weight did not change over the three weeks. They ate 12.3 grams of carbohydrate per kilogram of body weight a day [1]. Three decades later, investigators weighed everything nine riders on a WorldTour team ate through the three weeks of the Vuelta a España and found an average of 12.5 [4]. For a 69-kilogram rider, the average in the Tour study, that is about 850 grams of carbohydrate a day, every day for three weeks.

How it works: the limit is in the gut wall

Carbohydrate eaten on the bike has to leave the stomach, cross the wall of the small intestine into the blood, and be taken up by the muscle. Researchers can follow it by labelling the carbohydrate in a drink with a traceable form of carbon and measuring how much of that carbon comes out in the rider's breath, which shows how much of the drink has actually been burned.

Such studies kept finding a ceiling. However much glucose a rider drank, the body burned no more than about 60 grams of it an hour. Intakes of 144 and 180 grams an hour did not raise the figure much [5]. The stomach was emptying fast enough and the muscle could have used more, so by elimination the limit was absorption. Glucose crosses the gut wall through a transport protein called SGLT1, and at about a gram a minute those transporters are working at capacity [5].

Fructose crosses by a different transporter, GLUT5. In 2004 Asker Jeukendrup's group gave eight trained cyclists drinks containing glucose alone, or glucose with fructose, during two hours of riding. With glucose alone, at either of two doses, the peak rate at which the drink was burned was about 0.8 grams a minute. With the mixture it was 1.26 [6]. A second route through the gut wall let more sugar through.

Four years later the same group tested whether that mattered for performance. Eight cyclists rode for two hours and then did a time trial lasting about an hour. On a glucose and fructose drink they finished it 8% faster than on glucose alone, and 19% faster than on water [7]. B

This is the basis of the standard advice for long events, which is up to 90 grams an hour from a mixture of sugars. The 2026 review treats 90 grams as the upper limit of established guidelines and anything above it as "ultra-high" [2].

Does more help?

The largest trial of dose was run across four research sites and published in 2013. Fifty-one cyclists and triathletes each rode for two hours at a steady, fairly hard pace and then did a 20-kilometre time trial, on four occasions. Twelve drinks were tested, from 10 to 120 grams of carbohydrate an hour in steps of 10, along with a placebo, and neither the riders nor the testers knew which drink was which [8].

Performance rose with dose, steeply at first. Riders were 1% faster than on placebo at 9 grams an hour, 2% at 19, 3% at 31 and 4% at 48. The curve peaked at 78 grams an hour with a gain of 4.7%, and above that the benefit shrank [8].

The 2026 trial described in the Durability chapter has the same shape. Going from nothing to 60 grams an hour saved 20 watts of critical power after a three-hour ride, and doubling the dose to 120 saved 9 more [9]. That carbohydrate during a ride of two hours or more improves performance is as well established as anything in sports nutrition. A

Above 90 grams an hour the evidence thins quickly. In 2022 a trial compared 120 grams an hour with 90 in eleven highly trained cyclists during three-hour rides. At 120 the riders burned more of what they drank, 1.51 grams a minute against 1.29. But they burned just as much of their own stored carbohydrate as before [3]. The extra sugar displaced fat as a fuel and spared no glycogen. And less of it was used: by the 2026 reviewers' calculation about 86% of the carbohydrate drunk at 90 grams an hour was burned and about 76% at 120, which leaves some 30 grams an hour unaccounted for. They suggest much of that is still sitting in the gut [2].

The study most often cited in favour of 120 grams was run during a mountain marathon. Twenty elite runners, who had trained their guts beforehand, were randomised to 60, 90 or 120 grams an hour. A day after the race the 120 group had lower blood markers of muscle damage and reported a lower overall load during the race [10]. The study did not show that they ran faster [2], and with 20 runners split three ways it had little chance of detecting it if they had.

The 2026 review, by a group based in Auckland, went through all of this and concluded that intakes above 90 grams an hour remain "poorly evidenced", with support "largely derived from anecdotal reports and elite athlete practice rather than controlled research" [2]. Two trials from one laboratory, comparing doses from 60 to about 110 grams an hour, found no significant difference in time-trial performance between them. C

The authors do not dismiss the practice. They point out that people differ: in one study, riders all given 120 grams an hour burned anything from about 1.3 to 1.9 grams a minute, so a few could use nearly all of it and most could not. And they suggest that the real benefit of eating a great deal during a stage may arrive the next day, as better recovery. That would matter in a three-week race, and no laboratory trial of a single ride would detect it [2].

A second review, published the same year by seven British researchers, reads the same studies and reaches a different recommendation. It argues that the upper limit could now rise from 90 to 120 grams an hour, at least for trained athletes, because more of the carbohydrate is burned at the higher intake [11]. The two reviews agree on the facts more than their conclusions suggest. This one states that the intakes of 120 to 200 grams an hour now being tried by athletes are "not yet substantiated by current scientific research", and concedes that a definitive study of dose and performance "is yet to be completed". Its case for 120 grams rests on how much is burned, and the Auckland review's case against rests on the absence of a performance gain. A reader can hold both: more is burned, and nobody has shown it makes you faster.

Reading the research: what the fast riders eat

In 2012 a group surveyed 221 athletes after Ironman triathlons, a marathon, a long cycle race and professional stage races, asking what they had eaten and what stomach trouble they had had. Intakes ranged from 6 to 136 grams of carbohydrate an hour. In the Ironman races, the athletes who ate more finished faster, and the correlation was a fairly strong one [12].

It is tempting to read that as proof that eating more makes you faster. The survey can't show it. The faster athletes in an Ironman are the fitter and more experienced ones. By my reasoning, not the survey's, they are working at a lower fraction of their capacity at any given speed, which leaves more blood flow for the gut, and they have had years to practise eating in races. Speed may be what allows the eating. The same survey found that higher intakes went with more nausea and flatulence [12], so the athletes eating the most were not having an easy time of it.

The argument that professionals eat 120 grams an hour and professionals are fast has the same weakness, and so does the argument that winners eat more than the riders they beat. Chapter 4 calls this kind of evidence observation. It can suggest a cause and it can't establish one.

It is also worth knowing who is involved. The review on training the gut that this chapter draws on appeared in a journal supplement supported by the research institute of a sports drink company, and its author received a fee for attending the meeting behind it [5]. The carbohydrate in the 2026 dose trial was supplied by a supplement company [9]. That does not make the findings wrong, and the big dose trial was double-blind. Of the seven authors of the review that argues for raising the limit to 120 grams, five declare a relationship with one sports nutrition company, and one of them is employed by it [11]. The lead author of the more sceptical review declares a post with a company that sells other supplements [2]. None of this settles who is right. It does mean that the questions which get studied are often the ones a manufacturer would like answered, and that a reader should know where a recommendation to eat more of a product comes from.

Is this why racing is faster?

I could not find a study that tests the claim, and it is hard to see how one could be done. Fuelling did not change in isolation. Over the same years bikes, tyres, clothing and riding positions changed, and so did training, altitude camps, the heat work described in the Heat training chapter and the age at which riders turn professional. A race has no control group, and there is no way to hold everything else still.

What can be said is narrower. Carbohydrate during long rides improves performance, and that has been known for decades. Riders in the Tour were recorded eating 94 grams an hour in the 1980s, and their total for the day, 12.3 grams per kilogram, is almost the same as the 12.5 measured on a WorldTour team three decades later [1,4]. Those are two teams in two different races, one recorded by diary and one by weighing, so the match should not be pressed.

The hourly figures that have been published tell a similar story with a recent upturn. The British review, some of whose authors work with professional teams, says that professional cyclists' intakes have usually been reported at 60 to 90 grams an hour. It gives Chris Froome's figures across nine stages of the 2018 Giro d'Italia, which he won: between 52 and 96 grams an hour, with the highest on the hardest day. It also passes on a press report that a stage winner at the 2025 Tour took about 116, and reports that some riders are said to be training their guts to tolerate 200 or more [11]. So the leading edge has moved up in the last few years, from the nineties to something over a hundred, if the press figures are right. That is a smaller change than the word revolution implies, and the 200 figure is hearsay. The controlled trials do not show that going from 90 to 120 makes anyone faster on the day [2]. So if modern fuelling has made racing faster, the laboratory evidence suggests it did not do so through a higher hourly number in the final of a single stage.

It might still be true by the recovery route. A rider who eats more on Tuesday starts Wednesday with more glycogen, and over three weeks that could add up. Both reviews say as much. The British one suggests that higher intakes help riders match what they eat to what they spend each day, "even if this does not necessarily confer an immediate performance benefit" [11].

It might also be true through consistency. An average from a handful of riders on one leading team says nothing about how many riders in the race ate that much, on how many days, or how well it stayed down. If every rider now eats what only the best-organised did then, the race is faster without the ceiling having moved. Both are reasonable guesses and neither has been tested. C

Can the gut be trained?

The stomach can, and the best demonstration comes from outside sport. Competitive eaters train for weeks by drinking large volumes of fluid and eating progressively larger amounts of the food they will compete on. The stomach learns to stretch further, and a full stomach comes to feel less full. When Jeukendrup reviewed the subject in 2017 the record stood at 69 hot dogs, with buns, in ten minutes [5].

The athletic version is less dramatic and works the same way. In one study he describes, runners practised drinking at a rate that matched their sweat losses during 90-minute runs, and their stomach comfort improved over repeated sessions, although the rate at which the stomach emptied did not measurably change. What changed was how the fullness was tolerated [5].

Diet changes how fast the stomach empties, and the change is specific to the nutrient. Volunteers whose diet was supplemented with 400 grams of glucose a day for three days emptied a glucose drink from the stomach faster afterwards, with a half-emptying time of about 21 minutes against 29, while a protein drink emptied no faster than before [5]. Three days was enough.

The intestine is where the evidence gets thinner. In animals the answer is clear: feed more carbohydrate and the gut wall makes more of the SGLT1 transporter. Mice had nearly twice as much after two weeks on a high-carbohydrate diet, and changes have been seen in other species within days [5]. Nobody has measured this directly in a human athlete, because it would mean taking samples of the intestinal wall. "No direct human studies exist," the review says. The human evidence is indirect, and the most important piece of it is one study in cyclists.

One study, up close: 28 days of eating on the bike

In 2010 a group at the Australian Institute of Sport led by Gregory Cox pair-matched 16 trained cyclists and triathletes and randomly allocated one of each pair to a high-carbohydrate group and the other to a low-carbohydrate group, for 28 days of their normal training, about 16 hours a week [13,5].

Both groups ate the same moderate-carbohydrate base diet. The high group was given extra carbohydrate, mostly as a glucose drink, before, during and after every training session. The other group was given a supplement of fat and protein with the same energy content, eaten after training. The difference between the groups was whether the gut met a stream of sugar during exercise every day for four weeks. Daily carbohydrate intake averaged 8.5 grams per kilogram of body weight in one group and 5.3 in the other [5].

Before and after, every rider did a test ride of 100 minutes at a steady pace followed by a time trial, drinking a glucose solution labelled with a tracer so that the amount burned could be measured. In the high group, the amount of the drink burned over the 100 minutes rose from 54.6 grams to 63.6. In the low group it did not change [13].

That is a rise of about a sixth in how much swallowed glucose reached the muscles and was used, after four weeks of practice. The likeliest explanation is that the gut wall had made more transporters, as it does in animals. The study did not measure that.

The performance result is less convenient. Time-trial performance improved by about 6% after the four weeks of training, and it improved by the same amount in both groups [13]. The riders who had trained their guts absorbed more and went no faster than the riders who hadn't.

There are limits on both findings. Eight riders a group is a small study by the standards of Chapter 4. The test drink was glucose alone, so the study cannot say whether training raises the higher ceiling that glucose and fructose mixtures reach. I have read this paper's abstract and Jeukendrup's detailed account of it, not the paper itself. And sixteen years later the 2026 reviewers note that its central finding "has not been consistently replicated" [2]. C

Symptoms are another matter

Whether or not practice lets you absorb more, there is better evidence that it makes eating on the move more comfortable.

A group at Monash University in Melbourne ran two trials in recreational runners. In the first, 25 runners did a two-hour run while eating 90 grams of carbohydrate an hour, followed by a one-hour distance test. They were then randomly assigned to two weeks of repeated practice with carbohydrate gels, with carbohydrate foods, or with a placebo, and tested again. Total gut symptoms during the second test were 60% lower in the gel group and 63% lower in the food group. They were also 25% lower in the placebo group, so some of the improvement came simply from doing the test a second time. The distance covered in the hour rose by 5.2% and 4.3% in the two carbohydrate groups and fell by 2.1% on placebo [14,15].

The second trial was blinded. Eighteen runners practised daily for two weeks, during an hour's running, with either 90 grams of carbohydrate an hour or a placebo, without being told which. Total symptoms fell by 61% in the carbohydrate group and 2% in the placebo group. Breath hydrogen, a sign of sugar reaching the large intestine unabsorbed, fell from a peak of 13 parts per million to 6 with carbohydrate and did not change with placebo [16,15].

A third trial, from the Australian Institute of Sport, did not find this. Nineteen elite race walkers and 18 elite runners spent two weeks training on either 30 grams of carbohydrate an hour or 60 to 90. In the race walkers on the higher intake, total gut symptoms were greater after the two weeks than before. In the runners, symptoms rose during exercise by the same amount in both groups. Breath hydrogen did not change, and neither did the runners' time-trial performance. The authors' own title describes the effects as "minor" [15].

All three are in a 2023 systematic review, which found eight studies of gut training in total. It concludes that two weeks of repeated feeding reduces gut discomfort and malabsorption, and that stomach emptying does not change. It also notes that no standard protocol exists, that 79% of participants were men, and that most of the studies were in runners [15]. Only one used cycling alone. The review was written by the Monash group, and its senior author is also the senior author of the two trials that found a benefit. That is normal in a small field, and the review reports the contrary trial fairly, but it means the positive case comes from one laboratory.

So the gut can probably be trained, in the sense that matters most to most riders. In recreational runners, two weeks of regularly eating during exercise made it more comfortable and reduced how much sugar passed through unabsorbed. B In elite athletes one trial found no such benefit, and in cyclists it has barely been tested. That practice raises the ceiling on how much carbohydrate can be burned rests on one small study. C And nobody has shown that a trained gut makes a cyclist faster.

From the saddle. George: what do you eat and drink on a long ride, and roughly how much an hour? Have you ever had stomach trouble from it, or found that you could take more with practice?

Not only fuel

One finding does not fit the picture of carbohydrate as petrol. In 2004 nine endurance cyclists did a time trial lasting about an hour, twice. In one they rinsed a carbohydrate solution around the mouth for five seconds at intervals and spat it out. In the other they did the same with water. Nothing was swallowed either time. With the carbohydrate rinse they finished in 59.57 minutes against 61.37, at an average of 259 watts against 252 [17].

An hour is too short for a rider to run out of glycogen, and nothing reached the gut. The researchers concluded that receptors in the mouth detect carbohydrate and that the signal raises what the brain is prepared to ask of the muscles. That is the subject of Chapter 3: effort is regulated centrally, and it responds to information. The 2026 reviewers raise this as one possible explanation for why riders report feeling better on very high intakes, while noting that rinsing works most reliably in riders who have fasted, and that nobody has shown the effect grows with dose [2]. B

Eating less on purpose

For some years the fashionable idea in sports nutrition ran the other way. Training with low glycogen, by riding before breakfast or withholding carbohydrate after a hard session, amplifies the molecular signals described in Chapter 2. A 2018 review counted the studies: training low increased cell signalling in 73% of them and raised oxidative enzymes in 78%. The same review counted performance, which improved in 37% of studies and did not change in 63% [18].

A meta-analysis in 2021 pooled the nine training studies done in well-trained endurance athletes. Periodically restricting carbohydrate had no overall effect on performance compared with doing the same training well fed [19]. B

This is Chapter 2's warning in a real case. A larger signal after a session did not become a larger adaptation over weeks, and the likely reason is that riders short of carbohydrate cannot train as hard. The Cox study points the same way from the other side. Its two groups ate very differently around training for a month and improved by the same amount [13]. Within the range those studies cover, how much carbohydrate you eat around training seems to matter less to how fit you get than either camp has claimed.

The real hazard is at the far end. Athletes who persistently eat less than their training costs develop a recognised syndrome, relative energy deficiency in sport, which affects men as well as women and damages both health and performance [20]. A cyclist trying to lose weight while training hard is an obvious candidate.

Water

For decades the standard advice was to drink enough to keep weight loss during exercise under 2% of body weight, because laboratory studies showed performance falling beyond that. In many of those studies the riders knew whether they had been allowed to drink, and they were tested in still air, which no cyclist outdoors experiences [21].

A 2011 meta-analysis took only studies that used cycling time trials. It found five, with 39 riders between them. Riders who finished an average of 2.2% lighter performed no differently from riders who had been kept topped up: the difference was 0.06%. Drinking according to thirst gave better results than drinking less than thirst asked for [22].

In 2015 an Australian group removed the rider's knowledge from the experiment. Ten cyclists were dehydrated by 3% of body weight and then given back all, a third or none of the fluid through a drip, without knowing which. They then rode a 25-kilometre time trial at 33 °C with a 32 km/h headwind. Performance did not differ between the three conditions [21].

Two years later a British group did the blinding another way, by putting water into the stomach through a tube, and got the opposite result. Seven active men, about 2.4% dehydrated and at 34 °C, did 152 kilojoules of work in a 15-minute test against 165 when hydrated [23].

So two blinded studies disagree, and they had ten and seven people in them. They differed in ways that could matter: trained cyclists against active men, a strong headwind against less airflow, a time trial against a short all-out test. I would not claim more than this: moderate dehydration costs a fit rider less than the old advice implied, it probably costs more in heat and still air, which describes an indoor trainer, and thirst is a reasonable guide. C

Drinking too much is the more dangerous error. At the 2002 Boston Marathon, researchers took blood from 488 finishers. Thirteen per cent had hyponatraemia, a blood sodium concentration low enough to be abnormal, and 0.6% had it to a critical degree. The runners at risk were those who had gained weight during the race by drinking more than they lost, and those who took more than four hours. Whether they drank water or sports drink made no difference [24]. Hyponatraemia can kill. A slow rider on a long cool day who drinks at every opportunity is more exposed to it than to dehydration.

Salt

Electrolyte tablets and salt capsules are sold on two promises: that they prevent hyponatraemia and that they prevent cramp. The evidence for both is poor.

At a 161-kilometre mountain run in California, 94% of the runners surveyed were taking sodium supplements, and 6.6% of the finishers tested still had hyponatraemia. Those who developed it had taken sodium at the same rate as those who did not. What marked them out was over-drinking [25]. Low blood sodium in endurance events comes from too much water, and salt tablets do not correct that.

On cramp, a South African group followed 210 triathletes through an Ironman. Forty-three cramped. Their blood electrolytes and weight changes before and after the race were no different from those of the athletes who did not cramp. What predicted cramp was a faster race pace and a history of cramping [26]. That points to a muscle pushed beyond what it is trained for, and away from salt.

One randomised trial found a benefit. Twenty-six triathletes took salt capsules or a placebo during a real half-Ironman, and the salt group finished faster [27]. With 26 athletes split between two groups and a p-value of 0.04, it is the kind of result Chapter 4 advises waiting to see repeated. C

Sodium in a drink does no harm, and a salty drink may be easier to keep drinking. It is not protection against either of the things it is sold for.

Caffeine

Caffeine is the best-supported performance aid a cyclist can legally buy. A 2018 meta-analysis pooled 46 randomised, placebo-controlled trials of endurance time trials. At moderate doses of 3 to 6 milligrams per kilogram of body weight, caffeine raised average power by about 3% and shortened finishing time by about 2%. The response varied, and two of the studies found riders slower [28]. A

A common belief is that regular coffee drinkers get nothing from it. A Brazilian trial tested that in 40 trained cyclists, divided by their habitual intake into low, moderate and high consumers. Each did a time trial of about half an hour three times: after caffeine, after a placebo and after nothing. On caffeine they averaged 29.92 minutes, against 30.81 on placebo and 31.14 with nothing, and the benefit was the same in all three groups [29]. The gap between the placebo and nothing is worth a second look after Chapter 4: a capsule with nothing in it produced about a quarter of the gain that caffeine did.

For a 70-kilogram rider, 3 to 6 milligrams per kilogram is 210 to 420 milligrams, which is roughly two to four strong coffees. It is usually taken about an hour before the effort. The costs are the familiar ones, and the one that matters most to training is sleep.

Ketones

Ketones are fuel molecules the liver makes from fat when carbohydrate is scarce, as in fasting. A ketone ester drink raises their level in the blood without the fasting. The idea became famous through a 2016 paper from Oxford, reporting five studies in 39 athletes, which concluded that the drink changed which fuel the muscles used and improved endurance performance [30].

The following year an Australian group gave a ketone drink or a placebo to ten professional cyclists before a simulated 31-kilometre time trial. On the ketone drink they were 2% slower, with stomach discomfort and a higher sense of effort [31]. It was a different ketone compound from the Oxford one, and the product's defenders have said so. But when a 2020 meta-analysis pooled all 13 randomised trials of ketone supplements taken before exercise, of either type, it found no effect on performance [32]. B

The use that has survived is after exercise. A group at Leuven in Belgium put 18 fit men through three weeks of deliberately excessive training, two sessions a day, with a ketone drink or a control drink after each session. By the third week the ketone group was sustaining a higher training load, and their power in the last half hour of a standard two-hour ride was 15% higher than the control group's [33]. That is a striking result from nine people a group in one laboratory, and it is waiting for independent repetition. C

The sport's governing body reviewed the evidence in 2025, said it found none that was compelling, and recommended that riders not take ketones, without banning them. The Drugs chapter has the details.

Beetroot juice and the red drink at the finish

These are two different things, taken for different reasons.

Beetroot juice is taken before a ride, for its nitrate, which the body converts into a compound that widens blood vessels and appears to lower the oxygen cost of exercise. The trial that started the interest came from Exeter in 2011. Nine club-level cyclists drank half a litre of beetroot juice, or the same juice with the nitrate removed, two and a half hours before time trials of 4 and 16.1 kilometres. With nitrate they were 2.8% and 2.7% faster, and their power over the longer distance rose from 233 to 247 watts [34].

Nine years and many trials later, a meta-analysis of 80 studies found that the effect is real and small, and that it depends on who drinks it. In recreationally active men it was clear. In well-trained endurance athletes, with a VO2max of 65 or more, it was absent. In the six studies done only in women it was not detectable [35]. A club rider might gain a little from beetroot juice before a race. B A professional almost certainly gains nothing, which makes it an odd thing to copy from one.

The red drink handed to riders as they cross the line is something else: tart cherry juice, as at least one professional team has explained on its own website [36]. It is taken for recovery. Tart cherries are rich in plant compounds that reduce inflammation, and the juice also contains sugar, which a rider needs at that moment anyway.

The trial closest to cycling gave 16 trained cyclists cherry concentrate or a placebo twice a day for a week, with a hard 109-minute simulated race on each of the last three days. Blood markers of inflammation and oxidative stress rose less in the cherry group [37]. The abstract reports no measure of how well they rode. A 2026 meta-analysis of 19 trials in athletes found that cherry juice improved the recovery of muscle strength and lowered one marker of inflammation, and had no significant effect on soreness or on the usual blood marker of muscle damage. The authors rate the certainty of the evidence as very low to moderate [38]. C

There is a reason to be careful with it in training. The inflammation and oxidative stress that follow a hard session are part of the signal described in Chapter 2. In a small study in men, backed by experiments in rats, a gram of vitamin C a day, another antioxidant, hampered the gains from endurance training and suppressed the molecular signals that build mitochondria [39]. Nobody has shown that cherry juice does the same. But the logic of taking it is strongest in a stage race, where the aim is to be recovered by tomorrow, and weakest after an ordinary training ride, where the aim is to adapt.

What to do with it

Scale it to the ride. Under about 90 minutes at an easy pace there is no need to eat on the bike; that is roughly where critical power began to slide in the trial described in the Durability chapter [40]. For rides of two hours or more, eat from early on.

Sixty grams an hour gets most of what is available. In the largest trial the first 50 grams an hour bought four of the 4.7 percentage points on offer [8]. Sixty grams is roughly two gels and a banana, or a large bottle of sports drink and a bar. Check the labels, because products vary.

Go to 90 only for long, hard days, and only with a mixture of sugars. Above 60 grams an hour, glucose alone is absorbed no faster, and the excess is the likeliest cause of stomach trouble. Products that combine glucose or maltodextrin with fructose are made for this.

Above 90 is unproven. It may help a professional recover between stages. For an amateur doing one ride it has not been shown to add speed, and at lower power outputs less of it will be used. A rider averaging 150 watts spends about 540 kilocalories an hour, and 120 grams of carbohydrate would replace nearly all of it.

Practise. If you plan to eat a lot in an event, eat that way on your long training rides for at least the two weeks before. That is the dose in the trials that reduced symptoms. Use the same products you will use on the day.

Don't under-eat to get fitter. Training short of carbohydrate has not improved performance in trained athletes, and chronic under-eating causes harm.

Drink to thirst. That is enough on most rides. Drink more deliberately indoors and in heat, and never so much that you finish heavier than you started.

Don't rely on salt to prevent cramp or low blood sodium. Pace and training are the better explanation for cramp.

Caffeine works. Three milligrams per kilogram about an hour before a hard effort is the low end of the range that has been tested, and regular coffee drinkers benefit as much as anyone.

Leave the rest to the professionals. Ketones have not been shown to improve performance. Beetroot juice may be worth a small gain to a club rider before a race. Cherry juice is a recovery drink for multi-day events.

Remember what sugar is. The 2026 reviewers warn that a habit of high carbohydrate intake around every session is a different thing from fuelling a race. They cite a study in which about 30% of athletes on a moderately raised carbohydrate intake showed fasting blood glucose in the pre-diabetic range within a month, reversibly [2]. Fuel the long and hard rides. An easy hour does not need a gel.

Chapter 15: Strength training

Draft · 2,844 words · 14 October 2026

In 1980 Robert Hickson published a study with three groups of volunteers. One lifted weights five days a week. One did endurance training six days a week. The third did both programmes in full. After ten weeks the endurance and combined groups had raised their VO2max by the same amount, about 25% on a bike test. Strength told a different story. The combined group kept pace with the lifters for seven weeks, then stopped improving, and in the ninth and tenth weeks went backwards [1].

The paper's title called this an interference, and the word stuck. For lifters it was a warning that endurance work costs strength. Many endurance athletes took it to mean that the two kinds of training don't mix, and stayed out of the gym.

The study never showed that. Its endurance result was that lifting did no harm: VO2max rose as much with weights as without. Eight years later Hickson tested the question the right way round. Eight runners and cyclists who had stopped improving added heavy leg training three days a week for ten weeks and changed nothing else. Their leg strength rose by 30%. Their VO2max did not change. But they lasted 11% longer in a hard effort of four to eight minutes on the bike, and their time to exhaustion at 80% of VO2max went from 71 minutes to 85 [2].

Eight people with no comparison group, tested by time to exhaustion, is the kind of evidence Chapter 4 says to hold loosely. It set the question for everything since: whether lifting makes a trained cyclist faster, and by what route, given that it does nothing for VO2max.

What the cycling trials found

Most of the modern evidence comes from Scandinavia, and the largest share of it from one group in Norway led by Bent Rønnestad.

Their first trial took 20 well-trained cyclists. Eleven added heavy strength training to their normal riding, and nine carried on riding only. The lifting was four leg exercises, three sets of four to ten repetitions with a weight that could only just be lifted that many times, twice a week for 12 weeks. The lifters increased the cross-sectional area of their thigh muscles and their power at a fixed blood lactate level, and improved their average power in a 40-minute all-out test. The riders who only rode came close to improving in that test too, falling just short of statistical significance, so the gap between the groups was smaller than the headline suggests. VO2max rose equally in both groups [3].

The same riders were also tested at the end of a long ride, and that result is in the Durability chapter. After 185 minutes of easy riding, the lifters' power in a five-minute all-out effort rose from 371 to 400 watts over the 12 weeks, and the riders who had only ridden did not change [4].

A Danish study gave 14 young elite cyclists 16 weeks of endurance training with or without strength work. Short efforts of five minutes improved by 3 to 4% in both groups. Performance in a 45-minute test improved by 8%, and only in the group that lifted [5].

The Norwegian group then ran a longer study in young elite riders: 25 weeks, nine lifting and seven not. The lifters gained more in peak aerobic power and in 40-minute power than the others, with no change in VO2max in either group [6]. And they repeated the design in women. Nineteen well-trained female cyclists were randomly assigned to 11 weeks with or without heavy strength training, and the 11 who lifted improved their 40-minute power, their cycling economy and the fraction of VO2max they could sustain. The eight who did not lift did not change [7].

Not every trial agrees. In 1999 an Australian group randomly assigned 21 trained female cyclists to 12 weeks of heavy squats twice a week or to a control group. The squatters' strength rose by 36%. Their power in a one-hour test did not change, nor did their lactate threshold or anything measured in their muscles [8]. A 2010 systematic review that admitted only randomised trials in highly trained road cyclists found five, of which two showed no benefit [9].

The Norwegian review offers reasons for two other trials that found nothing: one used light, explosive training instead of heavy loads, and the other lasted only six weeks [10]. It has no such explanation for the Australian one, which used heavy squats for 12 weeks.

The pooled result

A meta-analysis published in 2026 gathered every controlled study it could find of heavy strength training in cyclists, heavy meaning loads of at least 80% of the most a person can lift once. There were 17 studies with 262 participants, 60 of them women, and programmes lasting from 5 to 25 weeks at one to three sessions a week [11].

Lifting had no effect on VO2max, on the power at which VO2max is reached, or on threshold power. It had a small effect on efficiency, the oxygen cost of riding at a given power, and moderate effects on sprint power and on performance in time trials and tests to exhaustion [11].

The authors rate the certainty of all of this as low. Their assessment found a moderate risk of bias across the studies, "mainly due to a lack of group randomisation", and the pooled estimates are imprecise. Nine studies measured performance, and four of those nine came from the Norwegian group. The authors also state that they registered their protocol after analysing the data [11], which is the opposite of what registration is for.

So the best summary available says that heavy lifting probably makes trained cyclists somewhat faster in efforts from a few minutes to about an hour, and that the studies behind that conclusion are small, mostly unrandomised and concentrated in a few laboratories. B

How it works

The engine described in Chapter 1 has three parts: VO2max, the fraction of it that can be sustained, and efficiency. The pooled data say that lifting leaves the first two alone. What it changes is efficiency and the muscle's ability to produce force.

A review by Rønnestad and Iñigo Mujika sets out the mechanisms that have been proposed [10]. Rønnestad led most of the Norwegian trials, so this is the field's main contributor summarising work that is largely his own. The simplest mechanism is that a stronger muscle uses a smaller share of its maximum on every pedal stroke. The slow-twitch fibres, which are the efficient ones, can then do more of the work before the less efficient fast-twitch fibres have to be called in. The review cites a study in which, among cyclists of equal VO2max, the stronger ones showed less electrical activity in their muscles at a given power.

A second is a change within the fast-twitch fibres themselves. Both the Danish study and the Norwegian women's study took muscle biopsies and found that lifting shifted fibres from the most fatigable fast-twitch type, IIX, towards the more enduring IIA type [5,7]. In the women, the riders whose fibres shifted most were the ones whose 40-minute power improved most.

A third is in how force is applied. Strength training raised the rate at which the elite Danish riders could develop force by 12 to 20% [5]. In the 25-week Norwegian study the lifters came to produce their peak torque earlier in the pedal stroke, and that change tracked their improvement in 40-minute power [6].

These are proposals supported by correlations in groups of ten to twenty riders. Chapter 2's caution applies: a change seen in a biopsy alongside a change in performance has not been shown to be its cause.

Reading the research: one group of riders, two papers

The first Norwegian trial and the long-ride result described above have the same design in every detail: 20 cyclists, 11 lifting and nine not, seven men and two women in the comparison group, four leg exercises twice a week for 12 weeks [3,4]. They read as two reports from one group of riders.

There is nothing improper in that. A study that takes three months and several kinds of test produces more than one paper can hold. But a reader counting studies will count two, and a quick survey of the literature will find "several trials from Norway" where there are fewer independent groups of riders than papers. When a field depends heavily on one laboratory, it is worth checking how many separate experiments stand behind the list of references.

The abstracts of those two papers also say that the riders "were assigned" to their groups. The women's trial says "randomly assigned" [7]. The difference matters. Cyclists who choose to spend a winter in the gym may be the more motivated or the more ambitious, and Chapter 4 described how the same problem undid an early study of pain tolerance. The meta-analysis identified missing randomisation as the main weakness of this whole literature.

Will it make me heavier?

This is the usual objection from cyclists, for whom every kilogram counts on a climb.

In the first Norwegian trial the lifters' thigh muscles grew, and their body mass did not increase [3]. In Hickson's 1988 study, leg strength rose 30% with no change in thigh girth or in the size of the muscle fibres [2]. In the 25-week study of elite riders, lean mass in the lower body did increase [6]. The meta-analysis found that the riders' body mass made no difference to the size of the benefit [11].

The Norwegian review summarises the wider literature the same way: across trials of 8 to 16 weeks in cyclists, runners, triathletes and skiers, added strength training did not increase total body mass, while the muscles being trained grew by about 3 to 6% [10].

Hickson's original finding works in the cyclist's favour here. Heavy endurance training holds back the muscle growth that lifting would otherwise produce [1]. The review gives a likely reason, and it is one Chapter 2 introduced. AMPK, the energy sensor that endurance exercise switches on, suppresses the pathway through which lifting builds muscle protein [10]. A rider doing ten hours a week on the bike and two sessions in the gym is unlikely to gain much muscle. Strength can rise a long way without size, because much of the early gain comes from the nervous system learning to use the muscle that is already there.

How much, and for how long

The programmes in the 17 studies were similar enough to describe an average. They used about four exercises a session, loads of about 84% of the one-repetition maximum, which is a weight that can be lifted about seven times, and ran for about 14 weeks. The squat was the commonest exercise, used in 12 of the studies, followed by calf exercises, leg curls and the leg press [11]. All of it is heavy lifting for the legs. The review by Rønnestad and Mujika concludes that for cycling the evidence favours heavy loads over light, explosive work, and it adds a detail about how to lift them. The weight will move slowly because it is heavy, but the rider should try to push it up as fast as possible. In one study it cites, lifting with that intent produced larger gains in strength and in the rate of force development than lifting at a deliberately moderate speed [10].

Its recommended programme is two sessions a week for 12 weeks, with two or three sets of four to ten repetitions and two to three minutes' rest between sets, using exercises that work the same muscles through a similar movement to pedalling. It warns that legs feel heavy and sore for the first days after each session at the start, and advises cutting back the riding for the first two or three weeks. The suggested time to begin is straight after the end of the season, when riding matters least [10].

Strength gained in the winter can be kept through the season on very little. The Norwegian group had six cyclists lift twice a week for a 12-week preparation period and then once a week for the first 13 weeks of the racing season. They kept the muscle and the strength they had gained, and by the end of the 25 weeks had improved their 40-minute power more than six riders who had not lifted at all [12]. Twelve riders is very few, but the finding fits the pattern from Chapter 2 that an adaptation is cheaper to keep than to build.

Nobody has compared replacing riding with lifting against adding lifting on top. The 2010 review suggested that the trials which replaced some riding did better than those which added to it [9]. The Norwegian trials added it and found a benefit. The question is open, and it matters to a rider with a fixed number of hours.

The case that has nothing to do with speed

Cycling is weight-supported. The skeleton does not carry the rider's weight on a bike the way it does in running or walking, and bone responds to load.

A study in California compared 27 male masters cyclists, average age 51, who had raced for an average of 20 years, with normally active men of the same age and weight who were not athletes. The cyclists had lower bone mineral density at the spine and the hip. Four of them, 15%, had values in the range used to diagnose osteoporosis. None of the non-athletes did [13].

A 2012 systematic review of 31 studies reached the same conclusion: adult road cyclists have low bone density in key regions, the lumbar spine in particular, and road cycling "does not appear to confer any significant osteogenic benefit" [14]. Mountain bikers and cyclists who did other sports as well were better off. C

These are comparisons between groups, so they cannot show that cycling caused the difference, though it is hard to think of another explanation for fit men having weaker bones than their less active peers. I have not found a trial showing that lifting restores bone in cyclists, and this chapter does not claim it. Loading the skeleton is the standard advice for bone health, and a squat loads the spine and hips. For a rider over 40 whose only exercise is the bike, this may be a better reason to lift than anything in a time trial.

From the saddle. George: do you lift, or have you? What did you do, and did you notice anything on the bike?

Is this why racing is faster?

The answer is the same as for fuelling. Nobody has tested it, too much else changed at the same time, and the idea is older than it is made to sound: Hickson's cyclists were riding longer after ten weeks of leg weights in 1988.

What the evidence supports is a modest gain in trained riders from a specific kind of lifting. It does not support the idea that the gym is a recent discovery, or that it accounts for a large share of anyone's speed.

What to do with it

Lift heavy, for the legs. The trials used squats, leg presses and similar exercises at weights that could be lifted four to ten times, for about three sets. Light weights for many repetitions are not what was tested.

Twice a week for about three months builds it. Once a week keeps it. Start just after your season ends, and ride less for the first two or three weeks, because your legs will be sore.

Push the weight up as fast as you can. It will still move slowly. The intent is what the evidence supports.

Expect a small gain where it matters and none in VO2max. The benefit showed up in efforts of a few minutes to an hour, and in one trial at the end of a three-hour ride. Your VO2max will not move.

Don't expect to bulk up. Riders doing a full endurance programme gained strength with little or no gain in weight.

Learn the lifts properly. The Norwegian review says that endurance athletes should develop proper technique with lighter loads before lifting heavy ones [10]. None of the trials reports on injuries in a way I can cite, and a heavy squat done badly is a reliable way to miss a month of riding. If you have not lifted before, get the movement taught.

If you are over 40 and only ride, lift for your bones as well. The evidence that road cyclists have low bone density is consistent, and the bike will not fix it.

Chapter 16: Heat training

Draft · 4,401 words · 14 October 2026

Ice vests on the start line

The 2026 Tour de France began in Barcelona in temperatures above 30 °C and got hotter. Stage 3, from Granollers over the border to Les Angles, was raced in 36 °C while wildfires burned near Perpignan. Tadej Pogačar won it and called the day "a logistic nightmare", by which he meant the quantity of water and ice his team had to get to its riders. The next stage, out of Carcassonne, had up to 40 °C forecast. Riders that week waited for the start in frozen vests. Ineos had its riders stand with their forearms in iced water. Alpecin handed out carbohydrate gels that had been frozen solid. Ice socks, stockings packed with ice and pushed down the back of a jersey, are banned once the racing starts and were everywhere before and after it [1]. Four days later the organisers cut 30 km from stage 9, between Malemort and Ussel, after the Corrèze was put on a red heatwave alert [2].

Cooling a rider on the day is one answer to heat. The other is to send him to the race already adapted to it. In January 2025 Jeroen Swart, a sports scientist who has spent six years at UAE Team Emirates, described part of how the team had prepared Pogačar the previous season. They had been running training sessions at 35 °C and 90% humidity. Altitude training, he explained, thickens the blood by shrinking its liquid part, and heat does the opposite: plasma volume, he is quoted as saying, "can be increased by up to 25 per cent" [3].

The practice has spread well beyond one team. A group of Danish researchers who work with professional riders wrote in 2024 that heat training had become widespread among them, and that riders were using it to go faster in ordinary temperatures as well as to prepare for hot races [4]. The equipment has followed. A Swiss company sells a sensor the size of a matchbox that clips to a heart rate strap and shows an estimate of core body temperature on a bike computer, and its maker has announced partnerships with 10 professional teams [5].

Heat training is sold for two purposes, and only one of them is well supported.

The first is preparing for heat. If your event will be hot, training in the heat beforehand makes you faster on the day. Nobody disputes this. In one study, two weeks of training at about 35 °C improved cyclists' time-trial performance in the heat by 16% [6]. In another, 10 days improved it by 10% [7]. A Cooling on the day has good evidence too, and a later section covers the vests and the slushies.

The second is using heat the way riders use altitude: as a stimulus that makes you fitter everywhere, including on a cool day. That is the claim behind the sensors and the heat suits, and it is the subject of this chapter. It also makes a good test of the two questions this book keeps asking. What is the mechanism, and has the effect been shown or only inferred from it?

Where the idea came from

The modern interest dates from a single paper. In 2010 Santiago Lorenzo and Christopher Minson at the University of Oregon took 12 trained cyclists and had them ride for 10 days at an easy intensity, about half of VO2max, in a room at 40 °C. Eight matched controls did the same riding at 13 °C [8].

When the heat group was tested afterwards in the cool room, their VO2max had risen by 5%, from 66.8 to 70.2 mL/kg/min [8]. The work they could do in a time trial had risen by 6%, and their power at the lactate threshold by 5%. The control group had not changed. Plasma volume in the heat group was up 6.5%, and the heart's maximum output had risen with it.

For 10 days of easy riding in cyclists who were already trained, that is a very large effect, and people noticed.

Other labs tried to repeat it, and the first attempts did not go well. The two-week study mentioned earlier tested its cyclists in the cool as well as the heat. After two weeks their VO2max had gone from 4.79 to 4.82 L/min, and their power in a 43 km outdoor time trial from 300 W to 302 W [6]. The 10-day study used a crossover, in which the same seven men did 10 days of training at 38 °C and, separately, 10 days at 18 °C. The heat block raised their VO2max and time-trial power in a hot room by about 10% each. In a room at 18 °C, both were unchanged [7].

By 2016 the disagreement was formal. The Journal of Physiology ran one of its set-piece debates, with Minson and James Cotter arguing that heat acclimation does improve performance in the cool, and Lars Nybo and Carsten Lundby arguing that it does not [9,10]. Even the proposers hedged. Their summary describes the benefit as unresolved and possibly "modest or absent in some settings and individuals", and they pointed out that nobody had yet tested truly elite athletes or run the heat for longer than a couple of weeks.

Length turned out to matter, because the blood adapts to heat in two stages.

How it works

The first stage is fast. Within about a week of daily heat exposure, the liquid part of the blood expands. The studies in this chapter put the increase at 6% to 7% [8,7,11], which is a long way short of the 25% in Swart's interview, although he did say "up to". With more plasma, the heart fills more fully on each beat and has blood to spare for the skin. Sweating starts sooner and becomes more copious and more dilute: in one five-week study, riders' sweat rate rose by 0.44 litres an hour [4]. Core temperature at a given workload comes down. All of this is what makes a rider faster in the heat, and it is why the benefit there is so reliable.

For performance in the cool, the same change cuts both ways. Chapter 1 described VO2max as mostly a matter of oxygen delivery, which is the amount of blood the heart can pump multiplied by the oxygen each litre carries. Extra plasma helps the first and hurts the second, because the same number of red cells is now spread through more liquid. The Copenhagen group suggested that the two roughly cancel [11]. That would explain why 10 days of heat does so much in a hot room and so little in a cool one.

The second stage is slow, and it is the reason for the current fashion. If heat exposure continues for several weeks, the total amount of haemoglobin in the body starts to rise. Haemoglobin is the protein in red cells that carries oxygen, and its total mass, measured in grams, sets how much oxygen the blood can carry in all. Raising it is the whole point of altitude training.

Why heat should do this is not established. The leading idea is that the kidney, which makes the hormone that drives red cell production, responds to how concentrated the blood is. Dilute the blood with extra plasma for long enough and the kidney reads it as a shortage of red cells and orders more [4]. It is a neat explanation, and the evidence for it is indirect. When the Copenhagen researchers measured the relevant hormones during five and a half weeks of heat training, they found no change in them [12]. The haemoglobin went up anyway.

So the mechanism predicts two things that can be checked. Short heat blocks should do little for cool-weather performance, which is what the 2015 studies found. And blocks of four or five weeks should raise haemoglobin mass, and performance with it. Since 2019, a series of trials has tested the second prediction.

What the five-week trials show

Six have been published, and their designs are similar enough to compare. Riders add about five hour-long sessions a week of easy riding in the heat, for five weeks, and are compared with a control group doing the same training in normal temperatures. The heat comes either from a chamber at 38 to 40 °C or from a heat suit worn while riding in an ordinary room.

On haemoglobin mass the trials agree. In 23 elite male cyclists it rose by 42 g, from 893 to 935 g, against 6 g in the controls [13]. In a second trial from the same lab it rose 2.6% with a heat suit and 2.4% in a chamber, while the controls lost 0.7% [14]. In 25 elite cross-country skiers it finished 30 g higher than in the controls [15]. In a study of 20 elite women and 27 elite men it rose 4.0% and 3.5% [16]. In 20 elite male cyclists in a separate trial it was up 3% after three weeks and 4% after five [4]. Every trial points the same way, and the size of the effect, 30 to 40 g or 3% to 4%, is about what an altitude camp delivers. One study made that comparison directly in seven riders who did both: 5.4% from five weeks of heat, 3.5% from three weeks at 3,000 metres [17]. B

On performance they do not agree.

The first of these trials found that VO2max rose no more in the heat group than in the controls. Threshold power and 15-minute power both improved more with heat, by amounts the authors describe as small to intermediate, and neither difference was statistically significant [13]. The skiers showed no difference from controls in VO2max, in economy or in a 15-minute performance test, despite their extra 30 g of haemoglobin [15]. The second cycling trial found better threshold and one-minute power in the heat-suit group but not in the chamber group; only when the two were combined, and three measures merged into one index, did heat clearly beat control, by 4.9% against 1.7% [14]. The study of women and men reports that threshold, VO2max and 15-minute power all improved with heat [16]. The riders in the 2024 trial gained 12 W of peak power in a ramp test, with a change in VO2max that fell short of significance [4].

Among these elite trials, two say yes, one says yes after pooling and two say no. A meta-analysis that gathered 28 heat studies of all lengths put the effect on cool-weather VO2max, compared with control training, at 0.30 standard deviations, which is conventionally called small, with a confidence interval running from 0.06 to 0.54 [18]. My reading is that there is probably a real effect on performance in elite riders, that it is small, and that it is much less certain than the effect on haemoglobin. B

Carsten Lundby, who argued the "no" side of the 2016 debate, is an author on every one of these five-week trials. It is to his credit that he tested a position he had argued against, and it means the evidence comes from one connected group of labs in Copenhagen and Lillehammer. Nobody outside that group has yet repeated it.

The riders were nearly all elite, and the men among them had VO2max values in the mid-70s [13,16]. An elite rider has little room left to improve by ordinary training, so a 3% rise in haemoglobin is worth chasing. Whether the same applies to a rider with far more room is a separate question, and the one trial that tested it is described in the next section.

And the gain does not last. In the 2024 trial, haemoglobin mass was back to its starting value two weeks after the heat sessions stopped [4]. Rønnestad's group found that three sessions a week were enough to hold it [14]. Heat training is therefore something you keep doing or time carefully, like a taper.

One study, up close: the Copenhagen amateurs

In 2019 Nybo and Lundby's group published the first trial long enough to test the haemoglobin idea, and it remains the only one in riders who resemble the readers of this book [11,12].
